Working manuscript · developmental revision · 15 September 2026

An original reassessment of Richard H. Solomon’s Chinese Negotiating Behavior, with seven chapters, a conclusion and three research appendices. Individual cases have different event endpoints. No new interviews or private Chinese archive access are claimed. This is not an authorized new edition.

Use the contents to move between chapters. Numbered notes link to the sources and back to the text. Supporting local files are available with the research repository.

Introduction: What an agreement buys

On Friday, November 12, 1999, an American diplomat in Beijing wrote to China’s minister of foreign trade with a departure time. The delegation led by US Trade Representative Charlene Barshefsky would leave its hotel at eight the next morning. If Premier Zhu Rongji would meet her, the Americans could stay. They wanted an answer by seven.1

William McCahill’s letter did more than threaten to end the talks. It challenged the minister’s understanding of the American position, invoked the two presidents’ intentions and asked for a decision from the man whom China’s president had made responsible for the negotiations. The Americans were willing to discuss a disputed ownership provision as part of a package that satisfactorily resolved their demands for protections against Chinese imports. Their departure was conditional too. They could delay until Saturday evening, or even early Sunday, but no further. Barshefsky wanted Zhu to help close a bargain his negotiators had not closed.

There was much to lose on either side. China wanted to join the World Trade Organization. The United States wanted terms on which American firms and farmers could sell to, invest in and compete with it. Earlier that year Zhu had publicly defended accession as a way to expose Chinese enterprises to competition and encourage reform. He rejected press descriptions of his concessions as a gift to the United States. Both governments had reasons to reach an agreement, and both could reject the immediate package.2

For a company hoping to enter China, the package contained several different gates. A foreign investor might be admitted but denied a controlling share. It might obtain an ownership stake but face restrictions on where it could operate or whom it could serve. A concession could promise access after a specified interval rather than give it immediately. The negotiators had to decide how much each permission was worth and what to offer for it.

Life insurance shows why those distinctions mattered. The Chinese and American papers of November 12 both contemplated a fifty-percent foreign stake. An American paper attributed to the following day added a demand for managerial control. A limit on foreign ownership could remain while the distribution of power inside the venture changed. The Chinese proposal, meanwhile, made its offered concessions conditional on settling the package as a whole. A figure on a page was not yet an entitlement a firm could use.3

Behind the Chinese delegation stood more than one source of permission. Financial negotiator Deng Hongguo later described foreign requests passing through the trade ministry to regulators who prepared alternatives and sought clearance from their leaders. The trade delegation then had to assemble a package from what the sectors could offer. His account identifies a problem the visitor could not solve through rapport alone: the official who understood a proposal might still lack permission to accept it.4

Barshefsky was asking for more than another meeting. She needed a decision about which limits China would defend, which terms could change and what Washington would have to accept in return. McCahill’s letter put those choices on a clock. Chapter 2 follows what happened when the clock ran out.

The predicament recurs throughout this book: an official can defend one limit while making a valuable concession on another. The visitor must decide whether to seek compensation within that limit, challenge it, or reject a package that leaves the needed benefit out of reach. The choice requires an estimate of what the other government wants and a clear account of what one’s own side can accept. Neither comes from interpreting political language alone.

Since Richard H. Solomon studied the opening to China, WTO membership has given Chinese officials a forum in which to challenge a powerful counterpart’s decisions, and China has developed the capacity to use it. Later agreements have changed the procedures for scrutiny and disputed performance. Under Xi Jinping, Beijing has reorganized central supervision while retaining distinct responsibilities for carrying out decisions. China’s position in supplying foreign industries has also given its licensing decisions greater consequences abroad. These developments change what a counterpart can obtain, where it can contest a refusal and what it needs to secure before announcing success.

The inheritance

Solomon knew the difficulty of doing that work. He had served on the National Security Council during the opening to China, read official American records and interviewed more than thirty senior negotiators. His principal study covered 1967–1984. The volume often encountered today is a 2005 printing of the 1999 edition, which added an essay by Charles W. Freeman Jr. about later developments. Its publication date can conceal how far back the main investigation reaches.5

The book’s enduring quality is its attention to the predicament of the person across the table. A visitor wants access to someone who can decide. A relationship offers it, but may also create obligations. An earlier expression of goodwill can return as an alleged commitment. A Chinese host may ask an influential American to explain Beijing’s position in Washington; the American may believe that preserving the relationship will improve the chances of a useful agreement. Solomon followed these encounters beyond ceremonial language to the pressure they placed on the participant’s choices.

His advice was demanding. Know the record well enough to challenge a tendentious account of it. Decide what terms your own government can accept. Keep the strategic purpose in view when the conversation becomes personal. He gave interests and official discipline more explanatory weight than interpersonal tactics. He also warned that compressing their conduct into a characteristic style could make it seem more consistent and far-sighted than it had been.6

Cultivation still deserves Solomon’s close attention. In July 2023, Henry Kissinger received exceptional access and requests for help in Beijing, although he held no government office. The visit revived a familiar form of diplomacy. Whether it helped produce a particular decision is a separate question, examined in the crisis chapter. The distinction between access and an instruction matters to anyone considering an intermediary.7

Freeman had already made room for learning and institutional change. He connected officials’ room to improvise with communications, budgets and access to instructions; he described specialization and adaptation to particular counterparts. Solomon had expressly bounded his own study to an era when Beijing wanted a constructive relationship with Washington. Neither author requires us to believe that later China would negotiate every issue in the same way. The useful inheritance is a set of questions about relationships, authority and pressure, together with historical claims that must be tested at their original strength.8

What changed at the table

WTO membership changed where a refused demand could lead. China could contest American trade measures through an external procedure and obtain findings Washington had opposed. It also had to learn to assemble the legal arguments, economic evidence and organizational support that made such claims possible. The dispute known as DS379 shows the reach and limits of that capacity: China won important rulings, then faced further administrative proceedings over their application. By 2026, even the availability of appeal depended on arrangements that differed by counterpart.9

American demands about China’s domestic administration had already become detailed in the 1990s. The 1995 intellectual-property bargain prescribed enforcement bodies, a deadline for accepting petitions or explaining refusal, information and consultations, with Washington retaining the prospect of renewed trade action. Phase One therefore belongs to a longer argument about making promises perform. Its distinctive contribution in this comparison was the bilateral procedure the governments accepted for complaints, escalation and disputed remedies.10

The 2025 export-control negotiations made another dependency visible. Foreign factories needed Chinese supplies; after London, Beijing described approvals for eligible applications in exchange for relief from particular American restrictions. Transactions resumed while Chinese control authority survived. A government could obtain immediate relief yet leave its industries dependent on future permissions. The concession’s duration and coverage mattered alongside its announced size.11

The organization behind the counterpart changed too. The 2018 reforms consolidated specific coordinating functions in a central foreign-affairs commission and its office. Published responsibilities became clearer, but a visitor still could not read a delegation’s authority from its place in the hierarchy. Fieldwork in the early Xi years describes tighter supervision of informal diplomatic contact alongside continuing room for initiative. These changes affect how a proposal can be explored and carried upward; they do not establish that fewer concessions became possible.12

The cases demonstrate changes in institutions and instruments without measuring a national increase in patience, rigidity or deception, or establishing that cultivating old friends became more or less effective. Familiar conduct can serve a changed purpose. The explanation has to reach the decision an official was trying to obtain and the alternative that made refusal tolerable.

The terms on which China will agree

American experience supplies the center of this book. It does not set the limits of Chinese behavior. European officials negotiating accession had their own commercial priorities and authorization from member governments to manage. India sought local military arrangements while defending its position that the wider relationship depended on the border. Russia and China settled territorial questions through agreements that distinguished sovereignty from residents’ continued use, and developed gas cooperation in which an operating pipeline coexisted with unresolved terms for further projects. Friendship, rivalry and partnership created different settings; none of those labels settled the price or content of a concession.13

Foreign choices belong inside the explanation. Barshefsky’s delegation could invoke a departure time and then stay. European negotiators could seek authoritative confirmation of a Chinese constraint before asking their own governments to reconsider what they would accept. American leaders could impose tariffs and later accept a partial settlement with some of them retained. A Chinese negotiating position takes shape against those choices. Treating the foreign side as a passive audience would miss part of the process Solomon wanted his readers to master.

The chapters therefore ask what a negotiator should notice before interpreting an encounter as a breakthrough or a rebuff. Has a senior leader settled the policy question, or instructed the delegates to keep trying? Does the proposed concession change an ownership rule, an operating permission or only the date at which a request can be considered? Where can a refusal still enter the process? What does the other side gain by accepting a narrower bargain now? The answers can justify patience, a revised offer or a decision to leave. Familiarity with Chinese political language helps the negotiator ask them. It cannot answer them in advance.

The account rests on documents, published testimony, scholarship and reporting, including material from both governments where it is available. It does not reproduce Solomon’s access to private deliberations and interviews. Where the evidence cannot identify who blocked a concession or why a mandate changed, the narrative leaves that uncertainty intact. But uncertainty about motive does not erase a documented offer, an operative condition or a failure to perform. The task is to make the strongest explanation the record permits, and then show what follows for the person who must decide without a complete record of their own.14

A worthwhile bargain may change a defended rule, secure a useful exception or accomplish a limited task while the larger dispute remains. It may also be unavailable at an acceptable price. The chapters that follow examine how negotiators confronted those choices, what they managed to obtain, and what the agreement allowed anyone to do the next morning.

1. The price of a new relationship

Leonard Woodcock arrived a few minutes late for his nine o’clock evening meeting with Deng Xiaoping on December 14, 1978. Changes to his instructions had arrived at the last minute. The chief of the American liaison office in Beijing was trying to finish an agreement that would transform relations between the two countries. The words that might close it still depended on decisions in Washington.15

The most difficult words concerned Taiwan. Beijing wanted Washington to end diplomatic relations with Taipei, remove its military presence and terminate the defense treaty. The Americans were prepared to take those steps while maintaining unofficial relations with Taiwan. They also intended to continue selling it arms. China opposed those sales. The question was whether the governments could establish diplomatic relations while each continued to defend a position the other rejected.

Deng had already shown Woodcock that the answer might be yes. The previous day, he had accepted important parts of the American proposal and agreed to visit Washington. But he had also asked for changes in what the Americans would do and what they would say. His intervention made a bargain possible to pursue; it did not make the remaining disagreement disappear.

What could be left unresolved

The prospect of meeting Deng had itself been encouraging. At the end of their December 4 session, Vice Foreign Minister Han Nien-lung had told Woodcock that Deng wanted to see him. Han had presented a draft communiqué in both languages and a firm account of China’s requirements. He refused a pledge to settle the Taiwan question peacefully, describing it as a matter of Chinese sovereignty. Yet he offered a way to accommodate Washington: China could refrain from objecting to an American expression of hope for a peaceful solution, while issuing its own statement that reunification was an internal affair. A common announcement could coexist with separate positions.16

Washington was working to make the larger relationship attractive. On December 11, national security adviser Zbigniew Brzezinski urged Chinese liaison-office chief Chai Zemin to meet the January 1 target. A visit by a senior Chinese leader could give the new relationship political momentum in the United States. Brzezinski also mentioned the possibility of a Soviet summit in Washington, while denying that the invitations were linked. Timing and personal access were resources the Americans could offer too.17

Deng opened his December 13 meeting by acknowledging Brzezinski’s exchange with Chai. Woodcock then handed him the American draft. As the interpreter read it, Deng asked her to repeat particular phrases. His questions concerned what Washington’s promises would mean in practice. If diplomatic relations with Taiwan were ending, why would withdrawing the military presence take a year? Woodcock explained that it would not: troops and installations would be removed within four months. The one-year period applied to other arrangements and, separately, to the notice required to terminate the defense treaty.1819

Deng accepted the withdrawal timetable after checking that it was separate from the American position on arms sales. He was less satisfied with the treaty’s remaining life. Public reference to its termination article could make the agreement look as though Washington had ended the treaty in name while retaining it in fact. Could the Americans avoid citing the article and handle the legal process internally?

Woodcock explained that following the treaty’s own terms mattered to the president’s authority to terminate it. Other approaches could involve other branches of government. Deng asked him to consult the lawyers. He then proposed an accommodation requiring a concrete American commitment: no new arms sales to Taiwan during the year in which the treaty remained legally in force. His acceptance of Washington’s termination language depended on that restraint and on avoiding an explicit reference to the article in the statement.20

These were choices for Washington. In the margins of Woodcock’s report, Carter indicated that the administration should try to accommodate the one-year pause. He accepted the possibility of omitting the article from the communiqué and perhaps the exchanged statements, but insisted that public explanations could not conceal the legal position. On Deng’s request for language opposing hegemony, Carter was wary of wording that would unnecessarily antagonize the Soviet Union. Woodcock urged a formulation based on the already accepted Shanghai Communiqué. The American envoy was negotiating with instructions from a president who was making several judgments of his own.21

Woodcock also warned against treating Deng’s willingness to proceed as consent to future sales. The one-year pause did not settle what would happen afterward. His assessment was that Deng wanted movement and had political reasons to limit the immediate controversy. That was an envoy’s interpretation of a counterpart whose internal deliberations he could not fully see. It was reason to keep negotiating, not permission to tell Washington that Beijing had accepted the American arms policy.

By the evening of December 14, much of the bargain was in place. Woodcock delivered the revised American presentation and read the proposed texts. Deng accepted the communiqué with a few changes. One came from Vice Foreign Minister Zhang Wenjin: the governments should reaffirm the principles they had agreed on in the Shanghai Communiqué, rather than everything expressed there. The earlier document contained separate positions as well as common ones. Woodcock checked its language and accepted the distinction.22

A small change protected a large difference. Neither government wanted agreement on a common document to make every statement in it its own. The distinction would matter whenever either invoked the past agreement to demand more from the other.

Arms sales still tested it. Deng asked Woodcock to omit the reference to 1979 from what he understood would be a public answer about the pause: naming one year implied what might happen in the next. Woodcock explained that the assurance about 1979 was an answer to Deng’s request, not the proposed response to a press question. Deliveries already committed or under way could continue; the pause concerned new sales. Deng accepted the explanation. Woodcock reported that the American position was clearly on the record.

On December 15, the final difficulty lay as much in announcing the bargain as in writing it. Woodcock expected Chinese objections to future sales and thought their impact would depend on the wider relationship. He warned that an immediate presidential statement could embarrass Deng. Washington replied that silence was impossible. Congress would ask, reporters would ask, and the administration needed to reassure Taiwan. It wanted Chinese restraint when it explained its intentions, not explicit Chinese approval.23

Woodcock met Deng again that afternoon. When he confirmed the intention to sell arms after 1979, Deng emphatically rejected it. Continued supplies, he argued, would preserve the substance of the defense relationship and obstruct peaceful reunification. Woodcock answered that American political conditions made denying arms to Taiwan untenable. He also promised that Washington would not misrepresent China’s position or imply its consent.

The discussion turned to whether the announcement should wait. Deng urged Carter to avoid direct answers that would force a Chinese response and diminish the achievement. Woodcock said the purpose of raising the issue was to prevent surprises. Deng agreed to proceed, while maintaining that China would answer an American public assertion of future sales. The envoy recommended going ahead. An agreement was available; agreement on arms sales was not.24

Brzezinski’s final instruction preserved that difference. The United States would reaffirm its position when questioned and acknowledge that China did not endorse it. Woodcock conveyed the proposed response through Foreign Ministry channels. The joint communiqué established diplomatic relations from January 1, 1979, with embassies and ambassadors scheduled for March 1. The two governments had arranged recognition and the transformation of Washington’s official relationship with Taiwan without settling the dispute over future arms supplies.2526

For Woodcock, the task had been to carry an available agreement through a disagreement serious enough to endanger it. For Deng, proceeding meant accepting that Washington would retain and publicly defend a policy he opposed. The value placed on the new relationship made that limited result worth considering. Nothing in the arrangement required either government to stop arguing its case afterward.

The relationship Solomon studied

Richard Solomon made this world intelligible to people who would have to negotiate in it. His principal study covered 1967–1984, the years in which a previously hostile relationship was being opened and developed. He had served on the National Security Council staff, examined official American records and interviewed more than thirty senior American negotiators. He supplemented those interviews with other officials and businessmen. He could connect an exchange at the table with participants’ accounts of what they were trying to accomplish.2728

His central subject was the political use of a relationship. Chinese officials assessed their counterparts over repeated encounters, sought common principles, cultivated selected interlocutors and kept close track of what had been said. A visitor’s earlier expression of sympathy could return as a claim on future conduct. Recognition as a trusted friend could create an obligation to explain China’s concerns inside the visitor’s own government or to help rescue a threatened negotiation.29

The attraction and the pressure belonged together. Access might help an American understand a proposal, persuade a Chinese leader or get an answer that ordinary channels could not produce. Protecting that access could also make him more receptive to a Chinese request. Solomon’s practical warning was to keep the government’s purpose in view when the relationship became personal.

He did not imagine that Beijing had created all the American differences it could exploit. Agencies and advisers already disagreed. Selecting one channel could give influence to a sympathetic official without changing anyone’s convictions. Nor had personal diplomacy abolished Chinese institutions. Solomon described official discipline, vulnerability to political criticism and the increasing involvement of ministries and technical specialists. The person who understood a request might lack authority to grant it.30

The normalization sequence gives these observations concrete force. Woodcock found room to move because Deng could make decisions, yet the American still had to obtain instructions. The parties altered words and sequences while reserving fundamental positions. Solomon expressly described partial agreements as a way for China to develop a relationship from which it could continue bargaining over unresolved issues. That possibility is part of his explanation, not a discovery that a later update can claim for itself.

His scope matters just as much. He asked whether insights developed while Beijing wanted a constructive relationship with Washington would hold if confrontation returned. He left the answer open. The attraction of a relationship was a condition of the historical experience, not a permanent Chinese disposition to agree. An official dealing with a counterpart who values avoiding a concession more than improving relations faces a different calculation.31

Even during the opening, however, high-level engagement did not always produce the settlement the Americans expected.

The money Zhou could offer

In November 1973, Henry Kissinger and Premier Zhou Enlai discussed a sum small enough for both men to disparage and large enough to obstruct an agreement. American private claimants sought compensation for property taken in China. Washington had blocked Chinese assets that might provide it. But some Chinese deposits in third-country banks had already been returned to Beijing. A settlement that excluded those funds would leave much less for the Americans.

Nixon had authorized an approach the previous year: seek a Chinese lump-sum payment, possibly in installments, in return for unblocking assets; if that proved unacceptable, propose retaining blocked funds to compensate the claimants. In February 1973, Zhou and Kissinger had favored treating the problem politically. The later American review described an agreement in principle to assign claims to each other. The details had proved harder.32

On November 13, Zhou challenged the terminology inherited from the period of hostility. Accepting Washington’s designation of Chinese nationals, he argued, would imply accepting the American position that had justified blocking the funds. Kissinger treated it as something that should be settled politically, but admitted that he had learned of this particular problem only two days earlier. He would have to consult his lawyers. Zhou’s objection was not simply a dislike of precision. The precise category carried a judgment about the legality of earlier American conduct.33

Old Chinese bonds posed another limit. Kissinger could offer that the American government would not support claims on debts incurred by previous governments. Diplomat Arthur Hummel explained that the executive could not prevent private citizens from approaching the courts. Zhou wanted to know how the People’s Republic could be made responsible for money lent to governments it had displaced. Personal agreement between the two senior men could not supply every protection Beijing wanted.

Zhou then offered a way around the missing money. China could return $17 million, either to the Americans or through the banks. He preferred the first route. The banks had already given China its money back; he did not want to reverse their action. Kissinger welcomed the possibility of putting the money into the compensation pool without making China repay it through those institutions. In his estimate, it could help bring compensation to roughly forty cents on the dollar, a level he believed Congress would accept; twenty cents would not suffice.34

The proposed payment therefore served different purposes. Kissinger needed a settlement defensible to American claimants and legislators. Zhou wanted a route that did not undo China’s position on the blocked accounts. Both men could see an accommodation, but Kissinger still promised legal consultation and a further proposal. His report to Nixon six days later described continued work toward resolution. Zhou had offered a consequential sum. They had not completed the settlement.

Solomon was already trying to explain why progress at that level was not producing secure ties below it. In a December 31 memorandum to Kissinger, he warned that Chinese political and bureaucratic resistance might prevent the relationship from surviving Mao Zedong and Zhou Enlai. The claims talks were one of several examples. He criticized the manner in which officials had handled them, while acknowledging that the Chinese had some grounds for suspicion about the settlement’s mechanics.35

His diagnosis led to proposals. Kissinger should send Zhou a personal message about the condition of the relationship. A forthcoming Chinese trade delegation could be led by an important political official. A later American visit might include provincial cities and contact with a wider group of Chinese leaders. Solomon wanted to broaden the group of people invested in the opening, rather than rely entirely on the leaders who had begun it. Kissinger approved preparing the message and exploring a senior trade visitor. He held back on the broader itinerary and required his clearance before a State Department démarche.

That memorandum shows the future author at work as an adviser: interpreting obstruction and recommending a response. Its reporting was partly redacted and its attachments were not printed in the later public record. In his later book, Solomon attributed the aborted arrangement to bureaucratic resistance and political pressure associated with the rise of the Gang of Four. The memorandum and book cannot independently corroborate each other. They do show that his account was more than a description of frustrating manners. He was asking how a relationship dependent on a few powerful sponsors could acquire wider political support.

Control of the American channel presented difficulties too. In February 1974, Hummel privately described delayed answers, withheld instructions and the difficulty of getting Kissinger’s attention while other crises competed for it. An initial authorization to discuss the liaison office’s space needs had been retracted pending his return from the Middle East. These were not records of what caused the claims failure. They show why a negotiator could encounter delay on either side of a tightly controlled relationship.36

China withdrew Zhou’s monetary offer in 1974, according to the later Vance and Brzezinski memoranda. It had not been reinstated by the 1977 talks. In October 1975, Deng had made the continuing legal objection explicit to Kissinger: no settlement could require Chinese observance of American law. He professed little concern about how long the issue remained unsettled, while leaving open the possibility of a rapid agreement if the terms could be solved.37

For Washington, waiting also became a choice. In May 1977, Secretary of State Cyrus Vance explained that definitions and the missing third-country funds still determined how much American claimants could receive. Without movement, he expected congressional trouble. Yet making diplomatic normalization depend on solving the claims would allow a secondary issue to obstruct the larger relationship. He recommended deferring further approaches until the administration had decided its normalization strategy. Carter approved.38

The failure was real, and its object matters. An offer to contribute to a proposed settlement had been withdrawn; an agreement in principle had not become a completed instrument. Those events could damage confidence without being a refusal to perform a finished agreement. A negotiator needed to understand both the loss of the offer and why the remaining terms still mattered. The title and rank of the person who made it did not remove those questions.

What a leader’s arrival tells the negotiator

Solomon included the aborted claims arrangement in his book. On the following page, discussing an endgame characterized by concrete drafting, he called senior intervention “the most reliable sign” that agreement was at hand. A political leader, he reasoned, would not risk his face in a negotiation about to collapse. His description was stronger than a claim that senior contact sometimes helped.39

Normalization shows the attraction of that forecast. Han’s announcement of Deng’s interest preceded substantive decisions, detailed drafting and agreement. The claims talks show the difficulty in using it. Zhou could offer money while payment mechanics, definitions and the American ability to deliver remained unsettled. Solomon’s own evidence creates a tension with his confident account of the leader’s risk. It does not, from two cases, establish how reliably the signal predicts agreement compared with other signals.

The visitor can make the encounter more useful by bringing a decision the leader can address: accept this formula, authorize this exchange, or confirm that this limit will hold. The answer can then be carried back into the draft, with unresolved terms identified. That requires candor about the visitor’s own authority. Woodcock still needed Washington’s answers. Kissinger still needed legal advice and a package he believed Congress would accept. Chinese seniority could not remove American conditions.

Solomon’s related judgment that Chinese rigidity usually indicates leadership conflict poses a similar problem. The cases here cannot establish that frequency. A firm refusal may arise from a divided leadership; it may also express a settled preference or a legal objection the other side has not answered. The responses differ. Seeking a more powerful sponsor may help in one situation, while changing the substance or form of the offer may matter more in another.40

The same care applies to principles. Deng bargained over how Washington would describe treaty termination and future arms sales. Zhou objected to legal categories and routes of payment. The useful question was what accepting a formulation would concede. Neither a general preference for flexibility nor an assumption that principles are immovable can answer it in advance.

These are ways of using Solomon’s observations, not grounds for quietly weakening his original predictions until every outcome fits. He distinguished characteristic Chinese practices from uniquely Chinese ones. A later history must preserve that distinction while explaining how the people, institutions and purposes in a particular negotiation gave a familiar practice its force.

Freeman’s next question

Freeman’s essay in the 1999 edition had already begun that work. Negotiating at home might reflect budgets, communications and proximity to the officials who could authorize a concession. Inviting the foreign side to speak first could give a Chinese official a proposal to report and a basis for seeking new instructions. The visible conduct might continue while the reason for it changed.41

He described increased foreign contacts, research capacity and experience in international institutions, alongside the growing involvement of specialized ministries. He also put American domestic politics inside the account: the executive negotiating abroad might simultaneously be negotiating with Congress. A bilateral table could sit inside a larger process of assembling authority and support.42

Specialists, Freeman suggested, could develop different approaches to different counterparts. He nevertheless regarded the basic approach as persistent and endorsed Solomon’s confidence that its recurring patterns could help foreigners anticipate Chinese conduct. Adaptation and continuity belonged in the same account. That made the next question more demanding than whether China had become more modern or more rigid. Which officials had learned what, and what could they do with it in the negotiation at hand? Which apparent change reflected a new instrument or new expertise, and which reflected a different interest in the result?43

The next chapters follow those questions into a relationship with more institutions, more commercial interests and more means of pressure. They begin with an inheritance that is already complicated. The Chinese government could defend a political position and accept an agreement that left it unresolved. A foreign official could benefit from access while confronting limits in both governments. The relationship mattered because it made some bargains worth the concessions and remaining risks. Knowing China did not relieve the negotiator of deciding what those bargains were worth.

2. The bargain that came in pieces

On March 30, 1999, US Trade Representative Charlene Barshefsky and State Councillor Wu Yi disagreed about whose applause a trade agreement needed. Premier Zhu Rongji was about to visit the United States, and China wanted an agreement on the terms of its entry into the World Trade Organization. In the Chinese published account of their meeting in Beijing, Barshefsky explained that a strong package had to command support in a hostile American political atmosphere. Wu replied that American businesses and farmers would applaud the concessions already available; opponents of China and of Clinton’s China policy never would. Barshefsky agreed that the extremes were unreachable. The problem was the middle, whose support was eroding. Business leaders, she complained, said different things to Chinese officials, American officials and members of Congress.44

Wu was inviting Barshefsky to stop chasing people who could never be satisfied. Barshefsky was asking for terms that would hold together a constituency large enough to matter. Their disagreement was about the political value of additional concessions. If the existing offer was already enough to win business support, further concessions would buy China little. If it was not, declaring the commercial bargain complete would leave the administration’s domestic problem unresolved.

The Chinese account records Barshefsky making the same argument to Zhu through the composition of the package. Market access alone would not do. The accession protocol, agricultural sanitary arrangements and textiles all belonged in the bargain. A smaller agreement could lose the very interests the administration needed. Zhu answered that the decision had become political and that China could survive without joining. The two sides were testing whether the other government wanted accession enough to pay its domestic price.

Barshefsky’s later recollection adds a warning to that exchange. She says she told Zhu before his visit that even an agreement with her might be refused by Clinton. Her purpose, she recalled, was to avoid leaving him feeling deceived and to preserve the negotiation if Washington declined to finish. That warning separated her ability to negotiate from the president’s readiness to accept.45

An offer without an agreement

At their April 8 press conference, Zhu and President Bill Clinton made their different assessments public. Clinton announced substantial progress, with work still to do. Zhu said the gap was small and the political atmosphere was the real obstacle. He also rejected the suggestion, circulating in the Hong Kong press, that he had brought America a gift. Opening China would expose its enterprises to competition, he acknowledged, but that pressure would serve Chinese reform. He joked that calling it a gift might get Clinton into trouble over campaign contributions.46

The joke carried a serious defense of the price Zhu was prepared to pay. He was presenting exposure to foreign competition as a Chinese benefit, including when Chinese enterprises found it uncomfortable. That did not mean every sector official wanted the same opening, or that every concession was valuable to China in itself. It meant the premier could justify a package partly through consequences that an individual protected industry might resist. A foreign negotiator who saw only reluctance to yield would miss an important source of Zhu’s willingness to bargain.

Washington did not close. Nor did it simply put an agreed text away for signature at a more convenient time. The memorandum signed by Long Yongtu and Peter Scher on April 10 recorded extensive agreement on goods and services, subject to checking and correction, but identified unfinished banking and auto-finance terms, securities, audiovisual services, textiles, and the duration of special trade-remedy provisions. Washington faced both a dispute over its readiness to close and consequential terms still to settle.47

Publicizing the offer created another difficulty. At the April 8 American briefing, Barshefsky defended the commercial issues still outstanding and denied that publication was intended to mobilize business pressure. Economic adviser Gene Sperling acknowledged differences among advisers. In his later interview, Lawrence Summers criticized the attempt to claim the concessions publicly without completing the agreement. What could appear in Washington as evidence of negotiating success could appear in Beijing as a price paid for a bargain withheld.48

The surviving memorandum mattered because it preserved a starting point more definite than those competing presentations. Yet it also preserved unfinished business. When talks resumed, the sides would argue over which April positions still counted. A negotiator who publicizes a provisional package may gain supporters, but also creates an audience that will treat its contents as a minimum entitlement. The other side then has reason to insist that the offer depended on receiving the whole bargain. China’s November proposal would state that condition explicitly.

Bringing Washington to Beijing

The bombing of China’s embassy in Belgrade suspended accession talks. Communication did not cease altogether: the Chinese published history records messages and technical contacts during the interruption, before the leaders announced a resumption at Auckland. The earlier offers returned as disputed baselines, alongside differences over permanent normal trade relations, safeguards and the scope of the negotiation. The Chinese account reports Barshefsky treating high-technology export controls as outside her WTO mandate.49

Washington also tried to reassure Beijing. Summers later recalled going to China in October with a presidential mandate to make clear that, if Beijing was prepared to finish, Washington was too. He met Zhu in Lanzhou while the premier was touring rural China. Economic development and the Asian financial crisis were part of their conversation; the WTO assurance belonged to a relationship in which they discussed more than the unfinished offer. His contemporary speech confirms the visit and meetings, though the private message comes from his later account.50

Barshefsky wanted the composition of her November delegation to carry a similar assurance. In her oral history, she says she asked for either White House chief of staff John Podesta or National Economic Council director Gene Sperling to accompany her. Both, she recalled, had opposed closing during Zhu’s April visit. Bringing one to Beijing would demonstrate White House commitment and prevent interference from that official at home. Sperling went.51

Barshefsky wanted the same travelling companion to reassure Beijing and protect her position in Washington. In her calculation, his part in the decision mattered as much as his title.

Beijing brought fourteen departments into the final round, according to financial negotiator Deng Hongguo. He recalled six days and nights at the ministry, camp beds between sessions and a pager that could call him back. Its sound accelerated his heartbeat.52

Deng described sector regulators seeking clearance for alternatives while the trade ministry assembled the package. Knowing the answer and having permission to give it were different accomplishments.

The price of another day

By November 12, the choices were detailed enough to put in a closing package. China offered 49 percent foreign equity in value-added telecommunications and paging within one year, with separate terms for mobile and other basic services. It offered 50 percent in life insurance on accession. Its banking proposal adjusted geography but did not promise national treatment; foreign nonbank auto finance remained conditional on comparable permission for Chinese institutions. It proposed that special trade-remedy provisions end no later than three years after accession. The package depended on reaching the final bilateral agreement during the current negotiation.53

The American response preserved earlier schedules except where new provisions displaced them. The negotiators had a considerable accumulated text before them. In his November 12 letter to Shi, chargé d’affaires William C. McCahill Jr. made one proposed exchange explicit: discussion of sensitive equity terms was available within a package that met American requirements on safeguards and antidumping. Washington wanted protection against some consequences of the market integration it was negotiating; Beijing wanted to preserve ownership limits. The Americans asked for Zhu to help decide the combination.

McCahill attached a clock to that request. The delegation would leave its hotel at eight the following morning; Shi had until seven to say whether Zhu would meet it. If the premier could see them on Saturday, they could remain until later that day or early Sunday, but no longer. The Americans were offering more time for a particular decision, with a stated limit on how much time they would give.54

The size of the trade-remedy gap explains why those terms could not be left to ceremonial closure. China wanted a maximum of three years. The American November 12 paper sought fifteen years for the product-specific safeguard; the next paper reduced that demand to twelve, while proposing twenty years for the special antidumping provision. The administration’s public account after agreement announced twelve years for the former and fifteen for the latter. These were different protections with different clocks, extending far beyond the Chinese proposal.55

The equity bargaining was also more intricate than a contest between 49 and 51 percent. Both sides’ November 12 papers already specified 50 percent life-insurance ownership. In its next paper, the United States explicitly sought management control as well. Half the shares with control was commercially different from half the shares without it. In value-added telecommunications and paging, the next American paper sought 50 percent within two years, while distinguishing equity from management and operational control. Other telecommunications categories followed other schedules. A concession on one percentage could be offset, enlarged or qualified by the rights attached to it.

Zhu met Barshefsky on November 13. Deng describes new State Council authority for that day’s talks and a further financial-leasing approval from central bank governor Dai Xianglong that evening. Senior intervention still left individual commitments to be cleared.56

Auto finance shows the movement. The November 12 Chinese proposal tied foreign nonbank access to corresponding domestic permission. Deng recalls immediate access for foreign enterprise-group finance companies under the new authority. The American paper attributed to November 13 expressly accepts a Chinese auto-finance offer made that day. The post-agreement American summary announces nonbank auto finance on accession. Together, the records trace a move away from waiting for a domestic permission toward access tied to membership, even though they do not expose every step of the instruction or reproduce the intervening offer.57

That change mattered because a condition controlled by future domestic policy was different from a commitment activated by accession. The same sensitivity to clocks applies to banking. The American November 13 paper proposed customer-access deadlines no later than January 1, 2002 and January 1, 2005; the administration’s post-agreement summary described periods of two and five years after accession. A fixed date and an accession-relative period assign the cost of a delayed accession differently. The annexes, rather than an assumption that the formulations were equivalent, had to settle the obligation.58

The November 13 meeting did not finish the bargain. On Sunday, the morning beyond which McCahill had said the delegation could not stay, he wrote again. The Americans alleged that China had reopened settled matters and introduced new ones. They invoked Zhu’s willingness to meet again if the talks reached another impasse and asked for his intervention. The letter treated the leaders’ intention to conclude as a reason for a further decision, not as evidence that the negotiators had already made it.59

In a recollection he did not date, Deng described Shi mistaking a summons from Zhu for a telephone call, then discovering that the premier was already at the ministry. The person who could give an instruction had arrived.60

Zhu’s November 15 intervention was followed by the bilateral agreement. Its signed Chinese and English bodies sent industrial goods, agriculture and services commitments to three annexes, with provisions applying rules to access to each other’s markets in a fourth. The governments were to notify the WTO and transmit the full agreement for incorporation into the multilateral accession work. They had concluded an important bargain. They had not granted China membership.61

Washington had not completed its domestic task either. Sandy Berger told reporters that day that the administration would seek permanent normal trade relations from Congress, an independent branch whose decision could not be supplied by the executive. The WTO welcomed the bilateral settlement while identifying negotiations with other governments and technical protocol work still ahead.62

The later accounts disagree about the departure threat’s effect. Barshefsky recalled using it to accelerate Chinese internal decisions, with Clinton’s backing. Deng dismissed the Americans’ departure tactics as ineffectual. The letters establish the pressure and the request for higher authority; they cannot decide which participant correctly judged its effect.63

The Americans had attached an extension to a specified opportunity: a meeting with Zhu about the unresolved package. Staying kept that opportunity open, though they ultimately stayed beyond their announced limit. For a negotiator, leaving can cut off a decision close to authorization; extending reveals that the clock can move. The question is what another interval could obtain and who can decide it. More hours alone cannot supply missing authority.

What Europe still wanted

For the European Union, the American agreement changed the starting point without settling the price. At the January 2000 talks, chief negotiator Hans-Friedrich Beseler invoked most-favored-nation treatment and proposed building on the US bargain. But Europe’s fifteen member states had priorities of their own. Long Yongtu answered that the American package already contained concessions valuable to Europe, and that China had compensated for protecting sensitive sectors by giving more elsewhere.64

Each was describing the inheritance that favored his next move. Beseler wanted the American gains as a base from which to negotiate European priorities. Long wanted the balance of the American package recognized as a reason not to reopen its protected sectors. The same Chinese offer could be generous in a negotiation weighted toward American interests and insufficient for a coalition with a different distribution of interests. Neither delegation could close simply by announcing that the other should be satisfied.

The European demands extended well below the political symbolism of accession. In a February 23 technical package, Europe proposed opening Zhuhai to renminbi banking immediately on accession while dropping its request to advance Beijing’s opening. Deng recalls central-bank approval for a Portuguese request to open Zhuhai in one year rather than three. For a firm waiting to operate there, the date could matter as much as the ownership ceiling. The final WTO schedule bound a one-year opening: earlier than Deng’s recalled benchmark, later than the immediate access in the European proposal.65

By March, the difficult ownership questions remained. Trade Commissioner Pascal Lamy met Zhu but left without an agreement. According to the Chinese history, he told Shi that his authorization did not permit acceptance of China’s 50 percent ceilings in life insurance and automobiles. The same account records a more personal explanation after the meeting: Lamy had only recently become commissioner, had five years ahead of him and considered accepting the present terms too risky. He needed to return and coordinate a position.66

This was a consequential refusal by the visiting side. China’s premier was accessible, but the European commissioner could not accept what China would offer. For Shi, another explanation of the Chinese ceiling would not resolve Lamy’s problem. The package had to become defensible to the governments whose authority he carried.

By May, the terms of that defense had changed. The Chinese account reports Lamy saying he could accept 50 percent in life insurance if Europe received compensation elsewhere in insurance. He could also accept the automobile ceiling with better tariff treatment. Acceptance of an ownership limit had become conditional on an alternative commercial gain. The point was to find benefits that the disappointed constituencies could value, rather than to make every sector yield on the same dimension.

Mobile telecommunications remained a problem. On May 17, Lamy said he had understood Zhu’s March remarks to allow a 50 percent ceiling and had reported that understanding to European ministers. Accepting 49 percent exceeded his authority. The next day he sought confirmation from a Chinese leader that 49 was the limit, so that he could report to Brussels and seek new authority.67

A one-point difference was now entangled with a commissioner’s account of what a premier had said. Insisting on 50 would defend that earlier account but threaten the wider agreement. Accepting 49 without explanation would leave Lamy to justify the retreat himself. Authoritative confirmation could help him explain why the package had to be built around a constraint that he had previously described differently.

Lamy kept negotiating the surrounding terms. On the evening of May 18, he offered alternatives for mobile services: a faster path to the retained 49 percent ceiling, with 35 percent initially, or different ownership treatment for public and private networks. He also pressed on retail chains and insurance licenses. Shi agreed to coordinate with the relevant departments, while warning that improvement was unlikely. The following morning he reported that the coordination had not produced a better offer. Lamy then said he could accept 25 percent mobile equity initially, alongside a package in distribution and insurance.68

At their May 19 meeting, Zhu described 49 percent as a collective Chinese government decision. Lamy reported consultations over the preceding two days with member states and industry. With Zhu’s confirmation, he said, Europe would respect the limit. He asked Zhu to authorize compensation in telecommunications, insurance and distribution. Zhu accepted the principle, leaving the details to Shi and Lamy. Wu Yi emphasized that the scope of the remaining retail-chain exceptions still needed negotiation. The parties checked the text and signed that day.69

The premier’s contribution was partly to make a refusal authoritative. That supplied confirmation Lamy had requested before accepting the limit, alongside approval in principle of compensation. It was a different use of senior intervention from asking a leader to overrule an obstructive subordinate. The exact moment at which Brussels changed Lamy’s authority is not disclosed; the account places consultations before the meeting. What it does disclose is the problem he brought to Zhu and the answer Zhu gave.

For someone facing a similar impasse, the European sequence suggests a demanding option. A ceiling may be retained while its commercial cost is reduced through timing, geography, permitted activities or additional licenses. That works only if the counterpart can accept compensation and the people authorizing it regard the substitute benefits as worth having. Faster entry into a joint venture cannot automatically compensate a firm whose essential requirement is control. Nor is confirmation of a limit valuable merely because it comes from a senior person. Here it addressed a specific difficulty in the European mandate and travelled with a substantive package.

The limits inside the opening

The final terms show what that package could—and could not—buy. In mobile telecommunications, the WTO schedule retained the 49 percent ceiling within three years after accession, with 25 percent at accession and 35 percent within one year. Geographic restrictions followed a separate five-year timetable. Europe had obtained a path into the market while accepting the ownership limit. The Commission called the equity phase-in an improvement on the American agreement; the November US public summary does not reproduce enough of that timetable for a complete bilateral comparison.70

In distribution, the boundary of an exception determined the value of the opening. The Commission announced that the restriction on large foreign-owned chains had been lifted. The final schedule preserved a prohibition on foreign majority ownership for certain chains with more than thirty outlets selling specified products of different types and brands from multiple suppliers. That was a narrower restriction than treating every large chain alike. It was also different from requiring Chinese managerial control: an equally owned venture could satisfy a ban on a foreign majority. The commercially useful question was which enterprises and products fell on each side of the boundary.71

Insurance supplied another kind of benefit. The Commission recorded seven new licenses—five life and two non-life—and two sub-branch licenses to be granted before accession, outside the accession terms themselves. Its October 2000 memorandum was still seeking timing for five further new licenses and two sub-branches. The promise of an administrative permission required an act of administration. Its value could not be cashed simply by pointing to the signed agreement.72

The remaining multilateral negotiation also had a price. Existing WTO members wanted to know how China would give effect to its commitments. The Commission reported China accusing them of seeking burdens beyond those normally required of an acceding country. The bilateral settlements had narrowed the disagreement; they had not removed the need to turn market-access bargains into an acceptable body of rules.

The Americans threatened to leave and stayed. The Europeans met the premier and went home without an agreement, then returned with ways to accept limits they had resisted. Chinese officials prepared intricate offers, sought authority and defended important ceilings. All three delegations tried to make the other side’s domestic constraints part of the price.

Solomon directed negotiators’ attention to authority, timing and the arrival of senior leaders. Accession makes those questions more useful by giving them specific objects. The November letter sought an exchange across equity and trade remedies. The auto-finance negotiation changed the condition that would activate access. Lamy sought confirmation of a ceiling and compensation he could defend to his own principals. Senior contact helped when there was a decision for it to accomplish. It could not substitute for deciding what the bargain was worth.

The negotiations also place technical expertise firmly before accession. Chinese officials were already preparing schedules, distinguishing ownership from control and separating permission from implementation. Membership would give them another setting in which to use those skills. The next chapter follows a dispute in which China could challenge how the United States applied multilateral rules that membership now allowed China to invoke.

3. What expertise makes possible

In August 2012, the United States published the results of its work to comply with a Chinese victory at the World Trade Organization. One line seemed to tell an extraordinary story. Zibo Aifudi, a producer of laminated woven sacks, had received a countervailing-duty rate of 29.54 percent in the original American investigation. The revised determination put it at 83.34 percent. China had won important legal arguments, and the American subsidy calculation for this exporter had risen by 53.80 percentage points.73

Those were determination rates, not a record of taxes actually paid before and after the ruling. Elsewhere in the same notice, an anti-dumping adjustment moved in the exporter’s favor. Yet the apparent paradox poses the commercial question more sharply than the language of victory: what had China obtained that a company trying to sell into the United States could use?

The answer lay in the decisions the ruling required Washington to reconsider—and the decisions it left Washington to make again.

Learning to use membership

In February 2002, China’s leaders gathered provincial and ministerial officials to study the organization they had joined. Jiang Zemin warned that inexperience would bring losses. He wanted specialists in trade law, negotiations and anti-dumping investigations, and demanded urgent recruitment and training. The published speech linked that investment to a second task: changing how Chinese governments managed the economy. WTO rules constrained members’ policies, he told them; officials would have to reduce direct intervention and learn different ways of supporting industry. Expertise was part of a political program for improving China’s competitive position.74

The work extended beyond the government payroll. Gregory Shaffer and Henry Gao’s research describes how the commerce ministry paired Chinese firms with experienced foreign counsel. Chinese lawyers obtained access to earlier submissions and legal databases, helped establish the facts of Chinese measures and commented on drafts. Third-party participation in other countries’ disputes supplied another apprenticeship: lawyers could study the arguments before China had to defend an equivalent measure itself. From 2011, the ministry also brought young law professors into its legal department for one-year assignments, giving them work on actual disputes.75

The relationships mattered inside government too. Foreign counsel needed information from the agencies responsible for a challenged measure. Commerce officials arranged meetings and brought those agencies into WTO hearings. Interviewees described this participation as a way to prepare ministries to accept an adverse finding. Expertise could help explain why a policy was vulnerable without giving the lawyers power to order its removal.76

Membership thus gave a growing professional network work to do, and that network made membership more useful. An objection to an American restriction could become a developed legal claim rather than remain a request for accommodation. The counterparty now had to reckon with the treaty argument and the procedure available to test it. DS379 shows how consequential that change could be—and how much bargaining could remain after China won.

The work a victory leaves behind

China’s challenge covered four groups of products: two categories of steel pipe, off-the-road tyres and laminated woven sacks. The United States was applying countervailing duties against alleged subsidies while calculating anti-dumping duties using its non-market-economy method. The former sought to offset subsidies. The latter compared actual export prices with a constructed normal value using values from outside China. If a subsidy had already increased the calculated dumping margin by lowering the export price, adding a countervailing duty against the same subsidization could offset it twice.77

China challenged this combination and the findings supporting the subsidy duties. Its lawyers disputed what made a state-owned enterprise a governmental “public body,” how to value benefits and how the two duties interacted. The April 2009 submission summary argued from the treaty’s wording, principles of legal attribution and the distinction between ownership and governmental authority.78

The appellate ruling in March 2011 gave those arguments force without dictating a tariff. State ownership alone did not establish that an input supplier was a public body. The relevant inquiry concerned governmental authority. That required Commerce to do more than identify a controlling shareholder; it did not exempt state-owned suppliers from subsidy investigations. The distinction mattered immediately: China succeeded against the ownership-based findings concerning input suppliers, but failed to establish the same inconsistency in the finding concerning state-owned commercial banks, which rested on a broader record.7980

On double remedies, the appellate tribunal rejected Washington’s interpretation and required investigation of whether, and to what extent, the same subsidy had been offset twice. It described duplication under the method as likely, rather than inevitable in every instance. It did not tell Commerce to subtract every subsidy dollar automatically.81

In Washington, the favorable interpretation therefore became another investigation. Commerce’s account records the resulting dispute with China. The agency asked about Chinese laws, the conduct of enterprises and their relationships with government. China supplied material but objected that the requests were excessive and that the agency had not adequately explained what evidence would matter. Commerce replied that the fuller inquiry followed from China’s successful objection to treating ownership as sufficient. The winner had obtained a more demanding test; it now faced demands for information under that test.82

The reopened land inquiry shows how this could work against an exporter. The WTO panel had rejected reasoning that made land-use rights regionally specific simply because land occupied a designated place. It explicitly allowed that better evidence of a distinct regime might justify another finding.83 During implementation, China supplied a 1992 Huantai County circular setting land prices in different districts. It argued that Aifudi’s price reflected applicable law and transactions in a neighboring county. Commerce saw a different implication. It said the circular, absent from the original investigation, helped establish a distinct pricing regime inside the county. Prices remained fixed over a long period, the lowest rate also applied to foreign-invested enterprises, and other material linked preferences to investment. China disputed aspects of the evidence and its applicability. Commerce maintained that the land benefit was specific enough to countervail.84

This explains how an adverse WTO finding could produce a renewed American determination. It does not explain the 53.80-point rise. For the revised countervailing rates, the final memorandum points to a separate calculation concerning petrochemical inputs and the public-body inquiry. That calculation’s detailed attachment has not been recovered. The defensible conclusion is narrower: the ruling did not prevent Commerce from reaching another subsidy finding, and the final table cannot by itself establish the precise contribution of changed supplier classifications, valuation or other inputs.85

An adjustment opposed from both sides

The anti-dumping calculation offers a more visible result. American producers urged Commerce to grant no double-remedy adjustment. They argued that China and the respondent companies had not shown that subsidies reduced export prices in the relevant industries. Commerce disagreed. Information about how subsidized raw materials entered the companies’ accounts, combined with Chinese submissions about industry conditions and broader manufacturing data, gave it a basis for an adjustment.86

This was a consequential administrative choice. Commerce was implementing a new statutory provision under a compressed timetable. It acknowledged that its manufacturing-sector estimate was an imperfect substitute for measuring how particular subsidies affected particular prices. It nonetheless accepted the evidence as sufficient for these proceedings, over the objections of the American petitioners.

China wanted more. It disputed the restriction of the analysis to input subsidies, challenged the comparison of percentage changes in costs and prices, and argued for a full offset. Commerce rejected those arguments. A subsidy that reduced a producer’s costs dollar for dollar did not necessarily lower its selling price by the same amount, the agency reasoned. Changes in output, demand for inputs and market conditions could intervene. China contested the agency’s economic framework as well as its legal interpretation.87

The resulting table placed Aifudi’s original weighted-average dumping margin of 64.28 percent beside a revised anti-dumping cash-deposit rate of 20.19 percent. These are different measures; subtracting them does not establish how much less an importer actually paid. The new instructions applied prospectively to covered entries from August 21, 2012, and intervening administrative reviews could supersede the listed rates. Still, the memorandum records a real decision to recognize an adjustment, not merely a promise to study one.8889

Expertise had mattered at more than one stage. Before the appeal, China’s proposal for an inflation adjustment had persuaded Commerce to alter a loan benchmark used in the tyre investigation, although other Chinese suggestions failed and the benchmark’s overall legality remained unresolved. After the appeal, submissions helped support an anti-dumping adjustment that American producers opposed. Neither result depended on persuading Washington to accept China’s whole description of its economy.90

The practical difficulty was preserving that gain. In 2016, after American domestic litigation and a voluntary remand, Commerce withdrew the sacks anti-dumping adjustment; the Court of International Trade sustained the redetermination. A new notice listed 64.28 percent for the Aifudi exporter–producer combination, applicable from April 11 where an intervening review had not superseded it. This was a domestic proceeding, not a WTO judgment deciding whether the United States had complied with DS379. It demonstrates why a favorable interpretation and an initial implementation decision could leave further work for the exporter and its government.91

Keeping another route open

Four years earlier, China had prepared for continuing disagreement. On May 11, 2012, ambassadors Yi Xiaozhun and Michael Punke signed an agreement specifying how a dispute over compliance would proceed.92 China could request a compliance panel without repeating consultations; if it chose consultations, the governments would meet within fifteen days. The United States would accept establishment of the panel at the first relevant Dispute Settlement Body meeting. Later authorization to suspend concessions depended on a further adverse compliance finding and other specified steps. Washington waived a timing objection while preserving rights to contest the proposed suspension.93

The arrangement gave China a defined procedural option without requiring the Americans to admit noncompliance. It also required China to pursue review before the contemplated retaliation. Precision served both parties: it reduced a future argument over sequence while preserving their opposing substantive positions.

At the WTO on September 28, China’s representative put those elements together. She rejected the American claim of full compliance, welcomed its offer to discuss Chinese concerns, recalled the May agreement and reserved China’s right to take further steps. The American representative repeated the compliance claim and the offer to talk. Discussion, legal objection and a reserved route to adjudication coexisted in the same exchange.94

Chinese legal scholar Chen Weidong drew attention to the strategic problem soon afterward. A favorable interpretation could be valuable even when commercial relief remained limited; further litigation demanded an assessment of its likely return, its evidentiary burden and the time it would consume. His account was an argument about how China should act, not evidence of why officials chose a particular course.95

For a negotiator, the preparation begins before victory: identify what the counterpart would have to change if the argument succeeded, what it could lawfully decide again, and what evidence would be needed in that second proceeding. A demand for fuller explanation can produce useful disclosure and expose weak reasoning. It can also produce a stronger rationale for substantially the same restriction. The advantage of a review procedure is that the counterpart must defend a decision under an agreed standard; its cost is that another defensible decision may still leave trade obstructed.

China was using membership to contest the interpretation of rules and to preserve procedures for further challenge. That is a substantial development from negotiating entry. It is not proof that legal skill had displaced political bargaining, nor that the legal victory had restored profitable access for Aifudi. It had changed what the United States was required to answer, while leaving both governments decisions about how far to carry the dispute.

The route through the WTO has itself become a bargaining question. As of September 2026, its Appellate Body had no members, and an appeal could prevent a panel report from being adopted. China and the European Union participate in an interim appeal-arbitration arrangement; the United States does not. Arbitration under Article 25 requires agreement between the parties. Chinese membership alone therefore cannot give it that route against Washington. The American appeal in the tariff dispute DS543, filed in October 2020, illustrates the obstacle.96 With Europe, the alternative has been used. China and the EU agreed procedures for their intellectual-property dispute in 2023; a final award followed in July 2025. Their agreement required them to abide by it.97 Even then, legal finality did not settle implementation. China announced domestic steps while criticizing the award; the EU continued to assess compliance.98 A negotiator considering litigation must therefore ask two separate questions: what procedure has the other government accepted for reaching a final ruling, and what will still be needed to turn that ruling into changed conduct?

The climate negotiators faced the complementary problem. They were trying to agree on procedures that did not yet exist in their final form. Their task was to make a proposed obligation acceptable to governments before anyone could use it to demand an answer.

Making two different promises together

In January 2014, John Kerry asked Todd Stern what the United States should attempt next with China on climate change. Stern’s team proposed a joint announcement of national targets when Barack Obama visited Beijing that November, a year before the Paris climate conference. Stern liked the idea but doubted whether China would want to associate itself so closely with Washington when it also had developing-country partners to consider. In his later account, he approached Xie Zhenhua in February; Kerry raised the proposal with Xi Jinping and other Chinese leaders. An opening existed. Agreement did not.99

The proposal gave the specialists a problem that neither atmospheric science nor personal rapport could solve by itself. They had to find national actions their own governments would authorize and the other government would accept as sufficient for a public partnership. Each president would be lending political weight to the other’s effort. A domestic target could be defensible at home yet too weak for a counterpart to endorse. Conversely, a target impressive abroad could be impossible to authorize or deliver domestically. Technical work had to make those judgments better informed.

Stern and Xie had been building their working relationship since 2009. Stern recalled that Chinese officials initially wanted to know whether he really spoke with authority. Hillary Clinton’s treatment of him on her first Asian trip helped establish his standing.100 That was a practical matter. Time spent exploring an option with a well-informed official would be wasted if someone else could simply disown it. The question for each envoy was whether the other could carry a proposal into the decisions of his government.

What Copenhagen left unfinished

They had already experienced a more disorderly way to reach agreement. At Copenhagen in December 2009, Obama negotiated with Wen Jiabao and the leaders of Brazil, India and South Africa. Michael Froman, an American participant, recalled that he and Stern thought they had an agreement but were unsure whether China had accepted the listing of its commitments. A note passed through Clinton to Obama, who asked Wen to confirm. Wen agreed. Xie then loudly protested to his premier. Froman took this to mean that Wen had exceeded his mandate, but acknowledged that the Chinese interpreter would not translate the exchange.101

Shyam Saran, India’s climate envoy, remembered the immediate issue differently: Wen was accepting international consultation and analysis. Saran understood Mandarin and recalled Xie objecting to the demands on China and the inadequacy of what Obama offered. In both accounts, Wen let his acceptance stand.102 The witnesses had seen a disagreement inside the Chinese delegation. Neither supplied its written instructions. What the encounter established was narrower and more useful: the specialist’s objection did not prevent the premier from accepting the compromise.

The compromise also had to survive explanation at home. Four days later, India’s environment minister, Jairam Ramesh, faced questions in Parliament about what his government had conceded. He acknowledged moving beyond the information reporting he had previously promised to accept. His defense rested on the protection of sovereignty in guidelines still to be negotiated, and on a collective decision by Brazil, South Africa, India and China—the BASIC group—to avoid responsibility for the conference’s failure. He described pressure from countries seeking climate finance after the Americans linked funding to a transparency agreement.103 China was negotiating alongside partners with their own limits and audiences. Holding the coalition together required deciding which concession its members could defend.

The Chinese government’s published account likewise distinguished a national target kept outside bargaining from flexibility over transparency.104 That substantive distinction matters alongside the institutional explanation. Björn Conrad’s later interview study describes foreign-policy advisers displacing climate specialists in the handling of the summit, but also identifies reasons to resist the proposed distribution of emissions cuts. The presence of an expert could improve the advice available without settling either the allocation of burdens or who would choose the final position.105
