The Copenhagen Accord nevertheless provided for international consultation and analysis of information about developing-country actions. It distinguished that process from the international measurement, reporting and verification attached to actions receiving international support. He Jiankun, a senior Chinese climate expert, publicly described acceptance of consultation and analysis as a flexible adjustment while maintaining the objection to equivalent international verification. He also acknowledged that the guidelines remained to be negotiated. The choice was already more precise than accepting or rejecting scrutiny altogether.106

Copenhagen supplied a further warning for any bilateral initiative: agreement among major leaders could fail to secure assent from the whole conference. The COP took note of the accord rather than adopting it by consensus. Subsequent conferences carried the work forward. Cancun in 2010 specified a facilitative, non-punitive consultation process that excluded judgment on the appropriateness of domestic policies. Durban in 2011 authorized negotiation of an outcome with legal force applicable to all parties. Stern and Xie’s later work took place within that developing multilateral framework.107

Stern and Xie were also using machinery with a history. A memorandum released in July 2009 had provided for regular policy consultations and ministry involvement; the governments reported a climate policy dialogue in 2011. In April 2013 they gave the envoys a more specific assignment: chair a Climate Change Working Group, review existing cooperation and prepare proposals for the Strategic and Economic Dialogue. The July program identified sectoral tasks, required implementation plans defining agency responsibilities, and specified at least two group meetings and one report to the dialogue each year. Senior representatives of both presidents approved it. Stern later described the annual dialogue as a deadline for producing agreements.108 The envoys had a program to prepare and officials responsible for its parts. They still needed an acceptable proposal to put before their leaders.

National choice and reciprocal acceptance

The 2014 proposal asked the two envoys to use that relationship for a larger purpose. Stern recalls roughly nine months of confidential preparation and a shared understanding that neither president would endorse a joint announcement unless comfortable with the other’s ambition.109 Confidentiality allowed discussion before either government had publicly committed itself to the package. It did not give the envoys authority to choose the national numbers on their own.

Xie’s contemporary account supplies the Chinese side of that distinction. Interviewed by Caixin three days after the announcement, he foregrounded Zhang Gaoli’s September message to Obama and the teams’ subsequent exchanges. He said they had agreed not to negotiate one another’s targets, then acknowledged that some discussion was unavoidable. Both sides’ experts assessed the other’s effort as well as their own. A joint release required mutual acceptability. The statement was finalized on the announcement day, he said.110

Xie also rejected the idea that a Chinese target emerged from a single technical calculation. Researchers had spent nearly two years examining the emissions peak and disagreed: some put it in 2025, others around 2030, and others thought 2030 unattainable. The central leadership and State Council chose among competing considerations. In his explanation, the target was also intended to push domestic economic restructuring. International bargaining operated alongside a Chinese policy project with its own reasons for action.111

The announcement released in Beijing on November 12 paired visibly different intentions. The United States intended an economy-wide greenhouse-gas reduction of 26–28 percent below its 2005 level by 2025, with best efforts toward 28 percent. China intended carbon dioxide emissions to peak around 2030, with best efforts to peak earlier, and non-fossil fuels to supply about 20 percent of primary energy consumption by 2030. Neither the gases covered nor the form and date of the targets were identical. The document did not convert them into a common percentage reduction. It joined them to a commitment to work toward a successful Paris outcome.112

The arrangement let both governments preserve national choice while putting their intentions before the other participants well before Paris. Its diplomatic value lay partly in this sequencing: a counterpart could see a stated course of action instead of waiting for a final summit to discover what the other might offer. The announcement also connected ambition to further technical cooperation. Whether either country would achieve its target was a later question; announcing it together was an immediate political act.

The record does not show a numerical trade in which an American increment purchased a specified Chinese increment. It shows an agreement to announce nationally chosen actions together, conditional on finding each other’s effort acceptable. For a negotiator, it identifies a possible transaction even when the counterpart insists that a particular domestic decision is not itself for negotiation.

Taking a bilateral formula into a multilateral negotiation

An immediate test came within weeks at Lima. Observers who expected the presidential announcement to dissolve established divisions were disappointed. The Earth Negotiations Bulletin found that high-level signals had not immediately produced fundamental changes in negotiating positions. Differentiation—how responsibilities should vary among countries—remained disputed, as did finance. The bilateral announcement had created a resource for negotiation; it had not instructed every other government to agree.113

Stern and Xie did find a use for its language. Reporting from the close of the conference, Ed King described a late meeting between the two envoys and quoted Stern saying they had offered the formulation that reached the final text. It coupled common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities with consideration of different national circumstances. Stern did not expect the dispute to disappear. UN climate chief Christiana Figueres saw an important opening in the formulation; India’s Prakash Javadekar emphasized the preservation of differentiation and expected further accommodation from developed countries. The same words could support different expectations about the next bargain.114

The adopted Lima decision confirms the language but leaves consequential matters open. Its arrangements for intended national contributions were without prejudice to their legal nature and content. That reservation explains why an apparent success over a general principle still required work. Countries had accepted a formula through which responsibilities could be discussed; they had not agreed every obligation that would follow from it.115

By September 2015 Obama and Xi could endorse a more specific approach to transparency. Their second joint climate statement supported reporting and review of both action and support, with flexibility for developing countries needing it in light of their capacities. This made capacity and need relevant to flexibility, while retaining differentiation. It gave negotiators a more definite proposition than either identical treatment for every country or a general developing-country exemption.116

Finance remained differently structured. The same statement urged continued support from developed countries and encouraged other willing countries to contribute. The United States reiterated its pledge to the Green Climate Fund; China announced a separate South–South cooperation fund. The countries could both offer assistance without accepting identical responsibilities or using the same institution. A useful compromise on transparency did not have to settle finance on precisely the same terms.117

At Paris, those distinctions were still being fought over. China and Saudi Arabia objected to a proposed facilitative dialogue that they feared could become advance review of national contributions. The United States and European Union presented it as information for the next round. China’s broader position also insisted on balance across the negotiations’ components and leadership by developed countries. Agreement with Washington on a presidential statement had not eliminated concern about what a new procedure might authorize.118

The conference required other negotiators to work through these problems. Under the French presidency, Brazil’s Izabella Teixeira and Singapore’s Vivian Balakrishnan facilitated consultations on differentiation across mitigation, transparency and finance. On December 9 Balakrishnan reported that parties were still withholding their final positions. The task was not simply to transmit a bilateral bargain into a global text. Different governments had to judge how the obligations fitted together, and whether their own priorities had sufficient protection.119

The treaty text adopted on December 12 made that fit more explicit. Parties would have to prepare, communicate and maintain successive nationally determined contributions and pursue domestic measures aimed at their objectives. Achievement of each announced national result was not itself an internationally binding obligation. Reporting and review requirements made a different kind of demand, with capacity-related flexibility; the compliance mechanism was facilitative and non-punitive. Developed countries retained a distinct obligation to provide financial resources, while other parties were encouraged to provide support voluntarily.120

Those distinctions answer the problem Stern and Xie had been working on. National discretion over ambition could coexist with international obligations about stating, pursuing and reporting action. A negotiator could recommend accepting a review procedure without conceding that other governments would set the country’s emissions target. Another could obtain more information without securing an enforceable promise that every target would be met. The value of the agreement depended on knowing which of those results had actually been obtained. Adoption of the text was itself only a stage; consent to be bound and delivery of national action followed through separate decisions.

The comparison with the trade dispute is practical. In DS379, a Chinese argument could reach an adjudicator despite American opposition. Stern and Xie were negotiating rules whose acceptance still depended on governments with different interests. Their specialists could estimate effort, identify feasible policies and draft a procedure that preserved a political limit. Their leaders could authorize the package. Neither technical proficiency nor a strong relationship could supply the assent of the rest of the conference. What they made possible was a joint proposal for cooperation—and a way to keep negotiating the points it had not resolved.

What a specialist can change

An official could use a legal standard to demand a new answer, or technical work to recommend a proposal that another government might endorse. The first required an available institution; the second required political assent.

Before investing in another round of expert work, a negotiator needs to identify the decision it could alter. Better evidence might change an administrative calculation, reveal that a proposed target was credible, or show why an apparent concession would be unusable. Where the disagreement concerned what a government was willing to accept, further calculation could clarify the price without supplying the authority to pay it. In a crisis, that distinction could become urgent. Prueher knew a great deal about aircraft; getting his crew home required another kind of decision.

4. Negotiating in a crisis

Joseph Prueher knew enough about flying to be confident in his argument. A former naval aviator, now the American ambassador in Beijing, he was negotiating the return of the twenty-four crew members whose damaged EP-3 reconnaissance aircraft had landed on Hainan on April 1, 2001, after colliding with a Chinese fighter. He disputed the Chinese account of the collision. His counterpart, Zhou Wenzhong, continued to insist on American responsibility. In Prueher’s recollection, the argument had reached an impasse.121

Kenneth Lieberthal, a former Clinton administration official then visiting Beijing, advised him to stop trying to settle fault. Prueher had already obtained Colin Powell’s approval for his approach; changing it meant going back to the secretary of state. He recalled Powell’s reluctance and eventual permission to proceed. The immediate task became finding an arrangement that could bring the crew home while leaving responsibility disputed.

Zhou later described the Chinese task in similarly practical terms. In an interview conducted in July 2018, he recalled a leadership instruction to release the people in exchange for an apology. He said Chinese negotiators preferred the English verb apologize, while the Americans resisted; China eventually accepted very sorry. His recollection supplies a Chinese participant’s account of a conditional mandate, although neither its original wording nor the instruction itself has been recovered. It also shows a defended demand acquiring a form the other side could accept.122

The two participants did not subsequently agree about who had caused the collision. Their recollections nevertheless identify room for a bargain inside that disagreement. The public documents establish what the governments finally did with it. On April 11, Prueher delivered a letter to Foreign Minister Tang Jiaxuan, and China authorized the crew’s departure. The aircraft, reconnaissance flights and responsibility for the incident remained subjects of further negotiation.123

What could be conceded

At the start, the demands had been much broader. The Chinese pilot was missing, the American crew and aircraft were on Hainan, and both governments asserted rights the other disputed. On April 3, China’s Foreign Ministry called for acceptance of responsibility, an explanation, an apology and prevention of repetition. Bush demanded the return of both crew and aircraft and warned of damage to the wider relationship. Each government presented its practical demands as consequences of its account of what had happened.124

For Washington, sympathetic language posed a specific problem. What could the United States say without accepting the Chinese legal argument? A contemporaneous packet from the Pentagon shows officials working on that distinction. On April 6, acting Defense General Counsel Daniel Dell’Orto sent the defense secretary and deputy secretary guidance developed by Defense and Joint Chiefs lawyers in consultation with State’s Legal Adviser. Its talking points expressed regret for the Chinese loss while defending the flight, emergency landing and aircraft immunity. The responsibility argument was partly conditional on the reported conduct of the Chinese fighter.125

The lawyers also attended to the setting in which a position would be stated. One passage specified an oral, off-camera response if pressed. A clearance sheet recorded agreement among State, Defense, the Joint Chiefs and the relevant regional office on aircraft-immunity guidance. Officials could prepare a firm legal defense without requiring every point to appear in the most visible statement. The packet still demanded immediate aircraft release; it did not yet supply the crew-first arrangement eventually accepted.

The April 11 letter combined sympathy, preserved positions and prospective action. It conveyed sorrow for the Chinese loss. Its account of the landing described a severely damaged aircraft following emergency procedures and expressed regret about the absence of verbal clearance. It did not accept the Chinese account of the collision. Prueher’s later shorthand about China not hearing a landing request differs from these delivered words; the released letter is the evidence for the formulation accepted.126

The practical provisions were just as consequential. The letter recorded agreement to meet, stated the American expectation of prompt crew departure and set April 18 for talks. Developing a plan for the aircraft’s return was a future task. Acknowledging China’s intention to raise reconnaissance was not a promise to end the flights. Tang’s acceptance announcement supplied the immediate counterpart action: China would permit departure after necessary formalities, while treating the incident as unfinished.

Each side could therefore act without publicly adopting the other’s whole account. The limited bargain did not erase the dispute; it prevented that dispute from holding every decision in place. Its value to Washington lay first in the people who could leave. Its cost was that the plane and the broader questions remained exposed to another negotiation.

An agreement that had to be explained

The Chinese government still had to present the result. The Foreign Ministry called Prueher’s letter an apology and published selected passages using 深表歉意, an expression of being deeply sorry. It did not publish a complete Chinese counterpart to the English release. The omission of the American emergency-landing explanation was therefore an act of selection, not simply an error in translating an otherwise parallel document.127

At Sun Yuxi’s April 11 briefing, a journalist asked the difficult question directly. China had demanded an apology—道歉—but had allowed the crew to leave after an expression of deep sorrow. Had Beijing compromised? Had Washington given other undertakings? Sun defended the official character of the American letter, invoked humanitarian considerations and emphasized that negotiation would continue. The government’s account of success was being questioned even as it announced the decision.

An expression intended to preserve Washington’s position could be presented in Beijing as meeting China’s demand and still be criticized there as inadequate. Finding acceptable words did not require the two publics to attach identical meanings to them. It did require each government to accept the action those words made possible. The missing drafts and Chinese clearance record prevent identifying the exact moment at which wording, wider costs or the package secured that acceptance.

The American clearance was publicly acknowledged. On April 11, White House spokesman Ari Fleischer said Bush had approved everything in the letter. He described Prueher delivering a signed copy and receiving verbal assurances of release early that morning in Washington time; the Chinese report of delivery at 5:30 p.m. in Beijing fits that sequence. The ambassador’s signature did not remove presidential responsibility for the commitment.128

Approval also depended on information reaching people able to decide. Prueher recalled using additional Chinese channels when he doubted that Foreign Ministry reporting was reaching the leadership. He did not know which had helped. Having studied Solomon, he understood these encounters partly through the book’s account of Chinese behavior.129 His recollection is evidence of a participant seeking other routes, not proof that any particular route changed the decision.

Tyler Jost’s institutional study offers an explanation for the earlier impasse: separated Chinese military and diplomatic information channels limited the scrutiny of the initial account.130 An ambassador may have to act before knowing whether the difficulty is faulty reporting or a deliberate refusal. Another authorized channel can carry the same defined request and seek a specific response. If it instead carries a different offer, the negotiator risks creating a dispute over what his own government has promised. Prueher’s uncertainty about which route helped is a reason to keep the request clear, even when its passage through the other government remains obscure.

The aircraft followed a different clock

At the April 18 talks, China continued to press its account of responsibility and its objections to reconnaissance. The repetition did not itself repudiate the letter: the earlier arrangement had reserved those questions. The Americans had obtained the crew’s release without obtaining the return of their aircraft.131

That second task exposed a disagreement between the participants’ later accounts. Prueher remembered an opportunity to fly the repaired aircraft out that was lost after the American approach changed. Zhou said China would not accept a flyout and insisted on disassembly. Neither recollection supplies the complete dated offer and approval record needed to resolve the conflict.132 Treating one as the missing Chinese permission would give memory the authority of a recovered instruction.

The contemporary record traces a narrower sequence. On April 29, China announced that it had finished collecting evidence and would allow American personnel to inspect the plane. On May 29, it announced agreement in principle on removing the disassembled aircraft aboard a commercial An-124. Technical arrangements remained to be made.

The Pentagon’s June 7 briefing gives some of that work a concrete form: runway loads, loading and flight requirements, technicians, equipment and local support. Rear Admiral Craig Quigley expected component removal to take weeks and reported no communications hindrance under the arrangements reached. China’s July 3 statement then described completed disassembly and removal while payment negotiations remained open.133

The agreement on transport changed what Washington needed next. Before May 29, the method of removal was disputed; afterward, technicians still needed permissions, equipment and a workable loading plan. An official seeking faster implementation could ask the two sides to list the unfinished tasks, identify who must perform or authorize each, and agree on the next observable step. That would not reveal the motive behind every delay. It could help establish whether a further political concession would address the obstacle at all.

In 2023, the channel became part of the dispute

Twenty-two years later, a Chinese balloon over the United States produced another confrontation about surveillance, sovereignty and responsibility. There was no detained crew to negotiate out. One of the things Washington wanted was communication itself: a military contact that would remain usable when relations deteriorated. Solomon had limited the transfer of his findings from the construction of cooperative relations to confrontation. Here the distinction was concrete. A channel for managing danger had become something governments could withhold.134

The balloon episode began against an already damaged institutional background. In August 2022, after Nancy Pelosi’s visit to Taiwan, the Chinese Foreign Ministry announced countermeasures cancelling arrangements for theater-commander calls, Defense Policy Coordination Talks and Military Maritime Consultative Agreement meetings. It also suspended cooperation in five other areas, including counternarcotics and climate. These were identified mechanisms, not the severance of all diplomatic relations.135

That baseline matters. Military channels were not all lost in a single February 2023 rupture, and their later restoration cannot be described simply as undoing the balloon incident. China had already publicly made some mechanisms contingent on American political conduct. An institution designed to manage danger could itself be withheld as a response to an objectionable act.

On February 3, Beijing acknowledged that the airship was Chinese and expressed regret. It described a civilian research craft, limited in its ability to steer, that winds had taken off course. Its explanation invoked force majeure and unintended entry. It did not concede a surveillance mission. In Washington’s account of Blinken’s call with Wang Yi that day, the secretary acknowledged the regret but characterized the presence as an irresponsible violation that undermined his planned visit. He postponed travel while affirming the intention to engage when conditions allowed.136

The American postponement is a necessary counterpart observation. Washington too could make a particular encounter conditional on political circumstances. The important difference was which encounters each side sought to insulate from the deterioration. The United States wanted military contact during a crisis even when a diplomatic visit had become untenable. Beijing publicly linked acceptance of a ministerial military call to the atmosphere the American response had created.

American aircraft destroyed the balloon on February 4. Austin’s statement characterized it as a surveillance platform and explained the delay in shooting it down by danger to people on the ground. China’s response protested the use of force, repeated the civilian and accidental-entry account and reserved further action. These were incompatible public justifications, not a mutually accepted description of an incident awaiting only a suitable apology.137

The military refusal is directly acknowledged by China. On February 9, Defense Ministry spokesman Tan Kefei said the American use of force had failed to create the proper atmosphere for dialogue, and China had therefore not accepted the proposed call between defense ministers. This connects the refusal to a stated condition. It does not specify an apology, payment or operational concession that would satisfy that condition. The evidence establishes conditional access more securely than it establishes its price.138

Nor was this an unanswered ordinary telephone in the simple sense sometimes suggested by the word hotline. Contemporary AP reporting described a process for requesting an encrypted call or video conference. A much-cited recollection of a phone merely ringing referred to an incident in 2009. The distinction matters: a technical connection can exist while political permission to use it is withheld. Building more reliable communication requires knowing which of those failures has occurred.139

Diplomacy continued in February. Wang and Blinken met in Munich on February 18. The Chinese account described informal contact at American request and demanded correction of the damage caused by the use of force. The American account recorded demands about the airspace violation, concern about support to Russia and an insistence on keeping diplomatic dialogue open. The accounts corroborate the encounter while preserving disagreement. They rule out a chronology in which all senior communication stopped until June.140

Reopening one channel while another remained closed

Before Sullivan and Wang could use a strategic channel, their officials had to negotiate how it would work. Former Biden officials interviewed by Christopher Chivvis and Senkai Hsia described months of preparation: which subjects could be raised, how meetings would stay out of public view and what would be said afterward. Sullivan’s ability to travel discreetly helped recommend him for the task. From Washington’s perspective, predictable arrangements could do some of the work that missing trust could not.141

The American account describes an initial small dinner in Vienna, followed by extended conversations. Within the US government, officials also had to decide which messages belonged in this channel and which should go through other departments. That choice mattered: a senior encounter could be consumed by subjects better handled elsewhere, while the issue requiring high-level attention went unresolved. These recollections make the American preparation visible; they do not supply Beijing’s private assessment of the same arrangements.

The May 10–11 Sullivan–Wang meeting in Vienna supplies an observable result. Both governments recorded substantive exchanges and agreement to maintain the strategic communication channel. The American account emphasized responsible management of competition; the Chinese one emphasized removing obstacles and arresting deterioration. They could agree to continue a channel while differing over the purpose of the relationship it was meant to manage.142143

Later that month, the proposed Austin–Li Shangfu meeting in Singapore remained unsuccessful. Li was under American sanctions, and the two governments disagreed over what that meant for contact. Answering a question about the proposed meeting on May 22, Foreign Ministry spokeswoman Mao Ning demanded withdrawal of sanctions to remove obstacles. In Washington, State spokesman Matthew Miller denied that lifting Li’s sanctions was being considered. American officials maintained that the sanctions did not prevent official meetings. Washington offered contact without delisting Li; Beijing called for sanctions relief to create the conditions for dialogue.144

The Chinese Defense Ministry’s May 31 answer did not give a formula in which removing Li’s sanction would guarantee the meeting. It emphasized principles, corrected American conduct and an appropriate atmosphere. It also stated that military contacts had not been interrupted altogether. The objects being withheld remained specific.145

Blinken’s June visit made the distinction still clearer. He met Qin Gang on June 18 and Wang and Xi on June 19. The Chinese account of the Qin meeting listed agreements to maintain senior exchanges, pursue consultations and working groups, encourage exchanges and discuss additional flights. Qin accepted an invitation to visit the United States at a suitable time. The practical work of arranging visits, flights and further consultations remained ahead.146

On the military question, Blinken was explicit. Asked at his Beijing press availability whether the Chinese side had agreed to crisis or military communication channels, he said it had not and that there had been no immediate progress. He nevertheless defended continuing diplomacy. Access to Xi was compatible with failure to obtain this particular requested outcome.147

The June statements also prevent a broader exaggeration. Wang called for an end to unilateral sanctions and suppression of Chinese technological development. Xi disputed the American conception of great-power competition. Blinken reaffirmed targeted national-security restrictions and the American Taiwan policy, including the Taiwan Relations Act, the three communiqués and the Six Assurances. Repeating opposition to Taiwan independence did not amount to accepting all of Beijing’s demands. The encounter supported further engagement while leaving those positions in conflict.

What an old friend could do

Henry Kissinger arrived the next month. Li Shangfu received him on July 18, Wang Yi on July 19 and Xi on July 20. Xi’s welcome invoked his hundredth birthday, his many visits and the opening of relations more than fifty years earlier. Kissinger thanked Xi for choosing the Diaoyutai building associated with his first visit. The meeting offered a conspicuous example of personal recognition persisting across generations of Chinese leaders.148

There was also a practical appeal. Xi asked Kissinger and other Americans favoring better relations to contribute to improving them. Wang called for diplomatic judgment and political courage associated with Kissinger and Nixon, while repeating demands on Taiwan. A longer account of the Li meeting, republished from the Defense Ministry, attributed support for better military communication to Kissinger. It did not announce that Li had authorized official talks. Access and advocacy were visible; a resulting instruction was not.149

Kissinger reached a defense minister whom Austin had been unable to meet through the desired formal channel. Yet the official diplomatic track was already active: Blinken had met Xi in June, and Wang and Blinken met in Jakarta before Kissinger’s arrival. The visit’s meaning depended on what his particular access could accomplish, beyond the recognition on display.

Washington said he was not acting on the government’s behalf. John Kirby nevertheless expressed interest in hearing what he had learned, and Blinken later acknowledged seeking his counsel in connection with China travel and work on artificial intelligence.150151 Private status did not make him irrelevant to serving officials. Nor does that acknowledgment identify a message from July that changed an instruction or produced the November agreements.

Kissinger’s reception cannot stand for the access available to an ordinary counterpart. During research in the early Xi years, Dylan Loh encountered reports of tighter restrictions on informal contact with Chinese diplomats. A European diplomat described an intended private dinner becoming an encounter with the appearance of an official meeting. Other foreign diplomats reported Chinese counterparts arriving with colleagues; Loh observed accompanied meetings himself. Such constraints could change the way a relationship developed without demonstrating that the Chinese participant disliked the visitor or rejected the proposal. Loh also found room for disagreement before a decision, followed by discipline in defending it. The practical difficulty was to find a format the official could use and a proposal worth carrying through that process.152

An official considering an intermediary like Kissinger must decide what the person is being asked to do. Explain a proposal, test a warning, seek information and negotiate a commitment are different assignments. Kissinger’s reception demonstrated access and a Chinese effort to enlist his help. The public record does not trace that help through to a particular decision. A valuable relationship remains a resource whose usefulness depends on the task it can perform.

A summit agreement, then implementation

By autumn, the sanctions objection was explicit in the Defense Ministry’s own account. On August 31, spokesman Wu Qian listed sanctions on Central Military Commission leaders alongside Taiwan arms support and American military activity near China. He demanded changes in American conduct while again denying that all military contact had stopped. These were stated obstacles; his answer did not identify which had to be removed before any particular mechanism could resume.153

Li’s removal as defense minister on October 24 changed one part of the problem. The decree named no successor and gave no reason for his dismissal. Two days later, Wu announced planned exchanges with a visiting American defense official while continuing to invoke sovereignty, dignity and core interests. Working-level contact could be arranged while the ministerial seat was vacant. Li’s departure did not itself announce a new policy on the suspended mechanisms.154

At the November 15 Woodside summit, the two public accounts finally converged on named military mechanisms: high-level military communication, Defense Policy Coordination Talks, Military Maritime Consultative Agreement meetings and telephone conversations between theater commanders. The Chinese account specified equality and respect. This was more definite than a favorable description of the atmosphere, but the readouts supplied no guaranteed response time or unconditional promise that every mechanism would remain available during the next confrontation.155

The following day, the Pentagon said Austin would meet his counterpart when one was named. The broader agreement had preceded the appointment Washington awaited. Practical work on meetings and calls still lay ahead. Removing an awkward interlocutor may have helped, but the public record does not establish that Li’s dismissal caused the wider decision to resume communications.156

The summit also produced commitments on counternarcotics and government AI discussions. One adjacent American concession can be documented particularly well. A Commerce rule removed the Ministry of Public Security’s Institute of Forensic Science from the Entity List, effective November 16. The rule establishes the act and its date. Separately, a named State spokesman, Matthew Miller, explained that the listing had obstructed precursor-chemical cooperation and that China’s proposed steps justified removal.157

The documented exchange concerned counternarcotics; it does not establish the price of the military-channel agreement. Human-rights advocates still objected to lifting the restriction. Washington had accepted a policy cost for prospective cooperation whose value would depend on subsequent Chinese action.

The paired summit readouts continued to register disagreement over Taiwan and technology restrictions. Their value lies partly in that coexistence: a commitment to specified communication was compatible with unresolved rivalry. The parties had identified things they were prepared to do, rather than reached a comprehensive common understanding of each other’s conduct.

On November 30, China’s defense spokesman said the departments were communicating and coordinating implementation. Both the Chinese and official English formulations placed the resumption of the mechanisms ahead of them. An agreement to restore a channel had reached a follow-up stage; the statement did not establish that all the mechanisms had already convened. This chapter ends at that boundary, rather than borrowing later contacts to make the November announcement appear complete when issued.158

What changes between the two crises

Both episodes produced useful action short of a shared account of the dispute. In 2001, the immediate crew arrangement left responsibility, reconnaissance and the aircraft to other decisions. In 2023, renewed diplomatic contact and commitments to restore military mechanisms coexisted with disagreement about Taiwan, technology and the balloon. The continuity is the possibility of a limited commitment whose scope matters more than a declaration that the relationship has improved.

The bargaining objects differed. In 2001, people and an aircraft could be separated and moved through identifiable release and transport decisions. In 2023, several of the objects were institutions for repeated contact. Their value depended on future willingness to use them, including at moments when politics made communication difficult. That creates a distinctive implementation question: an announced channel is useful in a crisis only if the relevant officials will authorize participation and can act on what they hear.

This is not a clean estimate of historical change in Chinese character. The incidents differed in location, loss, custody, political background and desired outcomes. The comparison instead identifies a change in the problem a counterpart must solve. Establishing contact and obtaining a usable commitment are separate tasks; with institutionalized channels, maintaining contact can itself become a continuing negotiation.

Solomon’s strongest continuing contribution is to direct attention toward purpose, authority, the presentation of prior undertakings and the political uses of access. These cases make those questions more specific. Which contact was refused? Which action was authorized? Which unresolved issue was reserved? Which message reached someone able to decide? The answers explain more than a general claim that Chinese negotiators value relationships or protect face. They also identify where such explanations remain plausible and what evidence would make them persuasive. The next chapter returns to 2018–2020, when trade negotiators made the consequences of a failed consultation part of a formal bargain.

5. A partial settlement under pressure

In the Oval Office on February 22, 2019, Liu He found himself agreeing with the American trade representative while the American president disagreed. The subject was the document they were negotiating. Robert Lighthizer explained that a memorandum of understanding was a contract. Liu said yes. Donald Trump objected: the memorandum would have to become a final contract before it meant much to him. Lighthizer offered a solution. They would keep the document and call it a trade agreement. Liu accepted the new name too.159

The exchange was comic, but the problem beneath it was serious. What would make another promise worth accepting? To Trump, the title was inadequate. To Lighthizer, the document needed a way to make China perform. To Beijing, an acceptable agreement had to offer relief from American tariffs while preserving a defensible measure of control over China’s own affairs. The negotiators were bargaining over the authority each government would retain after they signed.

Lighthizer made his concern concrete five days later, before the House Ways and Means Committee. Companies would bring complaints to his office, sometimes anonymously because they feared the consequences of being identified. Officials would meet monthly; unresolved matters would rise through quarterly meetings of deputies to the trade representative and a Chinese vice premier. If the principals could not agree, he wanted the United States to be able to act proportionately and unilaterally. He was proposing a means of dealing with the next dispute before the present one was over.160

It took most of the year to reach an agreement containing such a procedure. In May the negotiation broke down over a draft whose disputed provisions have never been made available to this project. In October Trump accepted a first phase. In January 2020 he and Liu signed it. China undertook specific changes and substantial additional purchases; the United States accepted a limited settlement and left substantial tariffs in place. Each government kept instruments with which to press the other. Their success lay in finding a bargain they could accept without settling the conflict that had brought them to the table.161

The pause that did not hold

The problem of what had been promised was already a year old. On May 19, 2018, Liu, acting as Xi Jinping’s special envoy, concluded talks with Treasury Secretary Steven Mnuchin, Commerce Secretary Wilbur Ross and Lighthizer. Their joint statement described work on purchases, market access, investment and intellectual property. It expressly contemplated amendments to Chinese laws, including the Patent Law. It supplied neither an overall purchase figure nor a tariff suspension.162

The next day, both sides publicly described a pause. Xinhua reported Liu saying they had agreed against a trade war and additional tariffs. Mnuchin told an American television audience that the trade war was on hold while the governments developed the framework. Other American officials emphasized what remained to be done: Larry Kudlow denied that a completed trade deal existed, and Lighthizer stressed structural reform.163

Liu had an American statement to point to, but no published common text specifying how long the pause would last or what would keep it in force. That left room for the next disagreement. On May 29 the White House announced that it would impose 25 percent tariffs on $50 billion of Chinese goods. After Ross returned to Beijing, China’s June 3 statement reported progress while reserving final confirmation of the details. It then attached an explicit condition: if the United States imposed trade sanctions, including tariffs, the negotiated outcomes would not take effect. On June 15 Washington confirmed its tariff decision and threatened further action if China retaliated.164

For an official preparing the next Chinese offer, this sequence created a problem that a cordial relationship with Mnuchin could not solve. Would an understanding reached with one American official survive the president’s decision and the demands of the rest of his administration? For the American side, accepting a pause without a completed structural bargain risked surrendering pressure for purchases and further discussion. These were different tests of whether the talks were succeeding.

Beijing’s September white paper made American changes of position part of its indictment. It paired a case against tariff pressure with a declaration that China would continue opening its economy at its own pace. China was claiming both willingness to negotiate and the right to control the terms of its reform.165

Neither the later indictment nor the published May statement settles the full scope of the oral understanding. The practical weakness is nevertheless visible. Both sides had announced restraint without publishing the conditions under which it would endure. The June Chinese statement tried to remove that ambiguity from the package then under discussion: the additional goods and the absence of new American sanctions were to travel together.

Permission to negotiate

Trump and Xi reopened the process over dinner in Buenos Aires on December 1. The American account described postponing a scheduled tariff increase while the teams negotiated structural issues for approximately ninety days. It promised substantial Chinese purchases but said the amount remained unagreed. Wang Yi’s account presented the leaders’ understanding as principles from which negotiators would develop a concrete agreement. He described a pause on additional tariffs, purchases responsive to China’s needs, and talks toward removing the additional duties. He gave no purchase figure or ninety-day deadline.166

The leaders had supplied political permission to try again. Their teams still had to decide what China would change, what the United States would lift, and how either side could respond to a failure. Lighthizer continued to stress enforceability in his February testimony. As late as April 23, the White House’s announcement of another negotiating visit listed purchases and enforcement among the subjects still to be discussed.167

The tariff deadline itself was negotiable. In February, with Liu present, Trump said he could extend it if the talks made sufficient progress. The subsequent tariff notice records the postponement to March 2 and then until further notice.168169 Chinese officials could see that the clock was under presidential control. That knowledge gave them a reason to test what an extension would cost; it did not make the threat of higher duties harmless.

Solomon taught American negotiators to look for the moment when a Chinese leader entered a negotiation and detailed drafting began. Both were visible here, yet neither answered the question that would destroy the spring bargain: had the governments accepted the same obligations, or were their delegates still working on terms that one principal would refuse? A senior encounter can authorize more bargaining. An agreement still requires an authorized text.

In March 2018, Beijing had converted its central foreign-affairs leading group into a commission, chaired by Xi and charged with coordinating policy and supervising implementation. The published arrangements did not disclose which terms Liu could accept or what approvals his draft required.170

The missing agreement

On May 8 Reuters reported that Beijing had sent back extensive revisions to a draft approaching 150 pages. Its sources described changes across seven chapters, especially the removal of commitments to change Chinese laws. Liu, they said, proposed administrative and regulatory action instead. Mnuchin and Lighthizer considered that inadequate. A Chinese official familiar with the talks disputed the accusation of backtracking, emphasized the procedures required to amend laws, and said American demands had become harsher. Subsidies and biotechnology approvals were still sticking points.171

The domestic form of a promise was worth fighting over. If a concession depended on an administrative instruction, a foreign company would need to know who had received it and whether another official could reverse or disregard it. A published legal change could offer a different basis for challenging an adverse decision. For Chinese negotiators, an American demand to prescribe changes to domestic law could carry costs that the same substantive commitment, implemented through Chinese procedures, did not. Neither concern was answered by renaming the document.

Washington treated the revisions as a retreat from commitments already made. Its May 9 tariff notice put that accusation into an operative decision: the additional rate on the specified goods would rise from 10 to 25 percent, under the notice’s entry and export conditions.172 Liu continued to Washington for the May 9–10 round. Afterwards, he identified three unresolved Chinese concerns: all additional tariffs should be removed; purchases should be realistic; and the text should be balanced and respect national dignity. He also disputed the legitimacy of accusing a side of retreat while negotiations were still in progress.173

Each of Liu’s concerns affected the price of the other two. Beijing was being asked to accept domestic changes and substantial purchases while Washington sought to retain the means of coercion. Beijing wanted the existing additional tariffs removed; Washington wanted a means of imposing costs if China failed to perform. The parties were disputing the allocation of risk as much as the wording of reforms.

China’s June white paper defended revisions to an unfinished agreement as normal negotiating practice. It alleged changes in American demands and rejected what it described as interference in sovereign affairs.174 That defense did not require denying every deletion reported by Reuters. A negotiator can alter a draft and maintain that no promise has been broken. The answer depends on whether the earlier language was accepted, conditional or awaiting clearance.

Here the documentary history has a real break. Reuters reported from government sources and people briefed on the talks; it did not publish the draft. The record available to this book cannot settle whether Liu had exceeded his instructions, Xi had reversed an authorized bargain, or American officials had mistaken provisional agreement for final assent. It does establish a serious conflict over obligations, domestic implementation and the compensation offered for accepting them. Treating the episode as a demonstration of Chinese dishonesty would conceal those unresolved choices. Treating it as mere misunderstanding would minimize the costs of the breakdown.

Before pricing the whole package, an official can ask both delegations to record which provisions they regard as accepted, which depend on the rest of the bargain and which await a decision in their capitals. Such a record cannot verify private instructions or prevent reversal. It can expose a difference over the status of the draft while there is still time to negotiate its consequences. A refusal to give that account leaves a risk to be priced; it does not by itself prove deception.

The bargain becomes smaller

By September, according to reporting in the Wall Street Journal, Chinese officials were seeking to separate the trade negotiation from national-security disputes. The public result emerged in the Oval Office on October 11. Trump was now defending a deal in phases. Lighthizer told reporters that Huawei was outside the agreement.175

The details of the meeting revealed both a concession and an unfinished bargain. Mnuchin said Liu had requested, and Trump had approved, suspension of the increase from 25 to 30 percent scheduled for the following week. The December tariffs remained a separate presidential decision. Asked about enforcement, Lighthizer described an agreed structure for consultations and escalation, but said the consequences of an unresolved dispute still needed final work. Mnuchin added that Liu had work to do with his own team. The president’s announcement had not disposed of those questions.176

In December Chinese officials presented the first-stage agreement as a useful exchange. Some planned and existing American duties would be removed. Stronger intellectual-property protection, financial opening and imports could serve Chinese development; the agricultural package also contained benefits for Chinese exports. The officials defended the bargain as compatible with domestic reform, rather than a surrender of the right to direct it.177

This was less tariff relief than Liu had demanded in May. But China was also accepting a different package: a first stage with further disputes left outside it. Washington had changed its demand for closure too. Trump defended division into phases and kept tariffs as pressure for performance and subsequent negotiation.178 The two governments had reduced the number of disagreements that had to be settled at once.

The relief came in separate acts. The December notice suspended the duties planned for December 15. The January 22 notice then prescribed a cut from 15 to 7.5 percent on the relevant List 4A goods, effective February 14. Announcing relief, suspending a future charge and reducing an existing rate were different concessions, delivered on different dates.179

Partial agreement was familiar territory for Solomon. The lesson from 2019 is more particular than an injunction to be flexible. A position presented as indispensable to a comprehensive bargain may be negotiable in exchange for a smaller set of obligations. The test is to change the package and ask for a price. Demanding that the other side first renounce its principle can make it harder to discover which transactions remain possible within it.

Whose reform?

The signed agreement offered a way to combine Chinese domestic control with specified changes. Article 1.34 left each party to choose appropriate implementation methods within its own system and practice, including proposing legal amendments where necessary. It also required full implementation. China had discretion over means, not a general exemption from the promised obligations.180

The intellectual-property chapter shows what that meant. It addressed evidentiary and criminal-enforcement rules, confidentiality and penalties. Many provisions required Chinese changes while affirming that existing American measures already provided equivalent treatment. Equal standing as a party did not mean an equal number of new domestic burdens.181

The agreement therefore required China to prepare an intellectual-property action plan specifying measures and effective dates within thirty working days after entry into force. Its English and Chinese verbs differ over promulgation and formulation; the shared requirement for a dated plan is clear. The plan would give officials a schedule against which to pursue implementation.182

After the signing, Liu described the deal to Chinese reporters as consistent with China’s reform program. In the same briefing, he said that reform would proceed under Communist Party leadership and the socialist system. This was his public explanation of how the concession fitted the political order he represented.183 The foreign negotiator should take both parts seriously: a reform may be useful to Chinese leaders without entailing acceptance of the foreign government’s preferred economic system.

Lighthizer had described a similarly specific boundary to Congress. When Linda Sánchez asked about obtaining a larger share of Chinese box-office receipts for American studios, he called revenue-sharing a priority. Distribution was more complicated. He said the negotiation was not taking on “challenging control in China.” The terms remained unresolved. His answer described a commercial objective to pursue within a political limit; it did not announce a film concession secured in the subsequent agreement.184

Lighthizer had himself told Congress that the American approach should assume there were Chinese reformers with whom it could work. He also insisted on an enforceable process.185 Sympathy for a counterpart’s stated objectives and protection against failure were compatible. The unresolved historical question is how much domestic support particular provisions commanded. The practical obligation is to design an agreement that does not depend entirely on guessing the answer.

The right to complain

The enforcement problem had a history. On March 2, 1995, Congressman Toby Roth pressed Charlene Barshefsky on whether Beijing could enforce the intellectual-property bargain just reached with Washington. He then distinguished ability from will. Barshefsky was confident in Beijing’s ability and cited factory closures as evidence of its will. That will would have to be tested continually. Her proposed response combined pressure and vigilance with technical assistance and an American presence on the ground.186

The agreement already prescribed domestic enforcement machinery. Right holders were to have published contacts, access to progress information and a fifteen-day deadline for a task force to accept a petition or give written reasons for refusing it. That was a deadline for taking up the complaint, not for deciding its merits. Both governments undertook to supply enforcement statistics and consult regularly. Washington rescinded its scheduled tariff action while expressly retaining monitoring and the possibility of further Section 301 action if implementation proved unsatisfactory. Detailed administration, private complaints and the prospect of renewed pressure were part of the earlier bargain.187

Lighthizer’s concern about frightened companies reached the final text in a specific form. An appeal could omit identifying information about a company, and appeals and related material were to remain confidential unless the governments agreed otherwise. A written complaint still had to contain enough information for an assessment. These provisions acknowledged that the government seeking enforcement might need to protect the firm whose experience supplied the evidence.188

That is a concrete difficulty for anyone negotiating commercial access in China. An enterprise seeking permission from Chinese authorities may also be the enterprise expected to document their failure. A foreign government cannot assume that every beneficiary will volunteer a complaint, or that omitting its name will make it unrecognizable. Before treating a new obligation as enforceable, the negotiator needs a feasible way to learn what happened to the firms meant to benefit. Confidentiality can help; it cannot, by itself, remove their exposure.

The agreement gave the complaints a route. Bilateral offices would attempt resolution, then deputies, then the US trade representative and a designated Chinese vice premier. Working-level and deputy-level deadlines ran from receipt of an appeal—twenty-one and forty-five calendar days—not as consecutive allowances. Urgent matters could go directly to the principals. If an urgent meeting could not be scheduled in time, the complainant could proceed under the remedial provision. Refusing a meeting could not indefinitely hold that route closed.189

The route ended in political judgment. After failed consultations over a response to losses, the complaining party could suspend an obligation or adopt a proportionate remedial measure. If the respondent considered the action taken in good faith, it could not counterrespond or otherwise challenge it. If it considered the action taken in bad faith, the specified remedy was withdrawal by written notice. A separate provision allowed termination by notice, normally after sixty days. Neither government had entrusted the good-faith question to an independent judge.190

The 1995 American notice had reserved Washington’s return to pressure under American law. Phase One added a bilateral agreement about the respondent’s next move: no counterresponse to a measure it judged taken in good faith, or withdrawal over one it judged taken in bad faith. The earlier published letters and action plan contained no equivalent provision. This was the particular change the comparison establishes; neither confidentiality nor pressure-backed enforcement was itself new.191

Compared with the WTO procedure examined earlier, Phase One left its bilateral dispute in the hands of the governments imposing and bearing its costs. It could permit action without waiting for an independent ruling. It could also turn a dispute about one obligation into a choice about the survival of the agreement. Those were consequential terms to settle before accepting the underlying duties.

Both settlements formalized the continuation of negotiation after signature. Phase One’s procedure was bilateral, and the United States had publicly pressed for it. Its existence does not establish his stronger claim that China habitually insists on the other’s adherence while seeking indulgence for itself. That requires the record of actual complaints and responses. The confidentiality clause also prevents silence in the public record from establishing that the procedure went unused.

What the numbers promised

Purchases gave the agreement its most conspicuous test. Trump had spoken of $40–50 billion in annual agricultural sales in October. At China’s December briefing, a Reuters correspondent asked about $50 billion; the Chinese answer deferred disclosure of the figures. That question was not a Chinese undertaking.192

The final schedule required covered purchases and imports in 2020–2021 to exceed the corresponding 2017 baselines by at least $200 billion. The number was an increment across the two years. Agriculture’s scheduled increases were $12.5 billion in 2020 and $19.5 billion in 2021. A separate note recorded an undertaking to strive for another $5 billion annually. Projected growth in 2022–2025 did not renew the same quantified annual minima.193

The text also acknowledged market prices and commercial considerations, American facilitation of supply, and consultation over conflicting data and circumstances in the United States affecting Chinese performance. Those qualifications did not expressly erase the numerical requirements. They identified questions that would matter when either government tried to explain a shortfall.

Here the negotiators had promised a result dependent on many transactions. A regulator could issue a permission; it could not make a soybean price, an American shipment and a Chinese buyer’s demand interchangeable. A purchase commitment might justify asking the government to remove an obstacle or arrange additional buying. Judging its performance required knowing which goods and services counted, which benchmark applied, and what the government had undertaken to do when commercial conditions changed. The impressive number left those tasks to the officials who would live with it.

Bargaining for the next day

Was Phase One evidence that tariffs had taught China to take American demands seriously? Cathy Wu’s bargaining account makes learning about resolve a candidate explanation. Angela Huyue Zhang’s contract analysis asks a different question: could the parties observe performance and credibly punish a failure? Both help identify what needs explaining. Neither supplies the private instructions missing from the May breakdown.194195

The public sequence establishes changes in the bargain. China accepted partial tariff relief for a partial settlement. Washington accepted phases and provisions allowing domestic choice over implementation. The parties gave complaints a procedure and preserved room to contest the response. Those findings are more precise than a declaration that pressure either worked or failed. Pressure helped define the choices they faced; the documents do not isolate its contribution from the narrower package, the value of the concessions or changing political calculations.

For the next negotiator, the demanding part begins before signature. Identify which domestic act will make the concession useful, what evidence would show it has occurred, and how the affected firm can report a failure. Price the enforcement mechanism as part of the exchange. A threat costly enough to command attention may also be costly enough that one’s own government hesitates to use it. A faster remedy can sacrifice independent judgment. A partial bargain can deliver something valuable while allowing the hardest dispute to persist.

Lighthizer could seek cooperation from reformers while demanding a way to act if cooperation failed. Liu could defend China’s political system while accepting specific changes within it. Their bargain would reach officials and enterprises with choices of their own. The next chapter follows an opening that became an operating business, approvals that still required years of administrative work, and purchases that fell far short.

6. After the signature

Nearly six years after the signing of Phase One, Josh Gackle faced an awkward choice. The North Dakota farmer, representing the American Soybean Association at a December 2025 hearing, acknowledged China’s failures to fulfill the agreement. But he feared what another American investigation might do to the business his members still hoped to conduct. No single alternative market could replace China, he told the hearing. New purchase promises were already uncertain. He wanted the administration to pursue compliance through the negotiations under way.196

The next witness wanted a stronger response. Geoff Cooper, president of the Renewable Fuels Association, told the officials that American ethanol producers had invested in expectation of Chinese access and then lost it. His association supported the investigation and action to protect producers. Both men spoke for intended beneficiaries of the same agreement. One worried about losing prospective sales; the other described a market already closed to his members.

Their disagreement exposed a difficulty that no enforcement clause could settle in advance. A government must decide whether the expected benefit of enforcing an old promise is worth the risk to the business it can still obtain. Its bargaining position depends partly on whether the people it represents will sustain that choice. China had obligations, but Washington still had decisions to make.

Those decisions required a more discriminating account of performance than either government’s verdict on the agreement as a whole. Within the same political relationship, an American securities firm had obtained full ownership and begun earning substantial revenue; seed companies had secured particular approvals while continuing to contest the review process; aggregate purchases had fallen far short. Each result came through a different route. To understand what the bargain had bought, it is necessary to follow those routes past the signing ceremony.

Getting into business

JPMorgan’s new securities company opened in China on March 20, 2020. With the coronavirus epidemic disrupting ordinary gatherings, it marked the occasion virtually. Three days later, China chief executive Mark Leung explained to a Yicai reporter what he wanted to do with the opening. The firm could bring research and financial services to Chinese clients, but its Chinese operations remained divided among separate legal entities. He wanted regulators to permit a more integrated operation. Entry had made the next commercial ambition more immediate.197

The government negotiation and the firm’s preparations had proceeded alongside each other. In November 2017, after President Trump’s visit, Vice Finance Minister Zhu Guangyao announced that foreigners would be allowed to own 51 percent of securities, fund-management and futures companies, with the ceiling to disappear three years after implementation. Chinese officials placed this opening within the summit’s economic results while also advancing requests about American investment treatment and export restrictions. Investors from other countries would benefit too.198

The securities regulator issued implementing rules in April 2018. JPMorgan then pursued a majority-owned company. The China Securities Regulatory Commission approved its establishment in March 2019, with RMB 800 million in registered capital and five Chinese shareholders alongside the American firm. Approval allowed the organizers to proceed; it did not yet allow the company to conduct securities business. They still had to register the enterprise, supply capital and obtain an operating permit. Registration came in August, the permit in December.199

Inside the company, permission had to be made usable. Its 2019 disclosure describes hiring, training, systems procurement, internal procedures and regulatory inspection. The brokerage department expected formal operations around the middle of 2020, after connections and testing with exchanges, clearing institutions and custody banks. The following year’s report recorded two completed investment-banking projects. An official could alter an ownership limit; the firm still had to construct a business within it.200

Phase One made a particular contribution to this process. In October 2019, the regulator had scheduled the removal of the securities ownership ceiling for December 1, 2020. The agreement moved the deadline to April 1. When the regulator confirmed that date in March, it expressly attributed the change to implementation of Phase One. The bargain had advanced the published timetable by eight months.201

That was a concession worth identifying accurately. JPMorgan possessed its operating permit before the agreement was signed. American negotiators could claim acceleration of the ownership opening without claiming to have created every permission that preceded it. Martin Chorzempa made this distinction in his contemporary assessment: the value added by the bargain had to be separated from liberalization already announced. Nicholas Lardy and Tianlei Huang placed the opening to foreign institutions within a broader, uneven process of financial integration. These accounts leave room for both Chinese reform priorities and foreign pressure; the regulator’s revised date supplies the concrete gain.202

Eligibility for full ownership still left a transaction to complete. In October 2020, one Chinese partner offered its 20 percent holding for sale. Reports described the larger stake JPMorgan could acquire. Yet the company’s year-end shareholder table continued to show its original 51 percent. In August 2021 the regulator accepted the filing for it to acquire the five domestic partners’ holdings. Shareholder registration was completed on August 25. The firm was now wholly foreign owned.203

It was also doing business. Net brokerage fees rose from about RMB 0.93 million in 2020 to RMB 138.44 million in 2021 and RMB 555.95 million in 2022. Investment-banking fees rose sharply in 2021 before declining the next year. These accounts demonstrate commercial activity in the authorized businesses, beyond a license framed for display. They cannot isolate the effect of ownership from the firm’s staffing, capital, clients or market conditions.204

Leung’s ambition for an integrated operation helps explain why successful implementation does not end discussion with regulators. A group admitted to one business may want to combine it with another or offer a new product. JPMorgan’s 2022 disclosure recorded a newly registered Beijing branch whose operating permit was still being processed. A separate 2023 approval concerning its fund-management business imposed continuing requirements, including rules on data and personal information. Such decisions could matter greatly to the firm without constituting a reversal of the securities ownership concession.205

The negotiator’s task is to distinguish a new request from an old benefit withheld. If an agreement removes an ownership ceiling, the need for an additional branch license does not by itself show evasion. If the promised benefit depends on a license that remains unavailable, the ownership concession may be worth much less than expected. Before exchanging concessions, officials need the intended beneficiary’s account of the remaining approvals, responsible authorities and commercial timetable. Otherwise they may obtain a permission the firm cannot use—or fail to recognize the value of one it can.

Permission to sell a seed

For a seed developer, the decisive Chinese permission may govern a crop grown thousands of miles from China. A trait approved for cultivation in the United States can still face a commercial obstacle if China has not authorized imports. Grain moves through shared handling systems; developers and farmers must consider the risk that an unapproved trait will reach a customer who cannot accept it. The permissions examined here covered imported material for processing, not the cultivation of genetically modified crops inside China.206

The companies tried to make that problem an American negotiating priority. In May 2017, lobbyist Hunt Shipman sought a meeting between Dow AgroSciences chief executive Tim Hassinger and the new agriculture secretary, Sonny Perdue. USDA adviser Rebeckah Adcock directed him to the scheduler and later offered to press the request. On May 9, she needed talking points before the following morning. Shipman supplied regulatory summaries for Dow’s corn and soybean products and a paper on Chinese approvals.207

Days later, the two governments announced an undertaking to move eight pending American applications through China’s National Biosafety Committee. The committee would evaluate them by the end of May, explain any further information required and issue certificates for products that passed. It was a promise to undertake specified reviews, not to approve every application. Timely, science-based approvals had already appeared in the 2016 bilateral dialogue; the new arrangement attached work and dates to selected pending cases.208

The announcement changed the immediate argument in Washington. On May 16, another lobbyist, Joe Bischoff, sent Adcock proposed questions for a congressional hearing. They challenged foreign regulatory barriers that prevented American farmers from using new seed technology. Adcock asked whether that was the right formulation after the China announcement. Bischoff replied that Dow still wanted the question sent and that he had already softened it. The question Representative Rodney Davis delivered the following day acknowledged the new arrangement and asked how China would be held accountable. The correspondence does not identify who revised it, but it shows the company pressing a claim while an official defended the progress just announced.209

In June, Hassinger supplied a result that the administration could welcome. China had granted import approval for Enlist corn, he wrote, and Dow could now launch it for American farmers to plant the next year. He credited Perdue’s support through the bilateral arrangement and sought continued help with the remaining traits. Adcock said she had passed the message to the secretary. The exchange follows a corporate request into official preparation and back to a reported benefit. It leaves the Chinese decision itself out of view; Hassinger’s thanks cannot establish what persuaded the approving authority.210

Beijing was also changing its own procedure. In February 2017, before the bilateral announcement, the agriculture ministry had ordered a change in responsibility for specified safety tests. For an initial application for an import safety certificate, foreign developers would no longer have to arrange those tests before the application could be accepted. The ministry would commission them. Testing remained part of the process; arranging it had become the approving authority’s job.211

The ministry’s service guide, issued that May, separated acceptance, testing, preliminary laboratory review, National Biosafety Committee evaluation and the final ministerial decision. Its stated processing period was 270 days, but expert review and inspection/testing did not count toward that clock. A company waiting for permission needed to know more than whether its application was under review. The next action could belong to a laboratory, a committee or the officials routing the approval. The published procedure identifies those stages without locating the particular soybean or canola application within them.212

Securing one approval did not by itself establish a dependable process for the next application. Phase One sought to change the process as well as obtain decisions. Its biotechnology annex required a completeness check within five working days, written safety-related explanations for additional information and sufficient committee meetings. For products that passed safety evaluation, China would issue an approval decision and certificate within twenty working days of the committee meeting’s conclusion. Authorizations would last at least five years. The promised average interval from formal submission to a final decision was no more than twenty-four months.213

An average was a consequential choice. It did not give each company a twenty-four-month deadline, and a final decision could be a refusal. To know whether this obligation was being met, American officials would need dates for a body of applications, including when they entered the process and what decisions followed. Acceptance records, testing dates, requests for further information and committee dispositions would help explain the intervening time. A celebrated approval would establish less than proponents might claim; a single exceptionally long review would not settle the average. Negotiating the measure and obtaining the information to apply it were parts of the same task.

Enlist E3 soybeans show how several permissions could intervene between a diplomatic announcement and a launch. China’s approval became public in January 2019, during renewed trade talks. But the original certificate ran from December 20, 2018. The decision’s legal period had begun before that week’s diplomacy, and contemporary reporting distinguished the public announcement from the company’s receipt of its paperwork.214

The Chinese permission still left another importing country’s decision outstanding. Corteva and MS Technologies announced the American launch in February, after Philippine import approval. China later renewed Enlist E3 for a five-year period ending in December 2026, compared with the original three-year authorization. The longer term was a usable benefit consistent with Phase One’s requirement. It did not answer how quickly new applications were being processed.215

Optimum GLY canola required a much longer wait. According to contemporary reporting, the application for event DP73496 had been filed in China in July 2012. The certificate eventually issued was valid from January 5, 2023. Roughly ten and a half years lay between the reported filing and permission.216

That interval is striking, but assigning its whole commercial cost to China would misread the product’s other difficulties. The European Union had received an application in May 2012 and did not authorize the product until March 2022. A favorable food-safety opinion came only in June 2021; both the standing committee and appeal committee subsequently failed to deliver an opinion. The Commission’s eventual authorization excluded cultivation. China’s permission came about nine months after the EU’s. In neither jurisdiction does the filing date alone reveal every request for information or establish when a complete application was ready for decision.217

By late January 2023, a named Corteva representative described China as the final outstanding global approval. Stewardship and communications work remained. The following month, the company announced a commercial launch in the United States and Canada for 2023, with a later full launch in Australia.
