At this point the Chinese permission can be connected to a particular business decision: the company could proceed with the launch it announced. Actual acreage and sales would be further measures of its value.218

Meanwhile, the process remained disputed. At the December 2025 hearing, the seed industry’s representative reported an average review period of sixty-nine months and two approvals that had taken at least nine years. The 2026 US National Trade Estimate likewise alleged failure to meet the agreed average and criticized the absence of published results from committee meetings. Neither supplied the complete application histories needed here to reproduce the calculation. Their complaints remain serious: securing selected products had not satisfied those who wanted a dependable route for the next product.219

The contrast with finance is instructive. A published date for removing an ownership ceiling could be checked against a regulator’s announcement. A promise about average review time required continuing information from applicants and authorities. The second obligation was harder to monitor even though the text looked precise. A negotiator who asks only whether China has accepted a number may overlook the harder question of how anyone will know whether it has been met.

Buying a number

The purchase commitments posed a different problem again. China undertook to buy specified increments of American goods and services over two years. Government decisions could remove barriers, direct some purchasing or alter its incentives. They could not make prices, available supply and commercial demand stand still. The agreement acknowledged commercial considerations and market conditions while retaining numerical commitments. Those qualifications mattered to performance; they did not dissolve the undertaking.220

By the end of the period, purchases were far short. Chad Bown’s Peterson Institute calculations put covered goods at 60 percent of the commitment using American export data and 62 percent using Chinese import data. Adding covered services on the American basis produced $290.8 billion against a $502.4 billion benchmark, or 58 percent. His conclusion that China had bought none of the additional $200 billion concerned the increment above the baseline. China had continued buying large amounts; it had failed to deliver the promised increase.221

Several forces intervened. The pandemic damaged services trade, aircraft supply faced problems and the trade war’s restrictions persisted. Bown’s analysis also had to work around unpublished allocations and incomplete coverage of some services. The arithmetic established a major shortfall without assigning it to a single cause. When USTR initiated a new investigation in October 2025, it used a different benchmark—more than $535 billion—and a shortfall exceeding $217 billion. Its notice did not reconcile that figure with Bown’s. The precise estimates should remain separate; neither yields a plausible claim of full delivery.222223

Both governments had to decide what to do with that failure. China’s commerce ministry stressed the pandemic and disrupted supply chains while describing continued communication. Katherine Tai, appearing before the Senate Finance Committee in March 2022, said China had failed to meet its purchases and placed the administration’s response within a wider policy involving domestic investment and allies. The parties continued talking, but the public accounts do not establish that they agreed to extend the expired 2020–2021 numerical target. The agreement’s expectation of a continuing trajectory through 2025 was a different provision.224

China’s April 2025 white paper defended its implementation and invoked unforeseen events. Phase One supplied a route for that argument: Article 7.6.2 required consultation when an unforeseeable event outside a party’s control delayed timely compliance. It did not automatically release either government from its obligation. The white paper also said neither side had formally activated the agreement’s bilateral dispute procedure, despite repeated review contacts. These were China’s representations of the process, not jointly published minutes.225

The argument had consequently shifted from what China had promised to what each government was prepared to demand, concede or risk next. This is one reason Solomon’s attention to the continuing relationship remains valuable. But treating the dispute simply as a difference in national attitudes toward promises would discard the things that made it negotiable: an expired purchase period, disputed responsibility, a consultation clause and several possible enforcement routes.

The cost of enforcing an agreement

The investigation that brought Gackle and Cooper to the December hearing began on October 24, 2025. USTR asked whether China had denied Americans the benefits of Phase One, what commercial burdens resulted and what remedies would be appropriate. It invited evidence and proposals for duties, service restrictions or import restrictions. It also requested consultations under the American Trade Act. This was a domestic Section 301 investigation, not a documented appeal under Chapter 7 of Phase One. Washington was choosing an instrument as well as deciding how much pressure to apply.226

The agricultural witnesses exposed the constituency problem behind that choice. Gackle’s farmers still depended on sales to China. Cooper described ethanol exports that had ceased and investments made on expectations of access. The practical difference was the business each industry believed remained at risk. Their testimony does not prove that those interests determined American policy. It shows why an official could not infer support for a remedy from agreement that China had fallen short.227

The timing sharpened the choice. A new presidential accommodation in autumn 2025 had produced another American announcement of Chinese soybean purchases. Gackle questioned its certainty and timetable even as he feared disturbing the negotiations. The White House also described a suspension of the separate maritime and shipbuilding Section 301 action. That suspension did not establish that the Phase One investigation had been suspended too. In February 2026, China’s commerce ministry said the old agreement had been discussed during the five rounds of talks in 2025, defended its implementation and warned against further measures.228

An enforcement threat thus entered a relationship already carrying prospective benefits and other disputes. A government may gain negotiating room by keeping several matters open together. It may also find that obtaining the next concession becomes an argument for postponing a remedy on the last one. The records examined here establish that tension, an investigation and a contested response; they do not supply a final settlement of the enforcement dispute.229

The implication for an agreement’s design is practical. Before relying on a remedy, negotiators need to know which constituencies would bear its immediate costs, which would expect to benefit and what alternatives the government would retain if support weakened. A commitment to consult can keep a problem alive. A right to retaliate can improve the price of settlement. Neither compels a future administration to incur costs it has come to judge excessive.

Permissions in exchange for permissions

A separate dispute in the spring and summer of 2025 put a different kind of performance at issue. The immediate bargain concerned permission for future transactions; it did not settle the unfulfilled Phase One purchases. On July 2, Synopsys announced that it had received a letter from the US Commerce Department rescinding restrictions imposed on its China business in May. The chip-design software company was working to restore customers’ access. The following day, Siemens reported that its own May restrictions had been lifted and that it had restored access, sales and support for the specified software and technology. These short company statements recorded part of what China had obtained through another negotiation with Washington.230

China had something the Americans wanted in return. On April 4, its commerce ministry and customs administration had placed specified rare-earth items under export controls. The State Council had approved the measure. It took effect immediately. Exporters needed licenses, and customs could withhold shipments while questioning whether goods had been classified correctly. The notice covered items associated with seven elements, including some magnet materials; it neither prohibited every rare-earth export nor confined its controls to American destinations.231

The industrial position behind that authority was formidable. The International Energy Agency estimates that China accounted for about 91 percent of global separation and refining of the four magnet rare earths it measures in 2024. A foreign manufacturer could have willing Chinese suppliers and still need the government’s permission before they could ship. Restrictions at this stage could affect production far beyond the value of the materials themselves.232

The Geneva meeting in May had appeared to offer relief. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer met Vice Premier He Lifeng, and the governments agreed to suspend much of the tariff escalation. The joint statement required action by May 14, including China’s suspension or removal of non-tariff countermeasures taken against the United States since April 2. It did not name the April 4 notice, publish a list of licenses or specify how quickly individual applications would move.233

That omission left a commercially important distinction unsettled in public. Repealing a control and allowing transactions under it could both relieve a manufacturer, but they left different authority in Chinese hands. Reuters subsequently reported, on the account of one person briefed on Geneva, that China had privately accepted the rare-earth controls as covered countermeasures. The released statement did not disclose how that understanding was to be implemented. The same reporting described burdensome applications and efforts to add processing staff. A delayed license could reflect administrative work as well as pressure; the reporting could not establish the reason for each delay.234

The governments soon accused each other of undermining the accommodation. On June 2, MOFCOM defended China’s implementation and protested new American restrictions, including those on chip-design software. Bessent later explained the American action differently: critical materials had not moved as agreed, so Washington had imposed countermeasures. Each side described its own restrictions as a response to the other’s conduct. Each now had a specific administrative act it wanted reversed or accelerated.235

Xi and Trump spoke on June 5. The Chinese account said they agreed to another meeting of their teams; it supplied no licensing timetable. Talks in London on June 9–10 produced agreement in principle on an implementation framework. He Lifeng faced Bessent and Greer, now joined by Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick. Further communication followed. On June 27, MOFCOM said that, after approval, the parties had confirmed the details. China would approve eligible applications under its law; the United States would correspondingly remove a series of restrictions. The authority to control exports remained, while its administration supplied the terms of relief.236

The distinction could help the parties make a limited deal. Washington could seek supplies needed by manufacturers without first obtaining the dismantling of China’s control system. Beijing could seek the removal of particular American restrictions without first securing an end to US technology controls. This left further disputes outside the arrangement. Reuters reported, citing two people briefed on London, that China had not committed to clear some specialized materials needed by military suppliers. Its report also described an apparent attempt to connect that access with advanced AI-chip restrictions. Bessent publicly rejected such an exchange. An attempted linkage was not a concession obtained.237

The software companies’ July statements show one side of the accommodation taking effect. China’s commerce ministry likewise reported that it was processing eligible applications and that Washington had notified it of removals. Yet relief varied among customers. In late June, Volkswagen told Reuters its supply was stable, while Dexter Magnetic Technologies’ chief executive reported only five approvals from 180 applications, all for non-defence uses. Europe’s suppliers were pursuing their own relief: MOFCOM said in July that it had established an expedited channel for European enterprises. The Chinese licensing system served several diplomatic relationships at once.238

Shipments subsequently recovered. Reuters’ report of Chinese customs data put July rare-earth magnet exports at 5,577 metric tons, including 619 to the United States, slightly above the American destination’s July 2024 volume. Physical goods were moving again. Those totals could not establish that every controlled item or end user had received clearance, any more than the software releases could establish that all American technology restrictions had ended.239

The episode does not reveal a Chinese discovery that exports could be restricted for advantage. Earlier duties, quotas and trading restrictions on rare earths had already generated a WTO dispute; in May 2015 China reported that it had removed the condemned measures, while Washington disputed full compliance. What the 2025 sequence adds is a close view of how contemporary industrial dependence and administrative authority became negotiable together. The authority could survive the accommodation that restored trade.240

Research by Viking Bohman, Audrye Wong and Victor Ferguson identifies a related difficulty: a government can explain the offense that prompted a restriction without specifying what would secure its removal. Formal rules can coexist with discretion over relief. In the London sequence, the negotiators did identify reciprocal administrative actions, and some of those actions reached firms. Obtaining that path mattered even though the controls and wider political disagreements survived.241

For an official negotiating relief, that creates a choice with costs on either side. Insisting on repeal may seek a more durable opening but postpone the shipments firms urgently need. Accepting licenses may restore production while leaving the next application exposed to review. The value of the latter bargain depends on the products, end users, duration and practical usability of the permissions obtained—and on the reciprocal action promised in return. A general assurance that trade will resume leaves too much of that choice to the next round.

Negotiating the next mechanism

By the summer of 2026, both governments were considering how to organize further exchanges. The White House had announced Boards of Trade and Investment and new prospective agricultural commitments after the May summit. Chinese statements described preliminary results and further work.242 USTR’s June consultation contemplated equal-value trade in nonsensitive products, with tariff changes subject to agreement and law.243 In July, Chinese officials described discussions of reciprocal tariff reductions covering $30 billion on each side. Products and implementation remained to be agreed; the public accounts did not provide an agreed agricultural purchase schedule.244245

The new design would have to confront both problems this chapter has followed. A promise can prove difficult to enforce even when the shortfall is clear. A permission can deliver immediate relief while leaving future transactions exposed. Before exchanging another concession, officials can specify the act that would count as performance, seek information that would reveal its absence, and identify a response their own government could bear to use. None of those steps guarantees delivery. Each reduces the room for an attractive announcement to conceal a different bargain from the one its intended users need.

7. Rival, partner, supplier

India: deciding what progress was worth

After three hours with Wang Yi in New Delhi on March 25, 2022, Subrahmanyam Jaishankar faced a question that exposed the difficulty of his position. The Indian foreign minister had discussed trade, travel, students and international affairs with his Chinese counterpart. Did an agenda that broad itself amount to a return to normal relations?

Jaishankar answered no. He welcomed progress at several confrontation points along the disputed border, notably Pangong Tso. But large deployments remained, and their presence, he argued, violated earlier agreements. A ministerial visit could help resolve that problem. It could not make the problem cease to matter. India wanted stable, predictable relations; restoring them required peace at the border.246

Wang had offered a different way forward. According to China’s account, the two large developing countries should preserve the wider significance of their relationship and prevent the boundary question from defining it. Yet that same account recorded Jaishankar’s call to complete disengagement in the remaining areas. It also described agreement to restore border peace and move toward regular management on the basis of disengagement. The ministers could agree that military separation mattered while disagreeing about how much of the relationship should wait for it.247

This was a negotiation over the value of the relationship, with India pressing the condition. Beijing’s appeal to common interests encountered a counterpart unwilling to grant the political meaning Beijing sought for partial progress. The question was practical: what could each government do next without conceding that the other’s remaining demands had become unnecessary?

Keeping contact without conceding normality

Jaishankar did not answer that question by refusing other business. He raised the predicament of Indian students unable to return to China under pandemic restrictions and pressed for fairer market access. Wang, he reported, undertook to speak to the relevant authorities about the students. That was a promise to pursue the matter, rather than an immediate decision restoring their access. The relationship contained requests worth making even while India disputed its overall condition.

Nor did Jaishankar publicly offer a border concession in return for a summit. Asked whether a timetable had been set so that Narendra Modi could attend the BRICS summit in China, he acknowledged discussion of the invitation but described the border talks as a parallel, separate subject. Wang’s presence had provided an opportunity to press for implementation. Jaishankar stopped short of presenting either that presence or a future leaders’ meeting as the consideration for a bargain.248

He had reason to distinguish a new understanding from carrying out an old one. The ministers had met in Moscow in September 2020 and again in Dushanbe the following year. Fifteen rounds of senior military talks had followed the confrontation that began in 2020. Some friction points had been addressed, but implementation was slower than India wanted. Jaishankar’s immediate objective was to complete disengagement so that discussion of de-escalation could follow. Moving forces apart at a particular confrontation point and reducing the larger military concentration were different decisions.

The dispute was not over whether principles should precede negotiation. Earlier principles already supplied arguments for both sides. The 2005 agreement stated that boundary differences should not obstruct the broader relationship. It also required respect for the Line of Actual Control and maintenance of peace pending a final settlement. Wang could invoke the value of cooperation; Jaishankar could insist that the agreed security foundation had been disturbed. The text contained both propositions. Appealing to one did not dispose of the other.249

Turning those propositions into a local arrangement required officials with different responsibilities. The diplomatic Working Mechanism for Consultation and Coordination, established in 2012, included military representatives and could address border incidents. Its founding agreement excluded settlement of the boundary question, which remained the concern of the Special Representatives. Commanders, meanwhile, had to negotiate arrangements that forces could carry out on the ground. A conversation about managing an immediate danger did not authorize a territorial settlement.250

Saheb Singh Chadha’s interviews with Indian officials make the limits of delegated authority more concrete. His reconstruction describes political meetings setting a framework, diplomatic meetings elaborating steps and commanders working on their application. Before corps-commanders’ meetings, India’s China Study Group set the limits within which its commander could negotiate. The military interlocutor was therefore not arriving with unlimited discretion to exchange concessions. Chadha did not interview Chinese officials, so the corresponding Chinese instructions remain unknown. But the Indian evidence alone makes it unsafe to read every stalled military round as evidence of a dispute inside Beijing.251

What the local bargain required

The sixteenth military round, held on July 17, 2022, did not publicly announce closure. Its joint release promised continued work on remaining issues and interim stability. It also linked resolving those issues to progress in bilateral relations. That formulation gave both governments room to continue: India had not accepted that the border no longer mattered, and neither side had committed publicly to a complete settlement at that meeting.252

The next arrangement concerned one confrontation point in eastern Ladakh, in the western sector of the disputed frontier. On September 8, troops began disengaging in the area India called Gogra–Hot Springs, or Patrol Point 15. China’s publication used the name Jianan Daban. The joint announcement was brief. India’s explanation the following day showed that the undertaking it described was considerably more specific.

The process had begun at 08:30 and was to finish by September 12. Both sides would cease forward deployments in a phased, coordinated and verified manner, with troops returning to their respective areas. Temporary structures and associated infrastructure erected by both sides would be dismantled and checked through mutual verification. The terrain would be restored to its condition before the standoff. India also said the agreement required respect for the line and prohibited unilateral changes to the status quo.253

These terms made the next decisions concrete. A commander needed to coordinate a withdrawal with the other force, remove structures and verify the reciprocal action. A diplomat needed to preserve the distinction between completing that task and accepting that all remaining issues were resolved. The September explanation expressly kept further talks on the agenda. India’s October diplomatic account then reported that the September 8–12 process had been completed and connected it to the foreign ministers’ understandings, including their July meeting in Bali.

The published explanation is the Indian government’s account of the bargain; its maps and verification records are not available here. It cannot establish every change in patrol access or independently verify every movement. What it does establish is the specificity of the duties India publicly described. Inferring a vague operative agreement from the brevity of the joint announcement would miss the timetable, reciprocal tasks and checks that officials said lay behind it.

Completion at one location brought a further decision into view: how much wider cooperation should it unlock? On September 27, Chinese ambassador Sun Weidong described a relationship recovering momentum. He pointed to disengagement, resumed processing of some visa categories and business travel. He advocated restored direct flights and broader exchanges. In his account, the emergency response following Galwan had largely ended and the border was moving toward routine management. Outstanding differences should be handled without allowing them to govern the whole relationship.254

India resisted that conclusion. Asked about the ambassador’s remarks on October 7, spokesperson Arindam Bagchi acknowledged positive steps but maintained that more were needed. PP15 was another step. India still sought disengagement, de-escalation and restored border normality before normality in the wider relationship. Sun’s account of progress and Bagchi’s refusal to call it sufficient were not necessarily competing claims about whether the PP15 process had occurred. They assigned different political consequences to it.

This distinction matters to a negotiator deciding whether to accept a partial agreement. Local separation can reduce danger even when the parties cannot agree what it should purchase elsewhere. India could accept the reciprocal arrangement while withholding the broader response China advocated. China could participate in the arrangement and continue arguing that relations should improve. Continued bargaining over that response does not, by itself, show that either side had repudiated the local terms.

A different threshold for moving forward

Two years later, the negotiators reached an additional arrangement. After further diplomatic and military work, India announced an agreement on October 21, 2024, concerning Depsang and Demchok. When Modi and Xi Jinping met in Kazan two days later, they welcomed the progress and directed further work. Their meeting endorsed an arrangement already reached. Its anticipated occurrence may have encouraged agreement; the sequence cannot assign the terms to the encounter that publicly celebrated them.255256

In December, Jaishankar explained to Parliament what India understood it had obtained. The problem in the two areas had concerned obstruction of patrolling; Demchok also involved civilian grazing and access to places of local significance. He reported verification patrols followed by regular activity under the understanding. Patrolling and grazing access had been agreed, while temporary restraints at other locations remained dependent on local conditions and open to review. He called disengagement complete and identified de-escalation as the next task.257

His account also changed the practical question facing the relationship. India could now consider other aspects of engagement in a calibrated fashion while continuing to address military concentration. The choice was no longer whether PP15 alone justified a return to normality. It was how much cooperation to resume after the additional arrangements, with another category of security work unfinished.

By August 2025, the two governments publicly combined those courses. Their accounts of the Special Representatives’ talks called for discussion of de-escalation, beginning with its principles and modalities. They also commissioned work on border management and possible early results in delimitation, and extended military mechanisms to additional sectors. India’s account listed intended steps on flights, visas, border trade and other exchanges. Those commitments did not demonstrate that all measures had been delivered. They did show that renewed cooperation need not await completion of every military adjustment.258

In May 2026, diplomatic teams were still discussing delimitation, border management and cooperation while preparing another Special Representatives’ meeting. India described progress toward gradual normalization. Neither account announced a completed boundary settlement or supplied a census of forces withdrawn.259

The Indian sequence consequently offers more than a counterexample to a national style. It shows the counterpart helping determine what a familiar practice can accomplish. Appeals to the larger relationship did not automatically override India’s security demands; neither did those demands prevent every other exchange. Senior access mattered because officials used it to press for work through diplomatic and military channels. Local agreements mattered because they specified actions while leaving other questions open. The negotiator’s task was to decide which unfinished question still justified withholding the next benefit—and to recognize that the other government might value the same step differently.

Russia: whose island, whose hay?

On the Argun River, accepting a boundary meant deciding what would happen to the grass. Russian residents had used Menkeseli, a large island and sandbar area near Sredneargunsk, for cattle pasture and hay. The 1991 border agreement placed it on the Chinese side of the settlement. For the people accustomed to using it, the diplomatic achievement threatened an established livelihood. A line that satisfied two foreign ministries could still provoke resistance from residents and soldiers expected to live with it.260

Russia’s regional authorities did not all answer that problem alike. Primorye governor Yevgenii Nazdratenko campaigned against territorial transfers. In Chita, the local Border Guard consulted the regional administration and proposed preserving residents’ economic use while recognizing the transfer to China. Akihiro Iwashita reconstructs this initiative from regional reporting: Chinese negotiators accepted the idea early, before its eventual incorporation into a bilateral framework. In July 1995, Russian demarcation chief Genrikh Kireev publicly praised Chita’s cooperation with the commission and the local military. An official seeking to implement the settlement had found local allies as well as local opponents.261

The proposed accommodation separated two questions that the territorial argument appeared to make inseparable. China could exercise sovereignty over the place. Established Russian users could retain permission to carry out limited activities there. Neither side had to pretend that the border had disappeared. The arrangement made the consequences of accepting it less absolute.

This was possible because much of the legal argument had already been settled. The eastern agreement, signed on May 16, 1991, left two sections for later negotiation while committing the parties elsewhere to a line and an equal joint commission charged with putting it on the ground. Surveyors still had to locate river channels, assign islands, erect markers and prepare maps. The western agreement followed in 1994. By 1997 the governments could announce completion of demarcation in the agreed eastern sectors without settling the reserved islands near Khabarovsk and Abagaitui.262

Such progress did not require China to forget its history. Deng Xiaoping’s published account of his May 1989 conversation with Mikhail Gorbachev coupled a forceful description of past injury with acceptance of existing treaties as the basis for a boundary settlement. Historical condemnation and a workable legal starting point could coexist. Nor did the subsequent division of the negotiation into stages establish why Beijing accepted it. Fravel emphasizes the increased value of Soviet cooperation after Tiananmen; Iwashita traces substantive convergence to talks reopened in 1987. The practical fact was that agreement in one area could proceed while another remained unresolved.263264

The Menkeseli proposal addressed a different obstacle: making an agreed allocation tolerable. A November 1997 framework led to the detailed agreement signed by foreign ministers Tang Jiaxuan and Igor Ivanov on December 9, 1999. Its opening definition placed sovereignty first: the state possessing the territory would allow the other country’s established border residents to pursue traditional economic activities. China designated four areas in the Menkeseli island group and an island in the Amur for Russian use. Russia made Verkhnekonstantinovsky Island and adjoining waters available to Chinese users. The accommodation was reciprocal, though that does not establish equal economic value.265

The permissions were deliberately narrow. Only established users qualified; their total numbers could not rise. They could cut hay, fish and cultivate crops. They could not turn a seasonal activity into unrestricted access, erect new buildings or reconstruct existing facilities. Host-country law applied, and permitted activity could not limit the sovereign state’s own right to develop and use the area. The benefit offered to residents therefore came with an unmistakable change in their position: continued access would depend on the authority of the country now recognized as sovereign.

The permit rules make that change tangible. A Russian user needed a crossing pass from Russia’s border representative and an activity permit issued through the designated Chinese regional authorities—the governments of Inner Mongolia and Heilongjiang. In the opposite direction, China’s Ministry of Public Security issued crossing passes and the Amur regional administration issued activity permits. Crossing the frontier and working beyond it were different permissions, assigned to different offices. Each permit specified the person, place, activity and working time.266

The governments also committed themselves to a timetable. Their authorities were to request permits within sixty days of the rules taking effect; the receiving authority was to issue them within fifteen days of a request. Yet the next provision explicitly preserved the right to refuse any individual applicant. A deadline could discipline processing without eliminating sovereign discretion. Work would normally take place in daylight. An overnight stay required advance agreement, except in an emergency or natural disaster. At the end of the work, equipment and produce were to return to the sending country. These were arrangements for access and removal, not an invitation to permanent settlement.

Duration supplied another bargaining margin. Iwashita reports that an initially contemplated ten years became five. The signed text confirms five years from the beginning of use, with the dates to be agreed diplomatically. It also required consultations at least twice annually to resolve practical difficulties. In October 2004, the governments returned to the question: a new protocol identified March 1, 2005 as the existing end date and extended use for another five years from that date. Temporary accommodation had become renewable through another express agreement.267

The surviving instruments do not tell us how many people obtained permits or what happened to every application. They establish the promised terms of access and renewal, with the territorial settlement intact.

There were limits to exporting the solution. The reserved islands still required agreement on sovereignty itself. Nor could protection of selected livelihoods answer every objection to transferring territory. Near Khabarovsk, the disputed islands lay beside a major Russian city; agricultural land, airport approaches, defense installations and the political meaning of surrendering control all entered the argument. What could be eased through a permit was only part of what officials had to defend.

The final settlement also shows what a usable mandate looked like. On October 13, 2004, Putin accepted a Foreign Ministry proposal coordinated with federal agencies and the executives of Khabarovsk, Chita and the Jewish Autonomous Region. His order authorized the ministry to negotiate and sign three attached instruments, permitting changes that did not alter their principles. The next day, Li Zhaoxing and Sergei Lavrov signed the supplementary boundary agreement. The Russian minister had a defined mandate over specified drafts; the public record does not disclose the equivalent Chinese approval traffic.268269

Regional coordination did not mean regional unanimity. On October 22, Kommersant reported that a Khabarovsk legislative committee wanted federal parliamentarians to resist ratification. Governor Viktor Ishaev, who had accompanied the Russian delegation to Beijing, defended the result by saying that the airport approach, fortifications, vegetable-growing land and dachas remained on Russia’s side. He also welcomed the legislators’ expression of concern. His position illustrates the political work between agreement at the center and acceptance at home: defending retained interests while acknowledging that a loss had to be explained.270

When Lavrov presented the settlement to the State Duma in May 2005, he again emphasized the inhabited and economically used areas retained by Russia. Military and border officials addressed different concerns about defense and unauthorized crossings. The Duma approved the agreement by 307 votes to 80. Ratification and entry into force followed; the supplementary demarcation protocol came in July 2008, and the October exchange of notes and handover brought the later instruments into effect. The line agreed in 2004 still required legal approval and fieldwork before transfer.271

Both governments had conceded, against different starting points. China accepted less than the whole area it claimed. Russia relinquished control over part of the territory it held. Measuring only the land that changed hands would conceal China’s retreat from its claim; measuring only that retreat would conceal the practical Russian loss. The local-use arrangement, meanwhile, offered a means of softening some losses without reversing sovereignty.

For someone negotiating with Beijing, the demanding question is whether an apparently indivisible demand contains interests that can be treated differently. Here sovereignty, working access and the duration of access could be specified separately. Doing so required the assent of governments and the participation of offices capable of issuing documents, inspecting users and managing complaints. It also left a real risk with the residents: their benefit was temporary and subject to the host’s powers. That tradeoff is more useful than a general injunction to be flexible. An accommodation can help implement a settlement precisely because it preserves some interests while requiring others to yield.

The price friendship could not settle

At the end of his visit to Shanghai on May 21, 2014, Vladimir Putin faced a question the signing ceremony had left unanswered. What had held up the gas agreement with China, and what price had finally been accepted?

He began with the larger relationship. The visit had produced dozens of agreements, and the gas contract was historic. The experts had worked until half past three in the morning and resumed in the morning. Chinese negotiators had bargained hard, he said; both parties had compromised. The deal was done, although government decisions were still required.272

The questioner returned to the price. Putin described a formula but initially misstated its reference commodities. After answering questions about Ukraine, he noticed a prompt from his spokesman, Dmitry Peskov, and corrected himself: the formula was tied to a basket of oil and petroleum products. Asked how it compared with the European price, he again explained the mechanism. He did not disclose the coefficients that would determine the bill.

The exchange captures both the achievement and the unfinished work. Political leaders could make an agreement worth concluding, companies could agree a way of pricing deliveries, and each government could still have decisions to take. To assess what China had gained, a foreign negotiator needed to understand all three.

The agreement had a substantial history before Russia’s confrontation with the West over Ukraine. In September 2013, with Putin and Xi present, Gazprom and China National Petroleum Corporation had agreed principal terms for the eastern route. Contemporary reporting described a binding document that left price unresolved. Gazprom hoped to complete the sales contract that year; the target passed.273 The leaders had endorsed an important intermediate agreement. It had not resolved the term on which the project depended.

The Chinese company had a problem of its own. A January 2014 corporate question-and-answer exchange hosted on CNPC’s website explained why the project remained attractive and why profitability could not be read from the border price alone. Domestic selling prices, pipeline operating costs and taxes also mattered. Another answer described efforts to obtain more favorable price and tax policies from the Chinese authorities.274

This was a constraint a counterpart could investigate. CNPC’s listed subsidiary, PetroChina, reported a net loss of about 41.9 billion yuan on imported gas sales in 2013, despite an operating profit in its broader gas-and-pipeline segment.275 The previous June, China’s price regulator had explained that low domestic prices discouraged imports. Its reform raised non-residential prices while protecting household prices and phasing the adjustment for existing consumption.276 Buying more gas served a national purpose; recovering its cost required decisions about what Chinese customers would pay. A concession from the Russian seller was one possible source of relief. Chinese price and tax policy was another.

Contemporary Chinese reporting described the final bargaining in these terms. China Economic Weekly quoted a person close to the Chinese negotiators who said that Russia faced considerable pressure to finish during the visit, but China faced pressure too. The source claimed China had defended an affordability limit while accepting a price short of its preferred result. The same account described compromise on both sides.277 An unnamed source cannot establish the private reservation price. It does, however, complicate the public image of one side simply waiting for the other to capitulate: the claimed limit and the preferred outcome were different things.

The bargain announced on May 21 assigned costly work to each party. Gazprom would develop Russian fields, processing facilities and transmission; CNPC would build the Chinese pipeline and associated storage. The thirty-year eastern contract envisaged deliveries rising to 38 billion cubic metres annually. The contemporary Chinese account expected supply from 2018, a date the later operational record would overtake.278 The route opened a market for eastern Siberian resources. It did not immediately give Russia a way to divert gas already moving westward to Europe.279

Gazprom’s $400 billion valuation gave the agreement a memorable size. It supplied no public basis for calculating the price concession China had extracted. Dividing that figure by thirty years of maximum deliveries would ignore the gradual increase in supply and the oil-linked formula. Russia’s worsening relations with the West made a Chinese agreement more valuable, but that context alone cannot reveal how much it changed the accepted price.280

Financing offered a further possible exchange. Two days after signature, Gazprom Export chief Alexander Medvedev said a $25 billion prepayment had been agreed in principle, with details under discussion. In November, Alexei Miller said an advance had been considered in connection with reducing the gas price but was no longer under consideration after price settlement. Chinese bank loans remained a possibility.281 The available record shows a financing option disappearing; it does not establish breach of a completed Chinese payment obligation. For the negotiator, Miller’s explanation identifies the trade that would need to be priced: money earlier in return for cheaper deliveries later.

The eastern contract became an operating relationship. In December 2015 the companies were agreeing arrangements for the border crossing, communications and technical work. Supply began in December 2019. Gazprom subsequently reported 38.8 billion cubic metres delivered in 2025, and specially agreed quantities to maintain supply during scheduled maintenance in April 2026.282 These were corporate reports, not an independent audit of the contract. Yet they demonstrate what the relationship had made possible: gas delivered through infrastructure built for that purpose, with further coordination needed to keep it moving.

The cost of another route

The result encouraged expectations about a second bargain. The original western proposal would bring western Siberian gas toward Xinjiang through the Altai region. A framework followed in November 2014 and heads of agreement in May 2015. Keun-Wook Paik’s contemporary study treated stronger political support as reason to expect progress. But the same December 2015 corporate release that recorded implementation work on the eastern route scheduled further commercial negotiations on the western one.283 Friendship could sustain both conversations while only one supplied a completed sales contract.

The routes presented different commercial choices. As Vitaly Yermakov and Michal Meidan explain, gas entering western China would arrive in a region already served by Central Asian pipelines, far from major eastern demand centers. Reaching those customers required more transport. A later proposal through Mongolia offered access to northern China, but required another major pipeline and a Chinese purchase agreement. Engineering work and permissions in Mongolia could prepare the route without securing the customer.284

China nevertheless accepted another Russian transaction in February 2022: a long-term sales agreement for the Far Eastern route, initially for an additional ten billion cubic metres annually. That project drew on a different supply geography. Gazprom announced it alongside a completed feasibility study for the Mongolia project; the latter remained a study.285 A buyer’s refusal to close one transaction need not mean rejection of the supplier or the strategic relationship. The location of available resources, the infrastructure required and the competing sources of supply could make another transaction preferable.

In March 2023, Putin said almost all parameters of the proposed Power of Siberia 2 agreement had been settled. The joint statement used a more limited formulation: advance research and consultation on the trilateral pipeline. Neither disclosed agreed commercial terms.286 In 2024, the Financial Times reported Chinese demands concerning price and the amount of capacity Beijing would commit to buying. The accessible Reuters summary attributes those claims to the newspaper’s sources; it supplies no independently obtained term sheet.287 The reported disagreement concerned who would bear price and demand risk over the life of a dedicated pipeline.

Miller announced another advance in September 2025: a legally binding memorandum on construction of Power of Siberia 2 and its Mongolian section, Soyuz Vostok, with prospective capacity of fifty billion cubic metres a year. He separately announced prospective increases on the eastern and Far Eastern routes.288 The full memorandum remains unavailable. Its announcement cannot tell a prospective supplier how much gas the customer must purchase, on what schedule or with what remedy if it does not.

The subsequent Russian explanation was explicit about work still to be done. On September 3, 2026, Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said commercial terms remained under discussion and the final routing depended on agreement between Gazprom and CNPC. The following day, he described continued preparation of the contract and government help toward concrete company agreement. Russian reports were now using the name Power of Baikal.289 The public political commitment had still not produced the completed commercial package at that point.

Chinese planning documents also distinguished stages. The March 2026 national plan called for construction of the Far Eastern pipeline and preparatory work on the middle-route project. The July oil-and-gas plan placed imports alongside domestic production, diversification and flexible operation.290 These are stated supply priorities, not a disclosed reservation price. They help identify the alternatives a seller must examine before deciding that its own urgency should be shared by the buyer.

For a negotiator, the practical question is what must change to make waiting less attractive than agreement. A lower price might help, but so might a different route, less exposure to unwanted volumes, financing on better terms or a domestic decision allowing costs to be recovered. The record does not establish that each delay was calculated to extract a concession. It establishes why a valuable political relationship could support years of work without determining which allocation of commercial risks either side would accept.

The eastern pipeline belongs in that judgment as much as the unfinished proposal. Russia and China could carry out a large agreement and remain unable to close the next one. Knowing the strength of their relationship did not dispense with knowing the business.

What could be separated

Each negotiation required a decision about which interests could be treated separately. India could agree to disengagement at one confrontation point without accepting that normal relations should resume. Russia and China could settle sovereignty while preserving specified uses for residents of the other country. They could also complete one gas contract without accepting the commercial terms of another. A relationship helped define what was worth pursuing; it did not make those choices interchangeable.

The distinction mattered because an apparent concession could move the cost elsewhere. Temporary permission could protect a livelihood while leaving the user exposed to expiry and individual refusal. An advance payment could ease the supplier’s immediate financing problem in exchange for a lower future price. Accepting border progress as sufficient for wider cooperation could surrender a condition that the other side still wanted removed. Before proposing that interests be separated, a negotiator needed to decide whether the resulting accommodation would remain valuable on its own.

The practical test is in the terms. Specify the area from which troops will withdraw, the authority that will issue a permit, the date on which use ends, or the volumes a buyer must pay for. Seek approval for that defined obligation and make reciprocal commitments conditional on it where the bargain requires. Such requests cannot expose every private instruction. They can reveal whether the parties have agreed enough for the intended next step to occur.

Senior intervention must be judged against the same test. The Russian order authorized specified drafts; Modi and Xi endorsed an arrangement already announced; gas declarations left commercial terms to be completed. The visitor must distinguish permission to finish from encouragement to continue.291

Conclusion: What survives Solomon

In the summer of 2025, a useful agreement with China could be measured in materials leaving a port and software becoming available again. After the Geneva accommodation faltered, officials met in London to work out its implementation. Beijing subsequently described an exchange: China would approve eligible export applications, while Washington would remove particular restrictions. Chip-design software companies reported relief from American restrictions. Chinese rare-earth magnet shipments recovered. The controls under which China approved exports remained in place.292

The bargain changed the exercise of authority without abolishing it. That made relief possible while leaving another application, another customer or another intended use to be considered later. For manufacturers needing supplies, an immediate permission could be valuable. For the government negotiating on their behalf, it could also preserve a vulnerability. The decision was whether the relief obtained, on its particular terms, was worth accepting with that exposure still present.

This is a central problem in negotiating with China today. A politically defended limit can coexist with room for a substantial accommodation. The challenge is to identify the decision that would make the accommodation useful, what Beijing values in exchange, and who will carry the remaining risk. Solomon’s attention to purpose, relationships and authority remains essential to that work. WTO membership, new agreements governing scrutiny and a powerful position in supplying foreign industries have given those questions different practical consequences. A ruling, an operating license, an export clearance or permission to reopen a military channel can each be worth obtaining. None is adequately valued by the warmth of the encounter that produced it.

The price of a limited bargain

The limits defended in these negotiations did not all have the same standing. China retained the 49 percent mobile-telecommunications ceiling in its accession bargain; years later it removed the foreign-ownership ceiling for securities companies. In the earlier rare-earth dispute, China reported removing the condemned measures. In the 2025 exchange, licensing authority survived the relief. These are different sectors, instruments and circumstances, but they establish a necessary caution: a rule defended today is not thereby proved permanent.293294295

A government may protect an objective while changing the means by which it pursues it. Foreign ownership can be permitted while the business remains regulated. Shipments can be approved while export controls remain. The negotiator must therefore keep open two possibilities: compensation within the stated limit, and a proposal that requires changing the limit itself. Treating every refusal as a fixed political boundary would concede too much before the bargaining began.

Lamy’s response to the ownership ceiling illustrates one available move. He sought authoritative confirmation, then considered compensating gains in a package he could defend to his principals. That did not make compensation interchangeable. Earlier minority access might serve one company while leaving another without the control its business required. A government negotiating on their behalf needed to decide which of those interests it was securing, and what it was asking the disappointed beneficiary to accept.296

The same judgment applies to relief under an export-control system. A license can restore a factory’s supplies promptly, while repeal may offer broader protection and take longer to obtain. The government must compare that difference with the concession sought in return. If it would make a lasting concession for relief too brief or too narrow to secure the intended benefit, declining the package can be the better choice. The public London record does not reveal enough of the terms and alternatives to calculate that judgment for Washington. It makes the exposure visible.

A partial agreement should be judged by what the unfinished business can still prevent. Once the EP-3 crew had left, continued disagreement over responsibility could not undo their departure. Washington had obtained the immediate benefit without winning the larger argument. The value of the military channels agreed in 2023 would depend on willingness to use them during another confrontation.297 An approved magnet shipment left the next shipment exposed. Russian border residents likewise received access subject to expiry and individual refusal.298

These bargains call for different reciprocal offers. A completed act may justify payment of the agreed price at once. Continuing access gives the recipient reason to seek a longer permission or a staged exchange. Those protections have costs and may be refused. The negotiator must then decide whether immediate relief is valuable enough to accept without them—and whether today’s concession leaves anything useful to offer when the next permission is needed. Counting the subjects left unsettled will not answer that question.

Authority that can be used

Both sides’ authority mattered in accession. Chinese specialists could work out a concession that still required permission across sectors; American negotiators could prepare a package that the White House might decline. Barshefsky’s inclusion of Gene Sperling addressed the second problem. The American departure letters made more time conditional on an opportunity to address the first. Neither step removed the need to decide what the package was worth.299

A foreign official seldom sees the counterpart’s instruction record. The useful response is to present a choice that can be carried for approval: which provision would change, what the other side would receive and which parts remain conditional on the whole package. An extension can then be tied to a particular meeting or response. This does not reveal whether a delay reflects limited authority or bargaining intent. It gives the next interval a purpose and allows the delegation to decide whether the expected decision still warrants staying.

That decision has a cost. Departing may cut off an authorization that is close; extending reveals that the clock is movable. The accession accounts disagree about how much pressure caused the result. They support the narrower practical point that the Americans altered their deadline when staying offered another opportunity. A negotiator can make that choice without pretending to know what delay means inside the other government.

Solomon’s stronger prediction—that senior intervention was the most reliable sign of imminent agreement, in an account that also emphasized concrete drafting—remains unvalidated at that comparative strength. The episodes here do not rank every available signal or refute his forecast. They do show why a negotiator should identify the intervention’s function. Lamy’s March meeting did not close the bargain; his May meeting supplied confirmation and approval in principle around which negotiators could finish. In October 2024, Modi and Xi endorsed a border arrangement already announced. A senior encounter can authorize, confirm or celebrate different stages of a negotiation.300

An intermediary also has to be judged by the work the channel can perform. Kissinger’s July 2023 visit combined exceptional access, Chinese appeals for help and a relationship with American officials who valued his counsel. It deserves attention without attributing the November agreements to an unrecovered July message.301

An official considering an intermediary should be clear about the task: explaining an offer, testing whether a proposal is worth advancing, conveying a warning, or seeking support from someone with authority. Those functions require different kinds of access. A visitor who is welcomed as a symbol may be unable to secure an operational commitment. A technically informed counterpart with less ceremonial standing may know which permission matters and how to request it. The relationship’s practical value lies in that connection to a decision.

Performance and the cost of complaint

The work continues after a commitment is made, but the next act is not always another concession. JPMorgan’s securities business obtained operating permission before Phase One and later became wholly foreign owned. The regulator expressly linked an eight-month acceleration of the ownership opening to the agreement. Company disclosures then recorded ownership and substantial fee income. Further regulatory requirements did not erase those delivered benefits.302

Agricultural biotechnology supplied a less satisfactory experience. Individual approvals could enable an identifiable product launch while leaving the promised review process in dispute. A certificate’s duration and the time needed to obtain it mattered to a business planning a crop launch. Aggregate purchase commitments posed another problem: the examined measures showed a large shortfall, even though their different coverage prevented combining them into one estimate. A useful financial opening did not excuse that shortfall; the shortfall did not make the financial opening imaginary.

Those distinctions matter before agreement. The official seeking access should know whether the firm needs removal of an ownership ceiling, an operating license, an approval for a particular product, or an order on workable commercial terms. Different promises require different evidence of performance. The firm may also value timing differently from the government announcing the bargain. A permission that arrives after a commercial opportunity has passed can satisfy a narrow formal requirement while providing much less of the expected benefit.

Solomon’s warning that bargaining continues after signature survives these cases. His stronger claim—that China demands adherence from others while seeking accommodation for itself—requires further evidence. Some agreements expressly left permissions and consultations for later. Further work under those provisions was part of the bargain. To establish an asymmetric demand, the critic must identify the duty and the different treatment being sought. That distinction gives a government firmer ground for complaint when a promised benefit is withheld.303

Learning what happened is itself a negotiating problem. In 2019, Lighthizer told Congress that companies feared identifying themselves when they complained about Chinese conduct. Phase One allowed an appeal to omit a company’s identifying information and required confidentiality for the material. This recognized a conflict inside the enforcement relationship: the firm best placed to document a failure might still depend on the authority it would be reporting.304

Confidentiality could help the government obtain evidence. It could not ensure that the circumstances would not identify the complainant, or that the firm would risk the disclosure. The foreign negotiator therefore needed more than a well-written duty and an available remedy. The intended beneficiaries needed a feasible way to tell their government that the duty was not being performed. Where that route is weak, a quiet public record is a poor measure of whether the concession works.

Even a well-documented failure leaves a further political decision. The soybean and ethanol witnesses at the December 2025 hearing agreed that China had fallen short but differed over the response: prospective sales gave one constituency something to protect while lost access gave another reason to demand action.305

A government promising to enforce an agreement must anticipate that division. The beneficiaries of retaliation may differ from the companies or farmers bearing its immediate costs. Prospective new purchases can make action on an old shortfall harder to sustain. This does not make a threat worthless. It means its credibility depends partly on whether the government will still consider it worth carrying out after the next offer arrives. The price of enforcement belongs in the original bargain.

What the update requires

The history does not replace a China of personal relationships with a China of impersonal rules. Technical preparation, conditional offers and attention to wording were present during accession. Freeman’s 1999 essay already described specialization, institutional participation and adaptation to counterparts. He allowed relationship-building to fail or have uncertain results. Those qualifications belong to the inheritance, not to the discoveries of this book.306

One concrete change was the development of the people and organizations needed to use an external legal procedure against powerful trading partners. Membership supplied the rights; officials, domestic and foreign lawyers, and academic specialists learned how to turn a commercial objection into a claim. Their work also connected litigation to the Chinese agencies whose measures had to be defended or altered. An American official could refuse China’s bilateral request yet still have to answer its treaty argument.307308 With ordinary WTO appeals unavailable, appeal arbitration requires agreement between the parties: China and the EU have used that route, but China’s participation alone supplies no such agreement with Washington.309

Detailed demands on domestic administration had a longer history. In 1995, the intellectual-property bargain prescribed Chinese enforcement machinery, contacts and deadlines for complaints, reciprocal information and continuing consultations. Washington retained its domestic-law route back to trade action. Phase One’s particular addition was a bilateral procedure linking a government complaint to specified levels of consultation and a contested remedy. The respondent could not counterrespond to a measure it judged taken in good faith; withdrawal was the specified remedy if it judged the measure taken in bad faith. No independent adjudicator would decide that question. A dispute over performance could put the wider agreement at risk. That was a consequential allocation of discretion, built on a long history of detailed demands backed by trade pressure.310311

Other bargains allocated discretion differently. Climate diplomacy specified international scrutiny while preserving national determination of targets. China could accept common procedures without accepting an internationally binding obligation to achieve each announced national result.312 In the 2025 exchange, permission for Chinese-controlled supplies to reach foreign industries became central to the bargain. Obtaining access to China and obtaining supplies from China could expose a counterpart to different decisions by the Chinese state.313

Power matters through these choices. China’s position in processing magnet rare earths gave a licensing decision consequences for foreign production. Russia’s need for gas customers did not settle the route, finance or purchasing commitments for another pipeline. The operation of one line and the unfinished commercial terms of another showed both the value and limits of the partnership. India made progress at the border a condition for the wider relationship, while local arrangements, de-escalation and a final boundary settlement remained distinct objectives. The bargaining position belonged to the particular dependency and alternative, as well as to the countries involved.314

Under Xi, the 2018 reforms consolidated foreign-affairs coordination and supervision. The Foreign Relations Law of 2023 then stated distinct responsibilities for central direction, the State Council, military exchanges, diplomatic coordination and authorized provincial activity. A subsequent conference on local foreign affairs demanded discipline while also encouraging initiative. Officials were being told to exercise initiative within central objectives.315

For the visitor, this means following a proposal through more than the senior encounter. Political support must reach the body expected to issue the permission, arrange the call or carry out the project. The 2023 military-channel sequence makes that practical difficulty visible: strategic contact with Wang Yi coexisted with refusal of the proposed defense-minister meeting, and the later presidential agreement still required departmental arrangements. These observations do not reveal internal conflict; they show why securing one official’s attention cannot substitute for identifying the next act and who must perform it.

The cases cannot assign their differences to Xi’s personality or measure a national shift toward rigidity, patience or deception. They do establish changed responsibilities, capabilities and sources of pressure. A negotiator needs those particulars to judge what makes a refusal costly, what a concession would change and whether the official across the table can help obtain it.

The enduring lesson is demanding because it leaves the foreign negotiator with decisions to make. Test a specific reciprocal offer, and establish what one’s own government can sustain. Use relationships to understand and reach the relevant authority. Value the concession at the point where a person or enterprise can use it, while pricing the discretion the other side retains. Decide how much evidence and protection are needed before committing, and what costs one’s own side would accept if performance fails.

Solomon made Chinese negotiating behavior intelligible as purposeful political activity. That remains the right starting point. The update carries that explanation into the institutions, firms and administrative acts through which a bargain takes effect. A government may decide to take the licenses that restore production while continuing to seek more durable access. It may reject an attractive announcement because the permission its firms need remains untouched. It may enforce a broken promise despite the next offer of sales. Knowledge of China helps it understand those choices. The decision still requires its own government to say what it is prepared to obtain, concede and risk.

Appendix 1. What remains of the 59 baseline propositions

Assessment date: 15 September 2026. Complete assessment, revised after the separate proposition audit.

This appendix preserves all 34 context/Freeman propositions and all 25 process propositions in the unchanged research inventory. Its task is to make every current judgment traceable to a tested component, the strongest relevant support, contrary or limiting evidence, and the remaining uncertainty. The accompanying assessment data preserve each entire baseline record, including its initial proposed test and original evidence description.

The baseline statements below are the inventory’s exact paraphrases, not quotations claimed to be Solomon’s or Freeman’s exact sentences. Solomon’s principal empirical period is 1967–1984 and his central setting is constructive American–Chinese political negotiation. Freeman’s separate 1999 essay is already an update; his learning claims cannot be treated as original Solomon claims or as independent corroboration of them. Each entry also reproduces its claim-specific baseline qualification, including any original access caveat; the current evidence and linked dossiers control subsequent acquisitions. See the baseline reading, process reading and unchanged context and process inventories.

A retained verdict applies only to the tested component, not automatically to every mechanism in the full sentence. Evidence that officials cultivate relationships is different from evidence that this changes policy. Technical preparation does not by itself prove superiority; recurring conduct does not prove cultural origin. Supporting observations may be partial or attributed, as stated. “Contrary or limiting” includes counterexamples, competing explanations, reciprocal practices and access limits; it does not label all of them falsifications.

The source IDs link to local dossiers containing the registers, original documents, locators and reading limits. They are not votes: repeated editions, translations, official readouts and reports depending on the same interviews remain related source chains. Cases reused across propositions likewise do not become new independent observations. This appendix consolidates registered readings; it does not claim a fresh full reading of every source or access to undisclosed instructions.

All seven substantive chapters and their stable case dossiers supply this assessment. Dossier citations continue to control the original documents, reading limits and individual case endpoints. Each dossier retains its own event endpoint. The date of this appendix does not claim complete historical coverage through September 2026. The selected corpus cannot estimate general frequencies, identify a uniquely Chinese repertoire, or measure historical change simply by comparing unlike instruments.

Two qualifications govern the whole exercise. First, the unchanged S-P24 extraction says “can signal,” but this softens the printed claim. Solomon p. 149 calls senior intervention “the most reliable sign” of imminent agreement and invokes leaders’ reluctance to risk face; p. 150 asserts a highly consistent endgame. The failed claims/assets example on p. 148 creates a tension with that prediction. The current verdict evaluates this stronger original claim, in its concrete-drafting context, rather than silently substituting a weaker one. Second, S-P25 combines continued bargaining with a stronger asymmetry: demanding the counterpart’s adherence while seeking accommodation oneself. The first is well supported, but does not establish the second.

Three verified factual corrections—the Cushing treaty, normalization date and Geng Biao’s office—remain separate from judgments about behavioral propositions. No entire proposition is classified “correct baseline” on those factual errors alone.

Status key

Status Meaning Propositions
retain within tested scope The specified component is directly supported within the examined episodes. This does not retain every causal, comparative or cultural element of the full baseline proposition. 16
revise or narrow The proposition requires an explicit restriction or reformulation for this corpus. A scope correction is not necessarily evidence of temporal change. 0
correct baseline A proposition-level baseline statement is factually wrong on the acquired evidence. No entire behavioral proposition receives this status here; separate factual corrections are linked below. 0
unresolved Relevant observations or candidate mechanisms exist, but the available evidence does not decide a consequential part of the proposition. 38
untested The acquired corpus contains no adequate case-level test of the specified mechanism. Absence of a test is not evidence against it. 5

The totals describe this assessment’s coverage, not a score for the book. Many entries deliberately retain an observable practice while leaving its proposed cause, efficacy or comparative prevalence unresolved. The independent assessment review distinguishes a restriction already present in the original from a new finding. No proposition currently receives a revise-or-narrow verdict: missing validation of a frequency or causal mechanism is classified as unresolved. Advice against using rigidity or senior contact as a shortcut is stated separately from a claim of empirical refutation.

Solomon: context and constraints

S-C01 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 16–24): Chinese negotiating style varies with the political purpose of the relationship: officials can use markedly different approaches in confrontation and in constructing cooperation.

Baseline scope and qualification: The principal study covers a selected setting in which Beijing wanted a positive U.S. relationship. Different behavior after 2018 need not mean the original findings were wrong within that scope. The cited underlying records have not been independently acquired in this workstream.

Tested component: Whether differences in negotiating conduct follow the political purpose of the relationship, rather than the issue, channel, personnel or institution.

Strongest relevant support: Diplomatic contact reopened while defense access remained unavailable; later specified cooperation coexisted with political disagreement. C23-09, C23-10, C23-15, C23-17, C23-18 This establishes channel-specific variation, not a matched comparison isolating relationship purpose.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Close political relations did not settle successive gas projects. RUS-G14, RUS-G17, RUS-G19, RUS-G28 Relationship purpose does not determine individual commercial terms.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Keep Solomon’s original restriction to a constructive relationship as a boundary on interpretation. The later cases show variation but do not identify political purpose as its cause; the central comparative claim remains unresolved. Confrontation is not automatic refutation of the cooperation-era baseline.

S-C02 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 18–22, 25, 31–34): Chinese domestic social and political traditions help produce a persistent preference for managing negotiation through personal relationships and reciprocal obligations.

Baseline scope and qualification: Observed use of relationships does not identify culture as its cause. The study assumed distinctive style would be legible to foreign observers; unfamiliarity and observer expectations can influence that assessment.

Tested component: Persistence of relationship cultivation; cultural origin separated from its observable use.

Strongest relevant support: Xi and Wang publicly honored Kissinger and sought a constructive contribution. K23-01, K23-02

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No U.S. mission mandate was acknowledged; consultation existed without a specified policy effect. K23-04, K23-10 Neither record identifies cultural causation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Need comparative decisions linking reciprocal obligations to changed conduct, controlling for policy affinity, reputation, information and access. Persistence is not a test of its cultural explanation.

S-C03 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 28–38): Chinese negotiators resist terms or public presentations that depict China as dependent, even when material conditions create a need for foreign cooperation.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter itself records acknowledgment of mutual need and prior acceptance of a subordinate Soviet relationship. National humiliation and imperial status are proposed explanations, not directly observed motives in every episode.

Tested component: Resistance to unequal presentation alongside acceptance of qualified material concessions.

Strongest relevant support: Liu defended dignity and balanced terms; China later defended a partial agreement with partial tariff relief. CN-TRADE-LIU201905, CN-TRADE-SCIO20191213

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The accepted text imposed different new burdens, including Chinese IP changes. POA-EN, POA-CN Resistance to dependency language did not require identical obligations.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retained as a public positioning practice. National humiliation as motive and the bargaining price of equal presentation require instructions and draft changes.

S-C04 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 31–44): Cultivating a foreign official’s investment in access and reputation can create leverage when Chinese counterparts subsequently cast doubt on that relationship.

Baseline scope and qualification: Personal warmth and repeated demands for commitment are observable; their independent effect on final concessions is not established by the quoted exchanges alone.

Tested component: Whether cultivation becomes effective pressure through threatened loss of personal access.

Strongest relevant support: Public cultivation and an advocacy request are directly documented. K23-01, K23-02 This supplies a possible channel, not the hypothesized threat.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Expected debrief and acknowledged advice do not disclose a threat, responsive concession or modified instruction. K23-06, K23-10

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Recover the interlocutor’s prior preferences, stake in access, Chinese threat and resulting action. The mechanism has not been demonstrated in the later audit.

S-C05 — unresolved
