Baseline claim (printed pp. 32–34): Trusted intermediaries facilitate politically sensitive openings by conveying intentions, supplying information about leaders, and certifying that contact will not produce embarrassment or confrontation.

Baseline scope and qualification: The mechanism may be universal in secret diplomacy rather than distinctively Chinese. Channels also served American secrecy and political requirements.

Tested component: Added information or credibility from a trusted intermediary, distinct from a messenger’s presence.

Strongest relevant support: Stern describes confidential specialist exchanges and building trust with Xie. CLI06 Retrospective participant account, not a complete paired message chain.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Private status, possible debrief and general advice do not establish a carried operational message or its effect. K23-04, K23-06, K23-10

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Formal negotiator rapport and a retired intermediary are different channels. No later source packet here demonstrates the complete assurance-to-instruction mechanism or national uniqueness.

S-C06 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 39–42, 167, 174–175): Chinese officials exploit existing political or bureaucratic rivalries by favoring counterparts whose preferences better match Chinese objectives and making others fear a loss of influence.

Baseline scope and qualification: Selecting a receptive channel is not the same as changing a government’s preferences. American rivalries are part of the explanation and cannot be coded solely as Chinese tactical success.

Tested component: Chinese selection of congenial counterparts versus exploitation of bureaucratic rivalry.

Strongest relevant support: Senior officials welcomed a sympathetic former official; Wang contrasted current policy with Nixon/Kissinger diplomacy. K23-01, K23-02, K23-03

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: U.S. agricultural witnesses disagreed about new enforcement action. POI12 No evidence Beijing created or successfully exploited that division.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Need contemporaneous selection strategy, rival actors’ reactions and an altered balance of policy. Rivalry and preferred access alone do not demonstrate leverage.

S-C07 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 42–44): Uncertainty over meetings with senior Chinese leaders can be used to induce foreign counterparts to clarify intentions or accommodate Chinese conditions.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon expressly speculates that the second Nixon meeting was canceled over Taiwan; illness was the Chinese explanation. That case cannot be counted as proven tactical manipulation.

Tested component: Conditional access as a bargaining object, distinguished from uncertainty over a senior appointment.

Strongest relevant support: Chinese statements connected denied defense contact to political conditions. C23-06, C23-11, C23-12

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Blinken met Xi but announced no immediate military-channel agreement. C23-15, C23-16 A granted senior meeting was not sufficient for the requested result.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: This supports selective access as observable conduct, not the specific mechanism that uncertain scheduling induced accommodation. Need appointment communications and recipient responses.

S-C08 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 44–48, 172): Detailed preparation and coordinated management of information, protocol, and timing give Chinese negotiators an operational advantage when counterparts are less prepared.

Baseline scope and qualification: Preparation quality is not proof of a superior outcome. The p46 Geng Biao appointment narrative is present in the original scan but appears historically problematic and should not be reproduced without external verification.

Tested component: Preparation, information circulation, authority to challenge assessments and resulting advantage.

Strongest relevant support: Detailed Chinese submissions and benchmark proposals demonstrate preparation and some accepted technical argument. DS379-03, DS379-04

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Jost proposes a failure of institutional information handling during EP-3. EP319 Selected retrospective scholarship; contemporaneous Chinese deliberations and parts of its source chain remain unverified.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No comparative estimate of operational advantage. Correcting the Geng Biao anecdote in the baseline does not by itself refute preparation as a mechanism; see the separate verified-corrections dossier.

S-C09 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 48–50): Chinese negotiators shape their statements for superiors and political rivals as well as for the foreign counterpart, encouraging disciplined repetition and reluctance to reveal unapproved positions.

Baseline scope and qualification: Repeated language suggests instructions but does not expose their contents. Foreign observers’ impressions of internal audiences require corroboration.

Tested component: Whether official repetition reflects a domestic audience and withheld authorization.

Strongest relevant support: Sector participant describes requests moving through regulator and central approval hierarchies. EWT-13 Participant reconstruction with noted chronology/institution issues.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Published chronology describes unsuccessful coordination and a collectively defended limit. EWT-01 The same refusal can reflect an agreed substantive ceiling, not political rivalry.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Obtain instructions, reporting drafts and audience reactions. Repetition is observed; the domestic incentive causing it is not independently identified.

S-C10 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 49–50, 168, 170): In high-level political negotiations, flexibility often requires senior authorization after the foreign position has been tested; senior intervention can release an apparent deadlock.

Baseline scope and qualification: A leader’s visible intervention does not prove that lack of prior authorization caused delay. Issue stakes, tactical theater, and substantive incompatibility remain alternatives.

Tested component: Identifiable senior or regulatory decisions permitting or confirming particular negotiating terms, with frequency and the prior-testing sequence assessed separately.

Strongest relevant support: Deng and Washington made bounded decisions while specific disagreement remained. NOR-166, NOR-167, NOR-170, NOR-172 Participant identifies fresh State Council and regulator authorization for financial provisions. EWT-13 Authority chain remains retrospective on the Chinese side.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Zhu’s March 2000 meeting did not close the agreement; May confirmation concerned a limit as well as compensation. EWT-01

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain the observed authorization component in these episodes. The claim that flexibility often arrives only after fully testing the counterpart, prior lack of instructions as the cause of delay, leader persuasion and general success rates remain unresolved. Senior confirmation can concern a ceiling as well as a concession.

S-C11 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 50–56): Leadership weakness, interministerial disagreement, and problems obtaining local compliance can immobilize negotiation while the public Chinese position continues to appear unified.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon marks the Lin Biao, radical-faction, and Zhou explanations as obscure, inferred, likely, or circumstantial. Unified public speech does not establish unity or conflict by itself.

Tested component: Specific institutional blockage, rather than inferring division from a unified public stance or delay.

Strongest relevant support: Solomon’s contemporary analysis links the failed claims arrangement to internal pressures; the later U.S. review records withdrawal. CLA06, CLA10 Partly redacted source chain; memorandum and book are not independent witnesses.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Long canola approval histories exist in China and the EU. AG05, AG16 Elapsed time does not identify Chinese leadership weakness or interministerial conflict.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No contemporary Chinese veto record supplies the decisive mechanism in these cases. Distinguish capacity, substantive review, applicant responses and agreed priorities from factional conflict.

S-C12 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 44–45, 167–170): The shift from a small leadership-managed opening to restored bureaucracies and technical issues increased the number of relevant Chinese institutions and could slow decisionmaking.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon already recognizes both centralization and bureaucratic friction. The apparent late-1976 normalization date on p44 is printed in the scan but conflicts with his own p23 chronology and is not used here.

Tested component: Multiplicity of institutions and sequential permissions, not an inferred decline in central control.

Strongest relevant support: Establishment, licensing, shareholder registration and branch permissions are distinct decisions. FIN-07, FIN-16, FIN-26 WMCC has specified functions alongside the Special Representatives mechanism. IND-MEA-17963

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The same institutional system implemented an accelerated ownership timetable and operating business. FIN-15, FIN-26 Multiple stages do not establish that fragmentation caused delay.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The historical expansion mechanism was already in Solomon. Comparative timing and coordination effects remain unmeasured; the baseline’s normalization-date error is separately corrected.

S-C13 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 4–5, 170, 173): Chinese political negotiators tend to prefer broad understandings and principles to exhaustive legal detail, but can set principles aside or rationalize concrete compromise when agreement serves their interests.

Baseline scope and qualification: The study’s high-political baseline is not a representative sample of commercial or technical negotiations. A principle that can always be overridden risks becoming an unfalsifiable explanation.

Original-text qualification: Solomon p. 5 already permits a negotiator seeking agreement to set aside early principles and reach a concrete accord; pp. 170 and 173 discuss political relationships and American–Chinese differences in approach. A later legal or technical instrument is not by itself a counterexample to that qualified political preference.

Tested component: A relative preference for broad principles within political negotiation, together with the acknowledged ability to reach concrete compromise.

Strongest relevant support: Chinese public diplomacy continues to frame demands through broad relationship principles. C23-14, C23-16 Use of principles does not identify preference over an available, more specific alternative or its effect on the bargain.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: China advanced detailed legal arguments and accepted a precise procedural bargain. DS379-03, DS379-07 Litigation and an implementation-procedure agreement concern different institutional choices from the political understandings at the center of the original claim. Specific obligations coexist with political discretion. POA-EN, POA-CN The existence of specific provisions is compatible with the original allowance for concrete compromise.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The original scope and exceptions must be preserved, not counted as revisions discovered by this study. Public principle language and detailed later instruments coexist, but do not establish the relative preference, its constraints or a historical change. Those questions require comparable choices over document form, drafts and the terms negotiators rejected.

S-C14 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 6, 8, 172–173): Chinese continuity and command of the negotiating record can create leverage over American officials whose turnover and dispersed archives impair knowledge of earlier commitments.

Baseline scope and qualification: Claims of complete Chinese command should not be accepted as literal universals. Record knowledge, the legitimacy of an interpretation, and the ability to obtain concessions are separate variables.

Tested component: Knowledge and use of the prior record, separated from superior memory and concession leverage.

Strongest relevant support: Liu invoked an asserted Argentina purchase consensus. CN-TRADE-LIU201905

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The contemporaneous U.S. dinner readout explicitly left the amount unagreed. TUS05 This discrepancy does not determine which private understanding existed.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No comparative personnel/archive measure establishes Chinese memory superiority or resulting leverage. Both accurate invocation and favorable reconstruction must be checked against the common text.

S-C15 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 9, 173): Placing a technical issue within a broader relationship framework can make compromise easier for Chinese decisionmakers to justify.

Baseline scope and qualification: The formulation may accompany a prior decision rather than cause it. Advice is not itself a tested intervention.

Tested component: Whether relationship framing enabled a particular technical compromise.

Strongest relevant support: Opening was presented through bilateral benefit and domestic reform. FIN-01, FIN-14 Leaders endorsed nationally differentiated climate intentions and cooperation. CLI12, CLI15

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Domestic reform and timetable changes preceded the final bilateral agreement. FIN-03, FIN-10, FIN-11

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Framing can justify a prior decision as well as cause a new one. Need proposals and decision records showing an otherwise unacceptable technical term became acceptable through the broader frame.

S-C16 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 9, 165, 174): Deadlines create bargaining vulnerability for whichever side most needs an agreement; Chinese negotiators try to exploit American urgency but are themselves vulnerable when Chinese deadlines bind.

Baseline scope and qualification: Civilizational time horizons cannot substitute for documented incentives. Delay may also reflect authorization or uncertainty rather than tactical patience.

Tested component: Reciprocal use and adjustment of deadlines; not a universal Chinese time advantage.

Strongest relevant support: November 12 and 14 American letters used departure threats and an extension linked to a Zhu meeting. CN-WTO-B3 B3 pp.1067–1070 and 1094; effectiveness disputed.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: American tariff deadlines were repeatedly extended. TUS09 Regulatory duration includes non-Chinese as well as Chinese constraints. AG16, AG05

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain the conditional proposition that urgency can matter. Chinese exploitation of a particular deadline and measured concession effects remain unresolved.

S-C17 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 9, 168–169, 175): Confidential exchanges can reduce public expectation pressures and increase perceived seriousness, while publicity can be used to constrain a counterpart’s room to compromise.

Baseline scope and qualification: The claimed benefits of confidentiality may be selection effects: promising negotiations may be easier to keep quiet. Public constraints can also supply leverage or credibility.

Tested component: Documented management of different public audiences, with causal benefits left unproved.

Strongest relevant support: The released letter and U.S. legal guidance distinguish immediate settlement language from preserved responsibility positions. EP303, EP321

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Contemporary and later American accounts disagree over the purpose and effects of publishing accession commitments. US-APR08-BRIEFING

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain as an observed instrument of bargaining. Confidentiality’s independent contribution, audience constraint and publication effects require private decisions and reactions.

S-C18 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 157–160, 166, 176–177): Competent management of negotiating tactics may improve control and mutual respect, but its effects on agreement outcomes are often secondary to state interests and official discipline; excessive manipulation can undermine trust.

Baseline scope and qualification: Chapter 4 develops recommendations rather than testing competing strategies. Its proposed mechanisms should not be reported as demonstrated outcome effects.

Tested component: State interests, institutional obligations and available options as necessary controls on tactical explanation.

Strongest relevant support: Political warmth coexists with different commercial closure stages. RUS-G03, RUS-G17, RUS-G28 A negotiated timetable changed domestic regulation and produced usable access. FIN-15, FIN-26

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Blinken acknowledged seeking Kissinger’s advice. K23-10 Material-interest explanations should not erase possible interpersonal contributions.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain the anti-omnipotence qualification; the comparative magnitude of tactical and substantive effects has not been estimated, and counterproductive manipulation is not separately tested.

S-C19 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 175–176): Rigidity or polemical conduct by Chinese negotiators is usually a sign of leadership conflict, making accommodation less likely.

Baseline scope and qualification: This is stronger than the underlying evidence warrants without testing: chapter 2 itself lists several other causes of inflexibility. Public language cannot reliably identify leadership conflict on its own.

Tested component: Whether observed rigidity usually indicates leadership conflict and predicts reduced capacity for accommodation.

Strongest relevant support: A contemporary participant-analyst proposed internal pressure as a cause. CLA06 Its underlying evidence is partly inaccessible and is reused by the book.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Firm positions accompany specific collective ceilings and limits in the European mandate. EWT-01 The record does not establish the presence or absence of Chinese leadership conflict behind the collective ceiling. Institutional review and non-Chinese delay provide alternatives to a factional diagnosis. AG09, AG16 These are alternative mechanisms in different regulatory settings, not a frequency test of rigidity in high political negotiation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The empirical frequency claim remains unresolved: no matched sample or sufficient Chinese internal evidence establishes how often rigidity reflects leadership conflict. As practical advice, do not diagnose such conflict from public rigidity alone; investigate authority, substantive limits and competing explanations. That evidentiary caution is not a demonstrated revision of behavior or a finding that leadership conflict was absent.

S-C20 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 162–170): American turnover, divided authority, and relatively open information flows can both create openings for Chinese pressure and frustrate Chinese efforts to secure stable commitments and personal access.

Baseline scope and qualification: National-character contrasts in Table 4 are hypotheses, not measured attributes. Chinese perceptions of American institutions are often inferred rather than documented internally.

Tested component: Interactive effects of American divided authority and changing public positions.

Strongest relevant support: Executive disagreement and separate congressional PNTR authority are explicit. US-APR08-BRIEFING, US-NOV15-BERGER Industry witnesses favor different enforcement choices. POI12

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: A long-retired official remained available for consultation despite turnover. K23-10 Personal continuity can coexist with institutional dispersion.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain institutional constraints as part of the bilateral explanation. Chinese perceptions, deliberate exploitation and the net advantage of openness remain unproved.

Freeman: the late-1990s update

F01 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 182–184): By the late 1990s, Chinese negotiators still emphasized long-term relationships and shared principles, using deferral of difficult issues to allow common interests and trust to develop.

Baseline scope and qualification: Freeman does not disclose a new systematic archive or interview sample. Brief case mentions do not establish causal mechanisms, and endorsement of Solomon is not independent proof.

Original-text qualification: Freeman p. 183 presents the trust-building rationale as the Chinese view, then explicitly allows success in an appropriate context, failure and, continuing on p. 184, uncertain success. He does not claim that time or relationship language necessarily resolves disagreement.

Tested component: Deferral and relationship cultivation are observable; the intended and actual growth of common interests or trust requires a separate causal test.

Strongest relevant support: Reserved areas persisted across cooperation and were later delimited. RUS-T1991-CN, RUS-T2001, RUS-T2004 The sequence establishes reservation followed by later settlement, not the intention behind deferral or the mechanism producing settlement. Historical relationship recognition persisted in 2023. K23-01, K23-02 Recognition of an old relationship does not establish a deferred bargain or increased mutual trust.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Repeated political endorsement did not eliminate commercial disagreement. RUS-G14, RUS-G17, RUS-G28 Continued disagreement is compatible with Freeman’s explicit allowance for failure or uncertain success; it does not revise that qualification.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The repertoire recurs, but its intended and actual causal contribution remains unresolved. Do not convert Freeman’s conditional and partly attributed account into an automatic trust-producing rule and then claim to refute it. The Russia sequence requires intervening decisions and alternatives; the Kissinger case requires a specified transmission and policy effect.

F02 — untested

Baseline claim (printed pp. 184–185): Foreign-exchange and official spending restrictions materially reinforced the Chinese preference for negotiating in China.

Baseline scope and qualification: The anecdotes are not accompanied by the administrative records here. Budget constraints can explain some venue choices without exhausting cultural, intelligence, or strategic reasons.

Tested component: Budget and foreign-exchange rules as a cause of negotiating venue.

Strongest relevant support: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No dated travel-budget or entertainment-rule comparison was acquired. Beijing meetings cannot test this economic explanation, and later overseas meetings alone cannot establish why venue changed.

F03 — untested

Baseline claim (printed pp. 185–186): Having foreign counterparts state their positions first gives Chinese diplomats a politically safer record from which to seek revised instructions, reducing the risk of appearing to advocate foreign interests.

Baseline scope and qualification: Speaking second also has universal informational bargaining advantages. Freeman’s mechanism must be distinguished from tactical extraction rather than replacing it by assertion.

Tested component: Speaking second as protection against appearing to advocate foreign interests when seeking new instructions.

Strongest relevant support: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Authority accounts do not reconstruct speaking order, internal reporting or career risk. Need paired meeting and internal submission records; ordinary information-gathering is a competing mechanism.

F04 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 186–187): Reluctance to delegate and the need for rapid authoritative guidance reinforce negotiation near Chinese decisionmakers, even after communications technology improves.

Baseline scope and qualification: Home-ground intelligence and protocol advantages are additional causes. Faster communication could reduce delay while increasing central micromanagement, so technical improvement alone does not predict greater delegate autonomy.

Tested component: Proximity to authority, communications capacity and delegation as determinants of venue.

Strongest relevant support: Financial negotiator describes referrals to State Council and regulator leaders. EWT-13

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The strategic channel operated in Vienna, away from Beijing. C23-09, C23-10 A foreign venue does not identify how its mandate was supplied.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No matched venue-choice records show whether communications improvements reduced delay or increased micromanagement. Evidence of referrals is not evidence that proximity caused the venue.

F05 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 187–188): International opening, research networks, and greater political room improved Chinese knowledge of foreign politics and the candor and usefulness of policy reporting by the late 1990s.

Baseline scope and qualification: The essay supplies no systematic accuracy or candor measure. Its optimistic judgment is period-specific and may not hold under later political conditions.

Tested component: Improved acquisition of foreign information versus candor, accuracy and institutional use.

Strongest relevant support: Specialist submissions and methodological proposals demonstrate usable foreign-rule knowledge. DS379-03, DS379-04

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Retrospective institutional analysis proposes filtered military information in EP-3. EP319 Chinese internal records and raw interview chain remain missing.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: A later capability observation does not verify Freeman’s period-wide improvement in reporting candor. Need internal reports, corrections and their treatment across time.

F06 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 188–190): Weak public diplomacy and limited rapport with foreign legislators and interest groups hinder Chinese ability to shape or understand counterpart positions before negotiating instructions harden.

Baseline scope and qualification: The essay’s statements about China’s lobbying tools and capacity require independent period verification and must not be projected to 2026. Hostility may derive from substantive disputes rather than inadequate communication.

Original-text qualification: Freeman pp. 188–190 already records growing press and congressional outreach while criticizing its effectiveness, and expressly dates his diagnosis to the end of the twentieth century. Existing channels alone therefore do not contradict his account.

Tested component: Whether limited outreach to legislators, interest groups and the public impeded understanding or influence before counterpart instructions formed, and whether that capacity changed after Freeman’s period.

Strongest relevant support: Xie publicly described difficulty bringing forward Americans favorable to relations. K23-07 An official diagnosis is not an independent measure of the obstacle.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Kissinger was cultivated by Chinese leaders, and Blinken later acknowledged consulting him before the June 2023 visit. K23-01, K23-02, K23-10 This establishes access to an executive consultation channel, not effective legislative, interest-group or public outreach; it discloses no resulting change to Blinken’s instructions.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The audience-specific capacity claim and its change over time remain unresolved. Xie’s public diagnosis is a position, while Kissinger’s executive access is not a contrary test of Freeman’s named audiences. Need actual outreach, recipient responses and instruction formation, separating communication limits from disagreement over policy.

F07 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 190–191): Chinese participation in multilateral institutions tends to move from procedural learning and relative passivity toward more active, competent bargaining as experience and communications improve.

Baseline scope and qualification: Increased activism may reflect power, resources, or changed objectives rather than learning. The essay gives no enumerated institutional sample.

Tested component: Active institutional competence is observed; the proposed learning trajectory is not identified.

Strongest relevant support: China used legal interpretation, appeal and negotiated review procedures. DS379-03, DS379-04, DS379-07 Successive multilateral instruments specify international procedures with differentiated responsibilities. CLI07, CLI08, CLI17

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Some Chinese claims failed or remained unresolved. DS379-02, DS379-04 Paris required other governments’ bargaining and did not simply enact the bilateral package. CLI16

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Need earlier/later actor-specific evidence identifying learning, counsel, resources and changed objectives. Outcomes and meeting participation do not isolate learning; Freeman already proposed it.

F08 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 191): China has at times pragmatically negotiated changes involving its domestic policies and laws to preserve valued international relationships, even while objecting to foreign scrutiny.

Baseline scope and qualification: A negotiated pledge is not evidence of implementation. Public objection to intrusion can coexist with practical bargaining and cannot alone establish an absolute red line.

Tested component: Pragmatic acceptance of domestic-policy and legal changes, with separate implementation evidence.

Strongest relevant support: Article 1.34 preserves domestic methods while requiring implementation and possible law-amendment proposals. POA-EN, POA-CN An April ownership date was explicitly implemented to meet Phase One requirements. FIN-12, FIN-15

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Industry and USTR continued to contest biotech process performance. AG21, AG22 Attributed claims do not supply a full cohort audit.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain the possibility demonstrated by a specific regulatory act. It does not imply all commitments were fulfilled or reveal which international relationship was decisive in every reform.

F09 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 192–193): The expansion of technical, economic, legal, and military negotiations reduced the Foreign Ministry’s ability to coordinate all Chinese dealings with foreign counterparts.

Baseline scope and qualification: More participants need not imply less central political control. Agency autonomy, coordination costs, and final authorization are different variables.

Tested component: Specialist participation versus a causal reduction in MFA coordinating capacity.

Strongest relevant support: Sector authority and MOFTEC coordination are described. EWT-13 Working-group arrangements identify specialist agencies. CLI10

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Diplomatic, defense and other agencies participated within an articulated political framework. IND-CN-2024-WMCC31

Assessment and unresolved remainder: More participants do not establish diminished MFA control, autonomy from leadership or coordination failure. Need institutional mandates, actual referral paths and a longitudinal comparison.

F10 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 193): Chinese bureaucratic and industrial interests defending domestic positions account for part of the difficulty in agreeing to foreign terms for WTO accession.

Baseline scope and qualification: The essay is not evidence of who blocked which terms in April or November 1999. Domestic interests compete with bargaining tactics, foreign constraints, and leadership preferences as explanations.

Tested component: Domestic sector interests as a reason for resistance in accession bargaining.

Strongest relevant support: Participant identifies financial-sector options and approval constraints. EWT-13 Ownership ceilings and compensating terms were defended in sector-specific bargaining. EWT-01, EWT-13

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Lamy’s own authority required member-state and industry consultation; China also made concrete sector concessions. EWT-01

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Direct Chinese interagency submissions and veto decisions are missing. Sector sensitivity is established more securely than the political weight of particular industrial coalitions.

F11 — untested

Baseline claim (printed pp. 193–194): Long career specialization gives Chinese negotiators deep bilateral memory but may limit cross-issue breadth and leave policy integration concentrated in senior leadership.

Baseline scope and qualification: Length of service is not a direct measure of knowledge or power. The claim should be tested by institution and generation, not assumed from prominent long-serving individuals.

Tested component: Career specialization’s joint effect on memory, cross-issue breadth and centralized integration.

Strongest relevant support: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No career-level comparative sample or internal knowledge test was acquired. A specialist’s detailed testimony cannot establish limited breadth or locate policy integration in the senior hierarchy.

F12 — untested

Baseline claim (printed pp. 194): Mentoring and on-the-job instruction can transmit established negotiating practices despite increased staff turnover.

Baseline scope and qualification: Continuity after turnover could also come from unchanged instructions, interests, or institutions. Mentoring is a proposed transmission mechanism, not demonstrated by continuity alone.

Tested component: Mentoring and on-the-job teaching as the mechanism transmitting bargaining practices.

Strongest relevant support: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No training, mentoring, personnel-handover or observed instruction record has been acquired. Similar conduct by later officials is not evidence of this transmission mechanism.

F13 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 194–195): Repeated interaction produces counterpart- and organization-specific Chinese negotiating substyles as specialists adapt to the people and procedures they encounter.

Baseline scope and qualification: Freeman does not provide interview transcripts or a systematic comparative sample. A broader shared tradition and variation by individual or issue may coexist with specialist adaptation.

Tested component: Counterpart- and organization-specific adaptation through repeated interaction.

Strongest relevant support: The same accession campaign yielded a European package with distinct priorities and timings. EWT-01, EWT-03 Stern describes learning to work with Xie and the counterpart’s authority. CLI06

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Europe negotiated later, with different interests and an inherited U.S. benchmark. EWT-01, EWT-05 These confounders prevent attributing different terms to learned substyles.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Need longitudinal participant-level behavior and training/feedback evidence. Present comparisons establish variation, not its acquisition through repeated interaction.

F14 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 194–195; contrast Solomon 17): By the late 1990s, Chinese negotiators experienced with Americans had learned incremental bargaining and adjusted opening positions to American expectations of compromise between initial demands.

Baseline scope and qualification: Commercial issue mix and bargaining power may explain the difference as well as learning. The chapter offers no transaction-level dataset; this is a hypothesis to investigate, not an established universal transition.

Tested component: Incremental changes are documented; a learned adjustment to American midpoint expectations is not.

Strongest relevant support: November 12 Chinese and November 12–13 American papers change specific parameters conditionally. CN-WTO-B3 Detailed offers, not a dataset of opening anchors.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: European bargaining also adjusted timing and compensating terms while ceilings held. EWT-01, EWT-03

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No before/after record establishes adoption of an American split-the-difference convention. Issue mix, bargaining power and package design remain alternatives; incrementality alone cannot verify Freeman’s mechanism.

Solomon: process and tactics

S-P01 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 57–58, 75–76, 147–151): PRC political negotiations commonly progress from relationship and agenda construction, through extended assessment, to a brief agreement-making phase and subsequent implementation bargaining.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter selects a few major agreements and repeatedly reuses them. The endgame admits abortion or continued bargaining. Cultural and institutional interpretations are not independently established by the regularity of a reconstructed sequence. Do not count repeated references to one episode as independent cases.

Original-text qualification: The table on Solomon p. 58 expressly allows concluding an agreement, reserving a position or aborting negotiation; p. 147 likewise describes alternatives to settlement. Failure to finish is therefore not by itself contrary evidence to his process model.

Tested component: Typical order, overlap and duration of the proposed stages within comparable political negotiations, including failed and suspended efforts.

Strongest relevant support: An announced but incomplete October package became a signed January instrument. TUS12, TUS15 A sequence from incomplete announcement to signature does not measure the preceding stages or establish their typical duration. Implementation generated further senior exchanges. POI01, POI03, POI04

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Different channels moved at different speeds within the same relationship. C23-09, C23-15, C23-17 Different channels within one bilateral relationship are not necessarily stages of the same negotiation. Senior movement did not produce closure in the reconstructed claims sequence. CLA04, CLA05, CLA10 The original model already allows unsuccessful endings; this case helps prevent selection only on signed agreements.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Stage order and typical duration remain unresolved. Define a bargaining object and its entry and exit points before assigning stages; include interrupted, failed and repeated sequences. The model remains a possible descriptive organizer, but neither selected successful chronologies nor failures already admitted by the original decide its empirical regularity.

S-P02 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 59–61, 111–112): PRC officials try to channel important negotiations through sympathetic foreign decision makers with senior access, sometimes bypassing or separating less congenial officials and advisers.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon acknowledges that governments generally prefer friendly counterparts. Presidential access, substantive anti-Soviet alignment, and decision authority are practical explanations alongside guanxi. Physical isolation examples are principally commercial and should not automatically be generalized to summit diplomacy.

Tested component: Preferred interlocutors and senior access versus policy leverage through channel selection.

Strongest relevant support: Kissinger met senior Chinese leaders and a defense minister inaccessible through the sought official contact. K23-01, K23-02, K23-03

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: His U.S. representation was explicitly denied; Xi had already met the serving secretary. K23-04, K23-14

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Observed access is retained. Deliberate exclusion to influence internal U.S. competition and more favorable terms remain unestablished; do not turn retired advisory access into authority.

S-P03 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 61–65, 108–110): Hosting negotiations and managing hospitality, protocol, timing, and uncertainty can give PRC officials influence over visitors’ mood, fatigue, and perceived control.

Baseline scope and qualification: The book gives practical reasons for Beijing venues, including communications and access to decision makers. It records gaffes and visitor suspicion. Kissinger expressly could not distinguish tactical delay from leadership review in July 1971; perceived manipulation is not proof of intent.

Tested component: Ceremonial setting and scheduling as causes of mood, fatigue or perceived control.

Strongest relevant support: The historical Diaoyutai setting was explicitly recognized by host and guest. K23-01

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Aircraft-return steps included material preparation and technical constraints. EP310, EP311, EP312 Time consumed cannot automatically be coded as deliberate exhaustion.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Hospitality’s behavioral effects and intent are not reconstructed. Need contemporaneous visitor reactions connected to changed offers, and comparable venue/schedule controls.

S-P04 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 65–71): PRC negotiators seek advantageous agenda wording and sequencing, and may withhold discussion of other issues to press a priority demand or protect relationships with allies.

Baseline scope and qualification: The final normalization dialogue included the broader security issues Washington sought. Agenda pressure failed to secure normalization under Ford. The chapter also attributes agenda precision to narrow instructions and preparation requirements, not only deliberate manipulation.

Tested component: Observable agenda separation and conditional sequencing, without assuming successful coercion.

Strongest relevant support: Crew departure, a future meeting and aircraft arrangements were distinguished from responsibility and reconnaissance positions. EP303, EP306 Access to particular contacts became a disputed object. C23-06, C23-11, C23-15

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Huawei was explicitly outside the partial trade agreement; agenda pressure did not absorb every issue into one bargain. TUS12

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The practice is documented; who proposed a sequence and whether withholding one topic caused a concession elsewhere often remain unknown. Universal uniqueness is untested.

S-P05 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 71–75, 128, 150–151): PRC political negotiators favor broad principles and flexible political documents, subsequently invoking those formulations to constrain the counterpart while retaining discretion over concrete arrangements.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon concedes agreements apparently inconsistent with early principles and American refusals to accept Chinese formulations. Relationship trust, uncertainty reduction, and tactical flexibility are competing explanations. A unilateral claim that a principle was violated is not proof of a mutually accepted obligation.

Original-text qualification: Solomon p. 151 expressly discusses a preference in political agreements. His earlier discussion allows concrete compromise and refusal or violation of principles. The presence of legal or technical detail does not by itself narrow an unqualified universal claim, because that was not the original proposition.

Tested component: The conjunction of preference for flexible political formulations, later invocation against the counterpart, and preservation of Chinese discretion.

Strongest relevant support: Public Chinese demands invoked principles and earlier assurances. C23-14, C23-16, C23-18 This demonstrates framing and invocation, not shared acceptance of the asserted meaning or a change forced on the counterpart.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: A precise procedural agreement preserved avenues for continued legal dispute. DS379-07 A precise legal procedure can coexist with a preference for broad political understandings in other negotiations. Detailed review-process provisions existed before and within Phase One. AG06, AG07, AG01 Regulatory specificity does not independently test the claimed political preference or asymmetric use of accepted principles.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The full preference-and-constraint mechanism remains unresolved. Principles are invoked, but public readouts do not establish a preferred alternative, original shared meaning, effective constraint or the alleged asymmetry in retained discretion. Recover linked drafts and responses; preserve the original political scope rather than treating it as a new correction.

S-P06 — untested

Baseline claim (printed pp. 75–77): PRC negotiators often seek a comprehensive account of the counterpart’s position before revealing their own, even when the host–guest justification changes with the venue.

Baseline scope and qualification: Footnote 21 says Chinese officials may present first when tasked with establishing a principle or limiting discussion. The behavior is an information-seeking convention, not sufficient evidence of a unique national characteristic or of concessions obtained.

Tested component: Obtaining the other side’s comprehensive opening position before disclosing China’s own, including host/guest rationales.

Strongest relevant support: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No adequate case-level test in the acquired corpus; no evidence is counted as confirmation or refutation.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No later paired transcript sample in this corpus tests speaking order and information gained. Final offers and leader readouts cannot substitute for the first-exchange sequence.

S-P07 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 77–79, 84–87, 89, 121–122): PRC officials sometimes use underspecified formulations, analogies, indirect signals, or apparent misunderstanding to elicit a concrete response while limiting their own immediate commitment.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter sometimes marks these meanings as conjectural. Its playing-dumb subsection lacks a dated illustrative case. Cultural explanations of indirection do not exclude ordinary exploratory bargaining, and both sides can overinterpret symbolism.

Tested component: Intentional underspecification used to elicit a response while retaining discretion.

Strongest relevant support: Chinese public responses give limited detail on commercial gas arrangements. RUS-G18, RUS-G29 Sparse disclosure is only a candidate observation, not direct evidence of projective testing.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The public account explicitly identifies no public office and names topics without operational details. K23-02 Confidentiality, mandate limits and incomplete agreement explain omissions without a tactical probe.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No dated probe–response–clarification chain establishes the proposed mechanism. Final-text precision neither verifies nor refutes indirect exploratory bargaining.

S-P08 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 79–84): Trusted intermediaries and lower-level or informal channels can convey sensitive positions, test ideas, and insulate senior leaders from direct confrontation.

Baseline scope and qualification: Trust and guanxi coexist with secrecy, access, common interests, and availability. Footnote 36 explicitly compares informal exchanges with U.S.–Soviet practice. A reported private message does not establish authorized policy unless its provenance can be verified.

Tested component: Trusted-channel transmission and its effect, distinguished from access and ordinary consultation.

Strongest relevant support: Stern describes specialist trust and confidential preparation of bilateral climate announcements. CLI06 One retrospective participant account.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Public private-status denial, anticipated debrief and acknowledged advice provide no July message-to-instruction chain. K23-04, K23-06, K23-10

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Recover paired messages, permissions and responses. Informality may be useful without being uniquely Chinese or an authorized substitute for official negotiation.

S-P09 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 87–89): Humor, self-deprecation, and acknowledgment of minor errors can build rapport or preempt criticism, making later changes in tone more salient to foreign counterparts.

Baseline scope and qualification: Politeness, sincere error correction, personality, and shared humor are alternatives to calculated influence. The chapter’s comparisons with Soviet humor are subjective and do not independently establish national cultural differences.

Tested component: Humor or self-deprecation producing rapport that changes later expectations or concessions.

Strongest relevant support: Zhu’s public gift/campaign-contribution joke accompanied a substantive argument about Chinese reform interests. US-APR08-PRESS A public joke is the observed component, not a measured effect.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: No independent contrary test has been established; the evidentiary gap below is not proof that the proposed effect is absent.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No linked evidence shows humor altered a counterpart’s expectations or subsequent offer. There is no independent contrary test; absence of that link is not proof humor has no effect.

S-P10 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 90–93, 97–100): PRC leaders use relations with third powers and rival foreign political figures to advertise alternatives, influence the counterpart’s sense of dependence, and improve their bargaining position.

Baseline scope and qualification: The proposed role of the March 1969 clashes in creating a wider coalition is conditional in Solomon’s account. Domestic politics and genuine security interests also explain third-party actions. The Soviet helicopter-release footnote expressly leaves its internal dynamics unresolved.

Tested component: Advertising third-power alternatives to change the counterpart’s perceived dependence.

Strongest relevant support: Analyses identify alternative suppliers and Russia’s changing external outlets. RUS-G09, RUS-G13 Material alternatives do not prove a deliberate message or its reception.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: American diplomacy also introduced summit sequence and the Soviet relationship. NOR-163 A counterpart comparison, not a refutation of Chinese use.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Need a dated Chinese invocation, recipient belief update and altered terms. The corpus does not isolate leverage from real security/economic interests or third-party agency.

S-P11 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 75–76, 93–95): PRC officials may delay substantive movement until leadership conditions or the wider strategic context permit a more favorable agreement, preserving talks in the meantime.

Baseline scope and qualification: Observed slowness does not identify its cause. The chapter relies partly on hindsight and foreign officials’ interpretations of internal politics; apparent rigidity may reflect substantive preference rather than leadership conflict or a calculated delay.

Tested component: Deliberate preservation of talks until leadership or strategic circumstances improve.

Strongest relevant support: Talks and political endorsement continued while commercial closure remained incomplete. RUS-G14, RUS-G17, RUS-G28

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Technical review and a similarly long EU process illustrate alternatives to strategic waiting. AG09, AG16, AG05 A counterpart’s missing authorization also delayed settlement. EWT-01

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Duration and eventual agreement cannot identify intentional delay. Need earlier acceptable offers, instructions to wait and the strategic or leadership change that released them.

S-P12 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 95–96, 148, 150–151): PRC negotiators can obtain partial agreements while explicitly reserving unresolved positions, and can preserve compromise options through wording that avoids a conspicuous reversal.

Baseline scope and qualification: An explicit reservation is not concealment and does not show agreement on the reserved issue. Solomon admits that normalization was not a fully specified plan and contrasts political credibility concerns with commercial retreats. The Sun-tzu explanation is an analogy, not demonstrated transmission of a military rule.

Tested component: Partial agreement with explicitly reserved issues; face-preserving wording treated as a separate unproved component.

Strongest relevant support: Normalization proceeded while U.S. arms sales remained expressly disputed. NOR-170, NOR-172 Crew departure did not settle responsibility or all aircraft arrangements. EP303, EP306 Reserved boundary areas were settled in a later instrument. RUS-T1991-CN, RUS-T2004

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Enforcement was still substantively incomplete after the partial package was announced. TUS12 A partial announcement is not a completed agreement on its own reserved terms.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain demonstrated partial settlement, not an inferred mechanism of saving face or concealed future concessions. Establish each reservation and its subsequent treatment separately.

S-P13 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 104–108): PRC negotiators can increase pressure through explicit threats, limited costly confrontations, or expanded demands, with the form and willingness to act changing as the relationship develops.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon states both that threats were sometimes abandoned and that some were carried out. The 1975 tour failed, and its factional purpose is disputed within the chapter. Neither threat issuance nor a canceled exchange proves bargaining success.

Tested component: Explicit pressure and conditional threats as conduct, not measured success or a national monopoly.

Strongest relevant support: China conditioned negotiated outcomes on the absence of additional U.S. sanctions/tariffs. CN-TRADE-STATEMENT20180603 Defense contact was publicly withheld under political conditions. C23-06, C23-11

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The United States also used and implemented tariff pressure; its deadlines and demands changed. TUS03, TUS04, TUS09

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain the repertoire. Specific Chinese concessions extracted through a threat, calibrated military coercion and relationship-driven changes in threat effectiveness require separate causal evidence.

S-P14 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 100–102): Foreign officials invested in a positive relationship with China may receive concentrated pressure because PRC leaders expect them to work hardest to preserve it.

Baseline scope and qualification: Guanxi is Solomon’s principal cultural explanation, but reputational investment and expected policy influence offer practical alternatives. The chapter also reports PRC weakness in reaching hostile Americans, so concentrated contact may reflect access limitations rather than superior manipulation.

Tested component: Concentrated pressure on friendly officials because of their investment in the relationship.

Strongest relevant support: A sympathetic old interlocutor was urged to contribute to better relations. K23-01, K23-02

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Private status and acknowledged advice do not demonstrate pressured concession or fear of losing status. K23-04, K23-10

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No recovered comparison of friendly and hostile counterparts identifies differential pressure or its effect. Availability and policy affinity remain sufficient alternatives.

S-P15 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 103–104, 106–108, 112): PRC leaders can invoke popular sentiment or the political vulnerability of accommodating officials as a limit on their flexibility and a reason for the counterpart to concede.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon explicitly cannot determine whether some warnings are hollow and compares them with American invocation of Congress. The asserted change in popular influence under Deng is a period-specific interpretation, not a general model of how public opinion controls PRC policy.

Tested component: Invoking public sentiment or an accommodating official’s vulnerability to induce concessions.

Strongest relevant support: The Chinese published accession narrative associates the post-bombing pause with dignity. CN-WTO-G1 Editorial interpretation, not an internal instruction or complete public-opinion record.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: American officials invoked independent congressional authority. US-NOV15-BERGER Actual institutional constraint and tactical invocation can coexist on either side.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Need the specific Chinese appeal, contemporaneous assessment of domestic constraint and foreign response. Neither later internet commentary nor a public dignity claim alone tests efficacy.

S-P16 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 110–111, 139–140, 153–156): Negotiating tone varies with individuals, assigned roles, senior instructions, and domestic circumstances; alternating harder and more accommodating presentations can affect expectations about compromise.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter calls the hard–soft tactic universal and notes that Deng could be abrasive while Huang could be conciliatory. The portraits are American-facing and partly impressionistic. Unresolved Mao–Zhou politics limit certainty about who directed every presentation.

Tested component: Variation in tone across individuals, instructions and roles, and its effect on expectations.

Strongest relevant support: Conrad’s interviews describe a shift from climate specialists to the premier’s foreign-policy team. CLI05 Retrospective explanatory account without released mandates.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Warm personal presentation coexisted with firm substantive demands. K23-02

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No sufficiently paired same-person or assigned-role comparison establishes orchestrated hard–soft tactics or a resulting concession. Tone and proposal changes must be coded separately.

S-P17 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 113–122): PRC officials use public communication around negotiations to establish agendas, raise or reduce expectations, advertise limits, and suggest openings, with different outlets offering different degrees of commitment.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon documents learning about television, failed expectation-raising, and respect for some confidentiality requests. The historical media hierarchy is not automatically valid later. His suggestion that a Chinese press criticism influenced the Mayaguez response is conjectural and needs independent causal evidence.

Tested component: Public communication used to characterize the agenda, prior obligations and permissible movement.

Strongest relevant support: White papers and briefing defended different stages of the negotiation. CN-TRADE-WP2018, CN-TRADE-WP2019, CN-TRADE-SCIO20191213 English and Chinese presentations supported immediate crew return while responsibility remained disputed. EP303, EP304, EP305

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The U.S. publication dispute shows counterpart publicity and disputed effects too. US-APR08-BRIEFING

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain observable public positioning. A contemporary outlet hierarchy, internal dissemination and causal effects on public pressure need direct evidence rather than projection of Solomon’s older media categories.

S-P18 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 122–125): Rapid collection and internal circulation of foreign reporting can inform PRC bargaining while also generating domestic reactions that constrain negotiators or intensify later responses.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon says the selection procedures for internal publications were unknown. Some feedback mechanisms rely on private explanations or chronological inference. Abundant access to information does not prove unfiltered reporting to leaders or correct interpretation of a foreign political system.

Tested component: Internal circulation and selection of foreign reporting causing a changed bargaining response.

Strongest relevant support: Jost’s institutional account offers an information-flow mechanism. EP319 Selected scholarship and source-chain limits do not replace Chinese circulation records.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: American assessments of Xi’s possible ignorance are not direct Chinese evidence. C23-20, C23-21 Later reports partly repeat the same assessment chain.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: No acquired Chinese internal report and subsequent decision establish the feedback loop. Information availability, accuracy, transmission and political constraint remain separate variables.

S-P19 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 105–106, 125–129, 140–143): PRC negotiators preserve and invoke the prior record, including protests and predecessors’ words, but may restate disputed commitments in forms more favorable to their current position.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter’s translation and distortion allegations require checking against originals. Cultural claims about written Chinese do not independently explain document management. Table 2 is a set of contextual signaling hypotheses, not an automatic dictionary of intent. Solomon also acknowledges mutual overreading of symbols.

Tested component: Invocation of prior commitments; favorable restatement and distortion remain separate tests.

Strongest relevant support: Liu invoked an Argentina numerical understanding. CN-TRADE-LIU201905 Both sides used prior understandings while emphasizing different relationship obligations. IND-MEA-6534, IND-CN-2022-SUN, IND-MEA-35739

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: The U.S. dinner readout left purchases unquantified. TUS05 Records distinguish common principles from unilateral positions and non-endorsement. NOR-168, NOR-172

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain invocation as interactive conduct. A discrepancy is not automatically strategic distortion; recover original agreed wording, reservations, translations and subsequent claims.

S-P20 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 130–131, 134–135): Statements of independence, indifference, or foreign indebtedness can portray the counterpart as the party needing agreement and responsible for repairing the relationship.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon interprets these claims through the relationship game and anxiety about dependence, but real asymmetries or genuine performance disputes may also explain them. Analyst disbelief is not a reliable measure of the speaker’s actual valuation.

Tested component: Publicly assigning repair responsibility and portraying Chinese cooperation as conditional on American conduct.

Strongest relevant support: Chinese officials called for corrective U.S. action and appropriate conditions for contact. C23-06, C23-11, C23-14

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Chinese leaders also requested constructive advocacy and mutual improvement. K23-01, K23-02 These are compatible with valuing the relationship, not proof of indifference.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain the public rhetorical practice. Actual private valuation, anxiety about dependence and whether the portrayal extracted concessions are unresolved.

S-P21 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 131–134, 138–140): PRC officials use challenges to the counterpart’s resolve, logic, motives, and policy consistency to contest its rationale and encourage behavior favorable to Chinese interests.

Baseline scope and qualification: Kissinger sometimes rejected the premise explicitly, and Deng’s debunking could resist perceived American pressure. The chapter’s linkage to dialectical reasoning is interpretive; similar forms of persuasion are internationally familiar and may express sincere substantive disagreement.

Tested component: Challenges to policy consistency and motives as visible argumentation.

Strongest relevant support: China accused Washington of changing positions and making unacceptable demands. CN-TRADE-WP2018, CN-TRADE-WP2019 Wang challenged the strategic logic of containing China. K23-02

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Blinken maintained U.S. national-security restrictions and Taiwan policy despite Chinese demands. C23-15

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The persuasion attempt is observed, not its success or derivation from distinctive dialectical reasoning. Sincere disagreement and familiar bargaining argument remain alternatives.

S-P22 — retain within tested scope

Baseline claim (printed pp. 135–137): Openly specifying differences can coexist with limited cooperation, reassuring domestic and allied audiences while clarifying the common interests that sustain an agreement.

Baseline scope and qualification: Solomon distinguishes the domestic and strategic conditions of different episodes and credits compromise by both parties. The explanation does not imply that all harsh rhetoric is compatible with agreement or that disagreement is merely theatrical.

Tested component: Explicit disagreement coexisting with a specified limited bargain.

Strongest relevant support: Both governments accepted normalization with the arms-sales disagreement preserved. NOR-170, NOR-172 Shared dialogue mechanisms coexisted with Taiwan and technology disputes. C23-17, C23-18 Different national responsibilities and common procedures coexist. CLI15, CLI17

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Immediate practical resolution did not settle responsibility or future reconnaissance. EP303, EP306 Limited cooperation does not imply the remaining disagreement is theatrical or harmless.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Retain coexistence. Domestic/allied reassurance as the cause of explicit disagreement and the conditions under which rhetoric prevents agreement remain unmeasured.

S-P23 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 143–147): Control over the schedule and apparent willingness to wait can help PRC negotiators exploit the counterpart’s deadline, but Chinese negotiators are also susceptible to time pressure.

Baseline scope and qualification: Kissinger described both sides using the 1972 deadline. The United States declined an unfavorable deal in February 1982, and Solomon recognizes initial British time leverage over Hong Kong. Cultural reserve does not establish an inherent Chinese advantage in patience.

Tested component: Chinese schedule control producing favorable terms, with reciprocal vulnerability retained.

Strongest relevant support: Long-running talks persisted despite Russian near-completion claims. RUS-G14, RUS-G17, RUS-G28 Optimistic targets are not proven binding deadlines or evidence of exploitation.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: U.S. departure letters actively managed the deadline. CN-WTO-B3 Washington controlled and extended tariff dates. TUS09

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Direct evidence supports reciprocal timing tactics more than a measured Chinese patience advantage. Need private deadlines, alternative offers and a concession sequence showing their effect.

S-P24 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 147–151): A shift toward concrete drafting and intervention by a senior PRC leader can signal that agreement is authorized and near, even after apparent deadlock.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter’s face-based explanation is not independently established, and selected successful cases cannot determine reliability. Senior authority does not eliminate bureaucratic reversal: the chapter cites repudiation of the 1973 claims/assets solution. Several local date inconsistencies require original-source checking.

Original-text qualification: The unchanged S-P24 extraction says “can signal,” but this softens the printed claim. Solomon p. 149 calls senior intervention “the most reliable sign” of imminent agreement and invokes leaders’ reluctance to risk face; p. 150 asserts a highly consistent endgame. The failed claims/assets example on p. 148 creates a tension with that prediction. The current verdict evaluates this stronger original claim, in its concrete-drafting context, rather than silently substituting a weaker one.

Tested component: The original ranking of senior intervention as the most reliable sign of approaching agreement, considered with concrete drafting and the proposed face mechanism.

Strongest relevant support: Concrete text and Deng’s intervention preceded successful normalization. NOR-159, NOR-166, NOR-170 A Russian authorization attached drafts and bounded discretion immediately before signature. RUS-AUTH2004, RUS-T2004 This is a counterpart authority record, not Chinese mandate evidence or proof outsiders had it prospectively.

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Zhou’s movement did not close the claims bargain and the monetary offer was later withdrawn. CLA04, CLA05, CLA10 Solomon already acknowledged this failure; the new reconstruction sharpens its unfinished terms rather than discovering failure as a new possibility. Zhu’s March meeting did not close accession talks. EWT-01 This defeats a deterministic shortcut from a senior meeting to closure, but does not rank the predictive value of all available signals. Xi received Blinken without military-channel agreement. C23-15, C23-16 The record does not show the full concrete-drafting condition for the military-channel request or a promise by Xi that it would be settled.

Assessment and unresolved remainder: The comparative ranking and frequency remain unresolved: selected successes and failures cannot establish whether senior intervention is the most reliable available sign. As practical advice, identify what a leader authorizes, confirms or leaves unsettled instead of equating contact with imminent closure. That revision in use is distinct from empirical refutation. Missing later drafts prevent a full test of the conjunction, and the face mechanism is unproved.

S-P25 — unresolved

Baseline claim (printed pp. 151–153): Implementation can remain a bargaining arena in which PRC officials demand adherence from the counterpart while seeking adjustment, accommodation, or new concessions themselves.

Baseline scope and qualification: The chapter mixes formal agreements with contemplated visits and expressions of intent; these are not equivalent commitments. Deng’s May 1978 acknowledgment that a new administration could reconsider qualifies the claim of rigid continuity. Commercial complaints require independent evidence before generalization to political agreements.

Tested component: Two components kept distinct: implementation bargaining, and asymmetric Chinese demands for adherence while seeking accommodation itself.

Strongest relevant support: China defended fulfillment, pressed U.S. obligations and restrictions, and carried issues into later bargains. POI03, POI04, POI07, POI14, POI17 Closest evidence of reciprocal demands; private reservations and formal appeals are incomplete. The parties negotiated procedures for further implementation disagreement. DS379-07

Strongest contrary or limiting evidence: Negotiated access produced a regulatory acceleration and an operating U.S.-owned firm. FIN-15, FIN-26, FIN-28 Consensual arrangements made boundary implementation workable. RUS-USE1999, RUS-MEMO2004 Later aircraft work concerned issues expressly deferred, not necessarily reopened obligations. EP303, EP306

Assessment and unresolved remainder: Continuing bargaining is strongly observed, but that alone cannot retain the full proposition. Need obligation-specific Chinese demands, its own requested departures, comparable counterpart conduct and agreed exceptions to establish asymmetry rather than ordinary implementation, mutual adjustment or disputed compliance.

Appendix 2. Selective chronology: authority, agreement and performance

Dates identify the stage supported by the evidence: an encounter, an offer, signature, publication, legal effect or reported implementation. Related stages sometimes share a row, with their dates stated separately. Rows are ordered by the beginning of the selected sequence, so longer intervals overlap. An official or corporate account is identified as such where it establishes reported performance rather than independent verification. “Not established” means that the cited record does not establish the next step; it is not proof that nothing happened.

Chapter notes contain the full citations and reading limitations. Source IDs refer to the registers linked below; FRUS and WTO document numbers are also stable source identifiers. A document’s later publication or archival release is not the date on which the underlying bargaining occurred.

No. Date and stage Consequential transition and its limit Chapter and source locator
1 February 17, 1973: senior discussion; agreement in principle described in a later American review Zhou Enlai and Henry Kissinger favor broad political treatment of private claims and blocked assets. The mutual-assignment understanding still leaves the money, legal definitions and payment mechanics to be resolved. 1, note 15; FRUS XVIII, document 10, pp. 111–112; XIII, document 29, “Background.”
2 November 12–13, 1973: offers and legal reservations; November 19: report to Nixon Zhou proposes restoring $17 million, favoring direct payment to Washington. Kissinger remains subject to legal consultation; his subsequent report describes continued work toward settlement. Senior movement is observable without a completed instrument. 1, notes 16–17; FRUS XVIII, documents 57, 59 and 62.
3 May 17, 1977: Vance memorandum; May 25: Brzezinski covering memorandum and recorded presidential approval The American review reports that the earlier monetary offer had been withdrawn and recommends deferring further approaches until Washington settles its normalization strategy. Carter approves deferral. The underlying Chinese withdrawal instruction remains unacquired. 1, notes 18 and 21; FRUS XIII, document 29.
4 December 4–15, 1978: closing exchanges; December 15, Washington: announcement; January 1, 1979: diplomatic relations take effect; March 1: scheduled embassies and ambassadors Normalization proceeds while China’s objection to future American arms sales remains explicit. The common communiqué and the parties’ separate positions are not interchangeable. Diplomatic establishment and the later representation timetable are distinct commitments. 1, notes 7–14; FRUS XIII, documents 159, 166–172; paired official communiqué.
5 May 16, 1989: Deng–Gorbachev conversation; May 18: joint communiqué Normalization of the Sino-Soviet relationship accommodates conflicting historical accounts. The communiqué envisages simultaneous settlement of eastern and western border sectors. The published conversation accounts are a Chinese selection and a Russian memoir-derived text, not two complete contemporary transcripts. 7, note 18; Deng, “结束过去，开辟未来”; border case, 1989 communiqué, paragraph 7.
6 May 16, 1991: eastern border agreement signed The border package becomes divisible: the eastern agreement precedes the western settlement and reserves two sections. A joint commission, channel criteria, demarcation duties and navigation provisions make much of the agreement technically actionable before all territory is settled. 7, note 19; RUS-T1991-CN, Articles III–IX.
7 February 26, 1995: IP bargain reached; March 2: enforcement testimony; March 11: formal signature; May 1996: renewed American action The earlier agreement already specifies domestic enforcement, petitions, information and consultations. Washington rescinds its scheduled tariff action while retaining monitoring and possible renewed Section 301 action. Later American allegations and measures do not independently establish every implementation failure. 5, note ip-before; IPPRE-01, 03–06 in the 1995–96 register.
8 March 30 and April 8–10, 1999: public positions, memorandum and joint statement Zhu Rongji’s American visit produces extensive WTO-related offers but no completed market-access bargain. The signed April 10 memorandum and joint statement must be distinguished from the disputed earlier American public account of concessions. 2; MOFCOM 双边卷③, pp. 191–193; April 8 White House conference and briefing.
9 November 12–15, 1999: exchanged closing papers, then signed bilateral agreement Chinese and American sectoral offers converge into the November 15 market-access agreement. The signed bodies refer to four annexes and incorporation into multilateral accession work. The agreement is neither immediate WTO membership nor a recovered complete set of annex terms. 2; 双边卷③, pp. 1067–1096; WTO Press/148; WTO manuscript follow-up register.
10 December 9, 1999: joint economic-use agreement signed China and Russia separate agreed sovereignty from specified traditional uses by established residents of the other country. Permissions, eligible users, duration and consultations are regulated. The recovered official Annex 2 names issuing authorities and sets request/issuance deadlines while preserving individual refusal power. Annex 1 and issued permits remain unacquired. 7, notes 23–24 and B-new1; RUS-USE1999; BDR-01/07, body and Annex 2.
11 May 17–19, 2000: final EU–China talks; May 19: agreement The Chinese published history records Lamy seeking authoritative confirmation of the 49 percent mobile-telecommunications ceiling, then accepting it with compensating terms.
