Zhu’s confirmation and the afternoon agreement are dated; an independent record of when Brussels changed Lamy’s mandate is absent. 2; EWT-01, 综合卷①, pp. 383–385; EWT-02, explanatory memorandum.
12 April 1, 2001: EP-3 incident; April 11: letter delivered and departure authorized Beijing accepts Ambassador Prueher’s letter and authorizes the 24 American crew members to leave. The wording permits different public interpretations while preserving disputes about responsibility and legality. Authorization and expected departure are not themselves evidence of the completed journey. 4, notes 1–6; EP301–307, especially the full delivered letter EP303 and acceptance announcement EP305.
13 April 18–July 3, 2001: aircraft negotiations and removal statements Crew release does not settle disposal of the aircraft. April talks, inspection permission on April 29, agreement in principle on May 29 and later removal arrangements lead to China’s July 3 statement that removal is complete. Each concerns a different operational stage. 4, notes 9–10; EP308–312.
14 October 1, 2001: services schedule circulated; November 10: Ministerial Conference approves accession package; December 11: China becomes a WTO member Multilateral approval and membership follow the bilateral market-access bargains. The schedule specifies phase-in terms and exceptions, but its final terms cannot by themselves establish the magnitude of concessions in an earlier round. Approval, circulation and membership are independently dated. 2; EWT-03; WTO accession timeline, CHRON-01; Press/252, CHRON-02.
15 October 13, 2004: Russian authorization; October 14: supplementary border agreement signed; June 2, 2005: entry into force A Russian presidential order specifies the drafts and limits of delegated discretion. The following day’s treaty settles remaining alignments; legal effectiveness follows ratification. The recovered mandate is Russian evidence and does not disclose China’s parallel authorization process. 7, notes 26–29; RUS-AUTH2004; RUS-T2004; ratification law 52-FZ.
16 October 14, 2004: local-use renewal signed and effective; March 1, 2005: extension period begins A separate protocol prolongs the 1999 joint-use arrangement for five years from the existing activity endpoint. It preserves a temporary accommodation; it does not establish numbers of issued permits or present continuation. 7, note B-new2; BDR-03, preamble and Articles I–III.
17 July 21, 2008: descriptive protocol signed; October 14: exchange of notes and handover China and Russia move from an agreed boundary to supplementary demarcation instruments and material transfer. The 2008 protocol is distinct from the 2004 agreement; underlying annexes and every field marker have not been independently inspected. 7, note 29; official July signing and October handover accounts in the Russia border register.
18 September 2008: Chinese request for WTO consultations DS379 opens a legal challenge to American anti-dumping and countervailing duties. China can contest public-body determinations and double remedies under an institutional procedure; filing does not prejudge the tribunal’s conclusions or later American compliance. 3; DS379-01–03; WT/DS379/R, paragraphs 1.1–1.7.
19 July 28, 2009: climate memorandum publicly released/initialed; November 5 and 16: formal signatures recorded The earlier arrangement already provides recurring policy consultations and ministry involvement. The later 2013 group specifies a continuing relationship rather than creating contact between summits from nothing. 3, note 19; CM01–02 in the machinery supplement.
20 December 18–19, 2009: Copenhagen closing negotiations and conference disposition; March 30, 2010: cited UNFCCC compilation published The accord distinguishes domestic review, international consultation and analysis, and scrutiny of internationally supported actions. The conference takes note of the accord rather than adopting it by consensus. A leaders’ package does not supply every participant’s assent. 3; CLI01–05; CP01–03 in the participant supplement; Decision 2/CP.15 and accord paragraph 5.
21 2010 Cancun / 2011 Durban: conference decisions; March 15, 2011 / March 15, 2012: cited compilations published Cancun specifies bounded consultation and analysis; Durban mandates work toward an outcome with legal force applicable to all. These institutional steps precede Xi’s leadership. Their occurrence does not establish the private cause of Chinese acceptance. 3; CLI07–08; Decisions 1/CP.16 and 1/CP.17.
22 March 11, 2011: Appellate Body report circulated; March 25: reports adopted DS379 produces qualified Chinese victories on public bodies and double remedies, alongside an upheld bank determination and other limitations. Legal success is issue-specific; adoption does not itself establish changed duties at the border. 3; DS379-04–05; WT/DS379/AB/R.
23 May 11, 2012: procedural understanding signed; May 16: circulated Yi Xiaozhun and Michael Punke agree how possible compliance review and later retaliation requests will proceed, preserving specified rights on both sides. Agreement on procedure manages continuing substantive disagreement. The published WTO text records signing without reproducing handwritten signatures. 3; DS379-07, WT/DS379/14, paragraphs 1–12.
24 August 21, 2012: implementation effective; August 30: notice published; October 30: correction published Commerce changes determinations following DS379. Rates and cash-deposit consequences vary; later administrative reviews and entry-date rules matter. China subsequently contests full compliance. The correction addresses an omitted combination, not an adjudicated settlement of China’s objections. 3; DS379-08–09; 77 FR 52683–52688 and 65672–65673.
25 April 13 / July 10, 2013: named climate working group and approved work program Xie and Stern receive a defined assignment; sectoral plans, agency responsibilities, at least twice-yearly meetings and annual reporting specify work within pre-existing consultation. These provisions do not reveal a change in concession latitude. 3, note 19; CM04–06.
26 September 5, 2013: contemporary report of eastern gas principal terms Gazprom and CNPC agree principal terms in the leaders’ presence while reserving price. The reported binding instrument and hoped-for year-end contract are not the same as an agreed sales price or a completed supply contract. 7, note 31; RUS-G01.
27 May 21, 2014: eastern gas contract announced; May 22: Chinese-hosted report published The parties announce a thirty-year contract and a build-up to 38 billion cubic metres annually. Putin describes mutual compromise and corrects his account of the oil-linked pricing mechanism. The contract formula remains unpublished; the headline valuation is not the actual price. 7, notes 32–35; RUS-G03–08.
28 November 12, 2014, Beijing: joint climate announcement; November 11, Washington: American posting The governments jointly endorse different national mitigation intentions. The shared announcement supplies political support for Paris but neither makes the targets identical nor resolves the subsequent multilateral negotiation. 3; CLI12; Joint Announcement, paragraphs 2–4.
29 December 12, 2015: Paris text adopted The agreement combines nationally determined contributions with binding preparation, communication and related procedural duties, differentiated obligations and capacity-related flexibility. Adoption is distinct from later consent to be bound, entry into force and achievement of national outcomes; this case ends at adoption. 3; CLI15–17; Paris Articles 4, 9, 13 and 15.
30 March 23 / 30, 2016: redetermination and domestic court judgment; April 11: effective date; April 21: notice Commerce withdraws the 2012 laminated-woven-sacks anti-dumping adjustment following domestic litigation. Revised deposit instructions retain the intervening-review qualification. This is not a WTO compliance ruling or an estimate of actual duties paid. 3; DEV-DS04–05; 81 FR 23457–23459; CIT Slip Op. 16-30.
31 November 10, 2017: financial-opening announcement Zhu Guangyao announces a prospective 51 percent ceiling for specified financial businesses, followed by removal of the ceiling. The opening predates Phase One and applies beyond American investors; its summit setting also prevents treating it as wholly insulated from bilateral bargaining. 6; FIN-01, FIN-03.
32 March 21, 2018: reform plan published; May 15: first foreign-affairs commission meeting Coordination and supervision are consolidated, with specified maritime-rights duties transferred into the commission and its office. Published responsibilities and adoption of working rules do not disclose the instructions for the later trade draft. 5, note authority-2018; AR-01–02 in the authority register.
33 May 19–June 15, 2018: joint framework, described pause, conditional Chinese outcomes and American tariff decision The May joint statement, American descriptions of a pause, China’s June 3 condition and subsequent tariffs are different acts. The sequence establishes a failed accommodation without proving that every element belonged to one fully documented, unconditional agreement. 5; May 19 joint statement; Mnuchin and Lighthizer accounts; June 3 Chinese statement; June 15 White House decision.
34 December 20, 2018: Enlist E3 certificate validity begins; January 8, 2019: approval list published; February 21: US launch announced after Philippine permission Chinese import permission precedes the trade-talk week in which it becomes public. Another destination’s decision remains a launch condition. Certificate validity, publication and commercial launch therefore cannot be assigned a single causal date. 6; AG03, AG14–15.
35 February 22 and 27, 2019: public drafting dispute and enforcement testimony Trump and Lighthizer disagree over the name and status of the document under negotiation; Liu accepts the proposed change in name. Lighthizer later explains a contemplated complaint process, including corporate anonymity. These statements do not establish the contents or cause of the missing May draft revision. 5, notes 25–26; DEV-TR01–02.
36 March 13, 2019: securities establishment decision; March 29: receipt; August 22: company registration; December 18: operating permit JPMorgan’s majority-owned securities company passes through separate Chinese administrative stages. The operating permit precedes Phase One signature. Authorization to establish a firm is not permission to conduct all the contemplated business immediately. 6; FIN-07, FIN-19, printed pp. 2–3.
37 October 11, 2019: Phase One progress announced Trump and Liu He present approaching agreement while Lighthizer says consequences of unresolved disputes still require work. The ceremony documents progress and remaining bargaining; it is not an already completed enforcement bargain. 5; October 11 Oval Office transcript.
38 December 2, 2019: first eastern-pipeline supply, as subsequently recorded by Gazprom Power of Siberia moves into operation. This actual start is distinct from the previously announced December 20 target. Later company-reported throughput concerns delivered gas, rather than prospective capacity or an independent audit of the whole contract. 7, note 36; RUS-G11–12; later operational check RUS-G26.
39 January 15, 2020: Phase One signed; February 14: agreement and specified tariff reduction take effect Detailed structural, purchase and dispute-procedure provisions become operative while substantial tariffs remain. The bilateral process lacks an independent adjudicator. The final instrument supplies obligations, not evidence that each was subsequently performed. 5; agreement Articles 6.2, 7 and 8.2–8.3; January 22 tariff notice.
40 March 13, 2020: revised securities timetable confirmed; March 20: JPMorgan launch; April 1: ownership ceiling removed The regulator links its eight-month acceleration to Phase One. The firm’s commercial launch and the date on which full foreign ownership becomes legally available are separate. Neither date alone establishes a completed ownership transfer. 6; FIN-11–12, FIN-15, FIN-17–19.
41 September 10 and 21, 2020: political and military border understandings India and China direct disengagement and respect for earlier agreements, then specify restraints on further frontline deployment and unilateral changes. These common directions do not publish a complete withdrawal map or settle all locations. India case; IND-MEA-32962 and IND-MEA-33047.
42 August 2021: ownership filing accepted; August 25: shareholder registration completed JPMorgan becomes sole owner of its Chinese securities company. Published fee accounts subsequently establish usable business activity, while the ownership change cannot by itself explain the growth of revenue. 6; FIN-24–28, especially 2021 disclosure printed pp. 2–3 and income-statement p. 6.
43 End of 2021: quantified two-year purchase period closes; 2022: cited PIIE assessment; 2025: separate USTR benchmark Bown estimates covered goods and services purchases at $290.8 billion against $502.4 billion, or 58 percent. USTR later uses a commitment exceeding $535 billion and shortfall exceeding $217 billion. Both imply substantial underperformance; their definitions are not silently merged. 6; POI08–11; agreement Article 6.2 and Annex 6.1.
44 February 4, 2022: Far Eastern gas contract announced Gazprom reports another long-term supply agreement. The same release’s account of a completed Mongolia-route feasibility study is not a second sales contract for that separate project. 7, notes 38–39; RUS-G12–13.
45 August 5, 2022: Chinese countermeasures announced Beijing announces cancellation or suspension of specified military and other contacts following Pelosi’s Taiwan visit. This precedes the balloon crisis: subsequent accounts of “restoring communication” must identify which already-suspended mechanism they mean. 4, note 11; C23-01, all eight listed countermeasures.
46 September 8, 2022, 08:30: PP15 disengagement begins, according to India; September 12: stipulated completion; October 14: later completion account A local border bargain specifies coordinated, phased withdrawal, dismantling and verification. The deadline is distinguished from the later official report of completion. Local disengagement does not resolve the wider dispute over de-escalation or normalization. 7, notes 8–10; IND-MEA-35691/35808; IND-PIB-PP15; IND-CN-2022-PP15.
47 January 5, 2023: DP73496 import certificate validity begins; January 13: list published; February 14: commercial launch announced China’s canola permission follows a reported July 2012 application. The same event’s EU authorization came in March 2022. Chinese delay is consequential, but the full preceding decade cannot be assigned to China as the sole global launch obstacle. 6; AG05, AG13, AG16–19.
48 February 3–9, 2023: balloon crisis and refusal of defense contact China expresses regret and advances its force-majeure account; Blinken postpones his visit before the February 4 shootdown. Beijing condemns the use of force and subsequently explains refusal of the proposed defense call. Public positions do not establish Xi’s private advance knowledge. 4, notes 12–18; C23-02–08, C23-20–22.
49 May 10–11, 2023: Vienna strategic talks; May 22–31: sanctions dispute and defense-meeting refusal An active Wang–Sullivan channel coexists with refusal of the proposed Austin–Li meeting. Beijing calls for sanctions relief to create conditions; Washington retains sanctions and says official meetings remain possible. The public positions do not define a guaranteed exchange of delisting for contact. 4, notes 19–20 and sanctions-may; C23-09–12 and CAD02/08/11.
50 June 18–19, 2023: Beijing foreign-ministry and leader meetings Blinken’s visit resumes high-level diplomatic engagement, following the May strategic-channel meetings. The parties do not announce restoration of military channels. A reopened diplomatic channel is therefore not a comprehensive settlement of the crisis or the relationship. 4, notes 19–22; C23-09–16.
51 June 28, 2023: Foreign Relations Law adopted; July 1: effective; December 2023 / April 2024: central and local conferences The law states distinct responsibilities for central direction, military exchanges, diplomatic coordination and authorized provincial activity. Later instructions couple discipline and initiative; neither text measures concession latitude. Conclusion, note authority-now; AR-03–05.
52 July 4, 2023: China–EU appeal procedure notified; July 21, 2025: DS611 award; September 2025–April 2026: implementation statements Agreed arbitration provides a final appeal award despite ordinary WTO appellate vacancies. China reports domestic steps while criticizing the reasoning; the EU continues to assess compliance. This consent is case-specific and does not supply a China–US arbitration agreement. 3, notes appeal-1–3; APP-01–08 in the current-appeal register.
53 July 18–20, 2023: Kissinger’s Beijing meetings The private visitor gains access to Li Shangfu, Wang Yi and Xi Jinping. The public record establishes advocacy and reception; it does not recover an authorized American negotiating message, a completed contemporaneous debrief or a policy concession caused by the visit. 4, “What an old friend could do”; K23-01–06 and K23-09–10; intermediary audit.
54 August 31, 2023: MOD names sanctions among obstacles; October 24: Li removed; October 26: working-level exchanges announced The dismissal leaves the ministry vacant; proposed lower-level contact coexists with wider Chinese conditions. The act does not establish why broader communications were subsequently authorized. 4, notes sanctions-august and vacant-ministry; CAD03–04/06/09.
55 November 15, 2023: Woodside agreements announced; November 16: separate forensic-institute delisting effective; November 30: military implementation coordination described The summit names four military communication mechanisms. The US delisting is connected by a named official to counternarcotics cooperation, not demonstrated as the price of military reopening. Subsequent coordination is a further stage, without an unconditional guarantee of future channel availability. 4, notes 27–29; C23-17–19, C23-24–25.
56 October 21, 2024: Indian patrolling-agreement announcement; October 23: leaders endorse; December 3: Indian parliamentary implementation account The local arrangement precedes the Modi–Xi summit. India’s later statement describes restored activity in specified areas and treats de-escalation as the next task. Endorsement, reported implementation and a complete military return to an earlier baseline are different claims. 7, notes 1 and 15; IND-MEA-38441/38457/38665; IND-CN-2024-KAZAN; fuller paired record in the India case.
57 April 4, 2025: specified rare-earth export controls take effect A State-Council-approved notice requires licenses and permits customs holds during classification questions. It is neither a blanket rare-earth ban nor a measure confined on its face to the United States. 6, notes 37–38; EC25-01, EC25-14.
58 May 12, 2025: Geneva statement; May 14: deadline for specified actions Tariff de-escalation accompanies a Chinese undertaking to suspend or remove post-April 2 non-tariff countermeasures against the United States. The published statement does not name the April 4 notice or give a separate clock for each license. 6, notes 39–40; EC25-02, EC25-25.
59 June 5, 2025: leaders’ call; June 9–10: London framework in principle; June 27: further confirmation after approval Continued talks connect lawful approval of eligible Chinese export applications with corresponding US removal of restrictions. The public account records approval but does not identify the approving body or release the detailed framework. 6, notes 41–43; EC25-05–06, EC25-10, EC25-15–17.
60 July 2–3, 2025: software firms report US relief; July 10: Chinese account of a European expedited channel Synopsys reports rescission and restoration under way; Siemens reports resumed access, sales and support for specified classifications. Chinese permissions are also administered through a separate European relationship. 6, notes 36 and 44; EC25-07–08, EC25-18, EC25-24.
61 July 2025: magnet shipments; August 20: published customs-data report Reuters reports 5,577 metric tons exported globally, 619 to the United States. Recovered flows do not establish clearance of all controlled items or end users. 6, note 45; EC25-11.
62 August 19, 2025: new border work commissioned; May 27, 2026: WMCC meeting; May 28: paired accounts published India and China move into discussion of de-escalation principles and modalities while commissioning delimitation and management work. The 2026 statements report continued technical activity; they do not establish completed delimitation or a formally amended 2012 mechanism. 7, notes 16–17; IND-MEA-40016/41241; IND-CN-2025-SR24; paired May 2026 account.
63 September 2, 2025: Power of Siberia 2 memorandum announced Miller describes a legally binding construction memorandum with prospective capacity of 50 billion cubic metres annually. The full text is unrecovered; capacity, enforceable construction duties and minimum purchase obligations must remain distinct. 7, note 42; RUS-G17.
64 October 24, 2025: Phase One Section 301 investigation initiated; October 28: notice published; December 16: hearing Washington opens a domestic enforcement process and requests Trade Act consultations. Industry testimony combines dissatisfaction with concern that new pressure might jeopardize purchases. This is not automatically a Chapter 7 appeal or a final determination. 6; POI11–12; September 15, 2026 index snapshot CH6-02.
65 May 2026: new boards and purchases announced; June 2: American consultation launched; July 10 / 27: comment / response deadlines; July 23: Chinese design-stage account US and Chinese statements describe a new trade mechanism, but give different purchase formulations. The July Chinese account reports consultations with domestic and American-invested constituencies and continued discussion of structure and products. Announcement, consultation, agreed tariff schedules and actual trade are separate stages. 6; POI15–18, POI21–22; CH6-01, complete July 23 relevant Q&A.
66 September 3–4, 2026: Russian statements on unresolved gas terms Novak says commercial terms and contract preparation continue, with final routing dependent on Gazprom–CNPC agreement. This supplies affirmative evidence of unfinished work after the 2025 memorandum. It does not negate the separate eastern pipeline’s operating record. 7, notes 43–44; RUS-G32–34.

Coverage and source key

The chronology’s latest date is not a uniform endpoint for the manuscript. The normalization check concerns December 1978; the claims reconstruction ends at 1977 deferral. The DS379 implementation examination runs through October 2012, with a bounded coda on the sacks domestic proceeding through its April 21, 2016 notice. The climate case ends at December 2015 text adoption. The 2023 crisis case ends with the November agreements and implementation statements. Financial-company evidence is bounded by the examined 2022 accounts and selected January 2023 decision. Biotechnology uses specified certificates and later procedural assessments rather than a census of every application. The export-control reconstruction covers April–July 2025, with July shipment reporting in August; it does not describe all later control changes. The India mechanism check ends May 27–28, 2026; the Board of Trade design account ends July 23; the gas case reaches September 3–4. The September 15 USTR page capture dates retrieval and observed index contents, not a new bilateral agreement.

Source families:

  • FRUS / normalization and claims: normalization register, claims/assets register.
  • WTO accession / EWT / CHRON: American sources, European comparison, manuscript follow-up. CHRON-01–02 are recorded in the Chapter 6 and chronology supplement. Chinese documentary volumes and exact printed/PDF pages are identified in Chapter 2’s notes.
  • DS379 / DEV-DS / CLI: WTO dispute register, WTO developmental supplement, climate register.
  • EP3 / C23 / K23: EP-3 case and source apparatus, 2023 crisis register, Kissinger register.
  • Trade / DEV-TR / FIN / AG / POI / CH6 / EC25: trade-process supplement, financial access, agricultural approvals, Phase One implementation, Chapter 6 supplement, 2025 export-control register. Chapter 5’s notes identify the trade-negotiation instruments used in rows 28, 30, 32 and 34.
  • IND / RUS: India border register, Russia border register, Russia gas register.

The table does not rank the cases by success. It makes the object of each claimed success or failure explicit: a changed offer, a signed instrument, a legal entitlement, an administrative permission, a reported withdrawal, an operating business, or a still-unfinished transaction. The conclusion draws the comparative implications; it does not extend every case’s endpoint to the final date in this table.

Appendix 3. Bibliography and source guide

15 September 2026. This guide consolidates the works and collections central to the manuscript and its supporting cases. Chapter notes identify individual documents and consequential passages; the registers below provide preserved versions, source IDs, locators and reading limits. It is a bibliography of material used, not the larger list of works identified for possible acquisition.

Complete means the specified work or version was read, including its notes; it does not mean every source in its bibliography was acquired. Selected identifies a bounded reading. A complete chapter, interview transcript or article is not a complete book, audio review or archive. The reading ledger records the broader contextual scholarship and exact version control. Translations, republications and works repeating the same interviews are not independent witnesses.

The book being reassessed

  • Solomon, Richard H. Chinese Negotiating Behavior: Pursuing Interests Through “Old Friends.” Washington, DC: United States Institute of Peace Press, 1999; supplied 2005 printing. Complete substantive text, notes and bibliography read across the foundation and process readings; selected page images checked. The principal study concerns 1967–1984. The bibliography was read, but its entire underlying literature was not.
  • Freeman, Chas. W., Jr. “Chinese Negotiating Behavior Revisited.” In Solomon, 181–196. Complete essay read. Its late-1990s practitioner reassessment is a separate baseline, rather than a description of conditions in 2005 or independent corroboration of Solomon.

The proposition assessment preserves the distinction between the inventory’s paraphrases and the book’s stronger original formulations. The factual-correction note records three verified errors in the supplied printing without treating them as automatic refutations of its behavioral explanations.

Scholarship central to the cases

  • Shaffer, Gregory, and Henry Gao. “China’s Rise: How It Took On the U.S. at the WTO.” University of Illinois Law Review 2018(1):115–184. Complete published article. Interviews, legal professionalization and institutional learning; the critical reading identifies the interview population and limits.

  • Gao, Henry. “China’s Changing Perspective on the WTO: From Aspiration, Assimilation to Alienation.” In China and the WTO: A Twenty-Year Assessment, edited by Henry Gao, Damian Raess and Ka Zeng, 45–70. Cambridge University Press, 2023. Complete chapter version, related to the 2022 World Trade Review article, not a second independent study. Empirical coverage principally ends in 2021–early 2022.

  • Prusa, Thomas J., and Edwin Vermulst. “United States – Definitive Anti-Dumping and Countervailing Duties on Certain Products from China: Passing the Buck on Pass-Through.” World Trade Review 12(2), 2013:197–234. Selected opening, §§4.2–4.3 and conclusion, including specified tables. Legal and economic analysis of DS379; reading scope, DS379-10.

  • Conrad, Björn. “China in Copenhagen: Reconciling the ‘Beijing Climate Revolution’ and the ‘Copenhagen Climate Obstinacy’.” China Quarterly 210, 2012:435–455. Complete article and 55 notes. Interview-based institutional explanation, tested against the actual accord and contemporary statements in the climate case.

  • Zhang, Hao. “China and Climate Multilateralism: A Review of Theoretical Approaches.” Politics and Governance 10(2), 2022:50–60. Complete review, including references. A map of competing explanations, not a separate investigation of private climate negotiations; CLI18 in the climate register.

  • Gries, Peter Hays, and Kaiping Peng. “Culture Clash? Apologies East and West.” Journal of Contemporary China 11(30), 2002:173–178. Complete author-uploaded text and fifteen notes; original PDF not acquired. Its cultural interpretation of EP-3 commentary does not supply private drafts or a decision-maker experiment; EP314 in the EP-3 register.

  • Jost, Tyler. “The Institutional Origins of Miscalculation in China’s International Crises.” International Security 48(1), 2023:47–90. Selected theory/methods and complete relevant EP-3 and alternative-explanation sections; selected appendix methods, coding and source list. Neither the whole article nor appendix was read. The case evaluates its institutional hypothesis and the letter-identification and chronology problems in its source chain.

  • Wu, Cathy Xuauxuan (name as published). “A Bargaining Theory of US–China Economic Rivalry: Differentiating the Trade and Technology Wars.” Chinese Journal of International Politics 17(4), 2024:323–345. Complete publisher HTML and 106 notes. Learning and commitment mechanisms, with published reporting distinguished from private authorization; critical reading.

  • Zhang, Angela Huyue. “The U.S.–China Trade Negotiation: A Contract Theory Perspective.” Georgetown Journal of International Law 51, 2020:809–866. Complete article and 376 notes. Observability, administrative discretion and enforcement costs; its prospective 2019 remedy discussion is distinguished from the final agreement in the critical reading.

  • Tian, George Yijun, and Carrie Shu Shang. “A Co-regulatory Approach to Bilateral Trade: Assessing and Enhancing Enforceability of U.S.–China Phase One Agreement in the Trump 2.0 Era.” Journal of International Economic Law 29(2), 2026:264–291. Complete publisher HTML, notes and figures. Legal analysis and institutional proposals, evaluated through selected original-record checks in the reading memorandum.

  • Fravel, M. Taylor. “Regime Insecurity and International Cooperation: Explaining China’s Compromises in Territorial Disputes.” International Security 30(2), 2005:46–83. Complete article and 125 notes. The critical reading separates the explanation of compromise from what paired 1989 public and memoir-derived records establish.

  • Iwashita, Akihiro. A 4,000 Kilometer Journey Along the Sino-Russian Border. Slavic Eurasian Studies 3. Sapporo: Slavic Research Center, Hokkaido University, 2004. Complete preface, introduction, Chapters 1, 3 and 6, conclusion and reference inventory; Chapters 2, 4 and 5 and index unread. Regional implementation and center–local relations; scope.

  • Loh, Dylan M. H. “Diplomatic Control, Foreign Policy, and Change under Xi Jinping: A Field-Theoretic Account.” Journal of Current Chinese Affairs 47(3), issue 2018, accepted April 2019:111–145. Complete article, references, table and figure. Original interviews and observation illuminate supervised contact and initiative; the blanket ASEAN textual claim is corrected against originals in the critical reading.

  • Taylor, Monique, and Jeremy Garlick. “‘Flexible’ versus ‘fragmented’ authoritarianism: evidence from Chinese foreign policy during the Xi Jinping era.” Australian Journal of International Affairs 79(2), 2025:189–208; online December 2024. Complete published article and references, acquired from a CC BY repository deposit. Competing delegation explanation, with source and causal limits examined in the critical reading.

Additional completed contextual readings—on institutions, audiences, translation, historical negotiation and measurement of assertiveness—are recorded in the linked reading ledger. They inform the research design; listing them there does not turn their distinct research populations into additional witnesses to the manuscript’s particular episodes.

  • Bohman, Viking, Audrye Wong, and Victor A. Ferguson. “The Old Logic Behind China’s New Economic Weapons.” The Washington Quarterly 48(3), Fall 2025:25–45. Complete published open-access article, three figures, table and 48 notes. Its 233 observations are restrictions, not negotiation outcomes; the comparative and causal limits, overlapping source chains and selected bibliographic errors are examined in the critical reading. This supplies mechanisms for the 2025 case rather than a second independent account of its negotiations.
  • Farrell, Henry, and Abraham L. Newman. “Weaponized Interdependence: How Global Economic Networks Shape State Coercion.” International Security 44(1), 2019:42–79. Selected complete pages 52–58 and 75–79, including notes; not the full empirical case narratives. Used to distinguish network position and regulatory capacity from economic size or bilateral dependence. Reading scope and limits.

The institutional citation-chain follow-up also reads Shu G. Zhang and Ni Chen, “Beijing’s Institutionalised Economic Statecraft Towards Brazil: A Case Study,” Journal of Current Chinese Affairs 50(3), issue 2021, online January 24, 2022:339–365. All text, table and references were read; two figures remain uninspected. The critical reading distinguishes the original anonymous informant’s uncertain ministry affiliation, author interpretation and a later article’s more specific oil-deal attribution. It is not added to the complete-reading count or treated as a reconstructed company mandate.

Participant accounts, policy research and reporting

  • Froman, Michael. Miller Center oral history, Interview 1: selected 4,826-word Copenhagen discussion. Webpage interview-year metadata is inconsistent with the retrospective content; exact date is not silently corrected from an unavailable PDF filename. Saran, Shyam. “One Long Day in Copenhagen,” in Navroz K. Dubash, ed., India in a Warming World (2019), 157–168, DOI 10.1093/oso/9780199498734.003.0009: complete public chapter rendering with its notice of reprint from the 2017 book. Different delegations’ retrospective accounts are compared without amalgamating their disputed details. Reading scope and register.

  • Ramesh, Jairam. Statement and reply on Copenhagen, Rajya Sabha Official Debates, December 22, 2009, printed 218–223 and 242–248. Both interventions read completely, intervening debate selectively; public parliamentary defense distinguished from private instructions. Original PDF and selected visual checks are registered in the same participant supplement.

  • 邓红国 [Deng Hongguo]. “我亲历的加入WTO金融部门谈判.” In 中国“入世”谈判风云录, edited by 张向晨 and 索必成, 284–315. Beijing: 人民出版社, 2023. Complete chapter, not the collection. Its authorization account and internal chronology problems are examined in the participant note.

  • Barshefsky, Charlene. Oral history, March 2, 2005; Summers, Lawrence. Oral history, October 17, 2002. Miller Center, University of Virginia. Selected WTO/China sequences and surrounding passages, not complete interviews. The US source register distinguishes their disagreement over 1999 from contemporaneous evidence.

  • Stern, Todd. Interview with James Green, Georgetown US–China Dialogue Podcast, August 26, 2019; Prueher, Joseph W. Interview with James Green, same series, May 6, 2019. Complete written transcripts, audio not reviewed. Climate and EP-3 participant recollections are checked against dated accepted texts. Prueher’s acknowledged use of Solomon complicates claims of independent theoretical corroboration.

  • Zhou Wenzhong. Interview dated July 5, 2018, republished by Wenhui on December 12 as “周文重：美国对华政策一直存在两面性，我们也要有两手准备,” credited to the Shanghai American Studies 40 Years, 40 People project. Complete published text read; original project volume and audio not acquired. Reported EP-3 instruction and conflicting aircraft-return recollections are examined in the developmental reading.

  • Xie Zhenhua. “解振华：让气候目标倒逼改革,” interview with Hu Shuli, Gong Jing and Kong Lingyu, Caixin, November 15, 2014, posted November 21. Complete Chinese interview and the later complete publication of English excerpts read; the English excerpts omit the exchange on reciprocal target acceptance. Reading record.

  • Chen Weidong. “对华贸易救济的争端解决与后续执行——以中国起诉案件为重点,” institutional publication, April 11, 2013. Selected sections III and V with corresponding notes; not a completed article reading or evidence of the government’s private strategy. Scope and source checks.

  • Lapres, Daniel Arthur. “The EU-China WTO Deal Compared.” China Business Review, July–August 2000:8, 10–12, 14. Complete article and table, not the issue. Practitioner comparison checked against schedules. Mattoo, Aaditya. China’s Accession to the WTO: The Services Dimension. World Bank Policy Research Working Paper 2932, December 2002. Complete narrative and specified tables; dense appendix and final figure incompletely audited. Both are documented in the EU register.

  • Chadha, Saheb Singh. Negotiating the India-China Standoff: 2020–2024. Carnegie India, December 2024. Complete main report and 279 notes; annex inventories and selected originals, not the whole statement compilation. His July 2026 Threading the Needle: India’s Path Forward with China was read only in selected border and negotiation-hierarchy sections. India register.

  • Chivvis, Christopher S., and Senkai Hsia. Implementing the Biden Administration’s China Strategy. Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, March 2026. Selected methodology, 2023 negotiation passages and associated notes. Its interviews with thirty-three former US officials are the authors’ retrospective source base, not interviews conducted by this project or access to Chinese deliberations. Crisis register, C23-21; intermediary register, K23-12.

  • Lardy, Nicholas R., and Tianlei Huang. China’s Financial Opening Accelerates. PIIE Policy Brief 20-17, December 2020. Complete brief, notes and five figures. Chorzempa, Martin. “Did the US-China phase one deal deliver a win for US financial services?” PIIE, January 27, 2020. Complete substantive text and reform table; embedded figure not visually checked. Finance register, FIN-30–31.

  • Bown, Chad P. “US-China phase one tracker: China’s purchases of US goods” and “China bought none of the extra $200 billion of US exports in Trump’s trade deal.” PIIE, 2022; the latter first published February 8 and revised July 19. Complete specified text, methods, notes and appendix tables read; charts and underlying datasets not replicated. Implementation register, POI08–09.

  • Yermakov, Vitaly, and Michal Meidan. Russia and China Expand Their Gas Deal: Key Implications. Oxford Institute for Energy Studies, March 2022. Complete thirteen-page paper, notes and figures. Paik, Keun-Wook. Sino-Russian Gas and Oil Cooperation: Entering into a New Era of Strategic Partnership? OIES WPM 59, April 2015. Selected sections and notes. Forecasts and estimated prices remain distinct from contract terms; gas register, RUS-G09/G13.

Contemporary reporting supplies attributed claims and traces decisions that public readouts omit. Particularly consequential items include David Lawder, Jeff Mason and Michael Martina’s Reuters report of May 8, 2019 on draft revisions (TUS08); Trevor Hunnicutt’s Reuters report of July 20, 2023 on Kirby’s anticipated Kissinger debrief (K23-06); Jennifer Hansler and Kylie Atwood’s July 2023 CNN reporting (K23-05); and Amrita Nayak Dutta and Deeptiman Tiwary’s Indian Express report of October 22, 2024 (IND-IE-20241022). The exact editions and complete versus selected reading scopes are in the registers. Syndicated copies remain one reporting chain; anonymous-source claims do not become authenticated drafts or patrol protocols through repetition.

The Chapter 7 development also reads Guo Fang, Li Fengtao and Wang Hongru’s two June 2014 China Economic Weekly reports, a contemporaneous interview with Xu Qinhua, selected PetroChina annual-report pages, the complete six-part June 2013 NDRC price explanation and four dated public corporate exchanges. Only the first news report supports the anonymous final-round account; the related article is not independent corroboration. Tadeusz Dmochowski’s 2018 boundary study and Li Chuanxun’s regional-cooperation study were read in selected sections only. Their acquisition leads recovered official treaty texts, while the latter’s undated-use claim and signature-date error remain insufficient to establish individual permit outcomes. Exact scope and bibliographic details are in the Chapter 7 development registers below.

Primary collections and documentary practice

The Foreign Relations of the United States series supplies selected contemporary American memoranda, instructions and editorial source notes: 1969–1976, volumes XVII–XVIII, and 1977–1980, volume XIII. The claims/assets and normalization reconstructions inspect bounded sequences, not the complete volumes. Clinton and George W. Bush presidential archives, USIA/State mirrors, and later archived White House records supply additional official releases. The acquired EP-3 legal-guidance packet is a Department of Defense disclosure. A Bush Library finding aid identifying a separate NSC release does not establish that its underlying assets were downloaded or read.

China’s Ministry of Commerce collection, 《中国加入世界贸易组织谈判文件资料选编》, compiled by its WTO Department under editor 索必成 (中国商务出版社, 2013), provides selected readings from 综合卷① and 双边卷③. Reproduced dated offers and signed bodies are distinguished from retrospective editorial narration. Most of both volumes remains unread; missing annexes are not supplied by a later summary. The participant collection above is another genre, not an additional archive.

WTO accession schedules and DS379 submissions, reports and procedural records; UNFCCC Copenhagen, Cancún, Durban, Lima and Paris instruments; paired US–China climate announcements; and IISD Earth Negotiations Bulletin conference reports supply institutional rules, positions and observed proceedings. They do not confer equivalent legal status on every national target or provide private authorizations. Final-text adoption, consent to be bound and implementation remain separate events.

For recent cases, paired Chinese and counterpart ministry texts are read alongside original tariff notices, the bilingual Phase One agreement, Chinese regulatory decisions, company disclosures, hearings and released correspondence. Indian and Russian official texts, treaty reproductions and contemporary reporting extend the comparison. Original treaty maps, commercial price formulas, complete licensing files and undisclosed negotiating drafts remain absent where the registers say so. Corporate or official announcements establish their stated milestone; they do not automatically verify every operational consequence.

The 2009–2013 climate machinery supplement distinguishes initialing/publication, formal signatures, authorized consultation, reported meetings and later specified tasks. The Brazil energy supplement follows the institutional scholarship into a bilateral action plan, a coordination meeting, auction results and a conditional Petrobras agreement. These original documents define formal responsibilities and terms; they do not recover Chinese company instructions.

Chapter-to-register map

Chapter Main documentary registers and reading guides
Introduction and 1: baseline, normalization, failed claims settlement Foundation; process; normalization; claims/assets; corrections.
2: US and EU accession Chinese collection; US record; EU comparison; manuscript follow-up; developmental original rereadings.
3: legal expertise and climate DS379; climate; trade-learning reading; capacity bridge and selected source return; 2012 redetermination and 2016 domestic proceeding; underlying-submission search and unacquired targets; Xie interview and Lima reporting; current WTO appeal routes.
4: EP-3, the 2023 crisis and intermediary access EP-3; 2023 crisis; Kissinger; paired EP-3 participants.
5: trade negotiation and the partial settlement Chinese record; US record; agreement; tariff dates; Wu; Zhang; February 2019 transcript and congressional Q&A.
6: implementation Finance; agricultural approvals; 2017 follow-up; 2017 Chinese review procedure; Phase One implementation; chapter supplement; 2025 export-control record; full scholarly reading.
7: India and Russia India; Russian boundary; Russian gas; paired March 2022 India positions; 1999 permit annex and 2004 renewal; gas reporting, company accounts and domestic prices.

Source captures are preserved outside the Git manuscript; registers identify their locations and hashes. No new interviews or outreach underpin this edition of the guide. The chapter footnotes and registers, rather than the presence of a title in this bibliography, control what evidence was actually examined.

The September 15 institutional supplement adds the 2018–2024 authority records, the 2023 sanctions and ministerial-vacancy sequence, and selected original checks supporting the two new scholarly readings. Formal responsibilities, reported professional constraints and the unrecovered instructions behind particular concessions remain distinct forms of evidence.

The 1995–96 intellectual-property comparison uses the complete English letters/action plan, selected Chinese reproduction and congressional testimony, and the operative 1995–96 USTR notices. Those records establish an earlier enforcement design and later American assessments; they do not establish equivalent performance across the 1995 and 2020 agreements.

Both English pages visually reread for this introduction. Official facsimiles; document audit. The letter establishes the departure announced to Shi, not whether the delegation would actually have left in the absence of a meeting. Its descriptions of Shi’s position and the leaders’ intentions are American representations.↩︎

Clinton and Zhu, joint press conference, April 8, 1999, archived White House transcript, opening WTO remarks and Zhu’s response beginning “I’d like here to respond.” This is the American transcript of interpreted Chinese remarks, evidence of Zhu’s public explanation, not a complete account of Chinese preferences. See Chapter 2.↩︎

  9. Chapter 3, DS379 case and source register.   10. Chapter 5, “The right to complain,” and the 1995–2020 original-document comparison.   11. Chapter 6, “Permissions in exchange for permissions,” notes 36–46, and 2025 export-control research.   54. William C. McCahill Jr. to Shi Guangsheng, November 12, 1999, in 商务部世界贸易组织司编、索必成主编, 中国加入世界贸易组织谈判文件资料选编：双边卷③ (2013), printed pp. 1067–1068/PDF pp. 1074–1075, original facsimile.   55. US closing papers of November 12 and the response attributed to November 13, 双边卷③, printed pp. 1071–1077 and 1087–1093; exact document audit. The English second paper has no visible date; the date comes from the collection’s heading and is supported by its reference to a Chinese November 13 auto-finance offer. Retention clauses mean an omission from its shorter list is not automatically withdrawal. White House summary, November 16, 1999, preserved as WTO-M03, is a contemporaneous American account of the result, not the missing signed bilateral annexes. It describes 50% for value-added services within two years but paging within three; the November 13 proposal had grouped them at two years. Life insurance remains at 50% with partner choice and branching provisions; the summary does not reproduce the proposed management-control clause in full. Banking returns to accession-relative two/five-year customer clocks and promises national treatment within designated areas, whereas the November 13 paper uses the January 1, 2002/2005 deadlines. The safeguard moves from a proposed fifteen to twelve years; the November 13 antidumping proposal is twenty, while the summary announces fifteen. These are version-specific differences, not authority to reconstruct missing final clauses. Barshefsky’s later account of losing 51% concerns telecommunications; the American April public summary specified 51% after four years for value-added and paging. It is not evidence of a 51% life-insurance demand in these November closing papers.↩︎
